#2854. Eagles ready to ride Joe Milton's confidence (and arm) as far as it takes them

Eagles ready to ride Joe Milton's confidence (and arm) as far as it takes them originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

ARLINGTON, Texas — Zach Ertz has played a lot of years in the NFL with a lot of quarterbacks. But even he was blown away by Joe Milton in the Eagles' improbable 39-38 comeback win on Thanksgiving.

He couldn't get one throw off his mind: The 50-yard bomb to DeVonta Smith in the second quarter.

"I don't know how far that first ball went to Smitty but it had to be one of the farthest throws I've ever seen," Ertz marveled.

Ertz was told it went 64 yards in the air — 64.4 to be exact.

"That's insane," Ertz said. "Rolling away. Smitty is going right, he's going left. Just a really impressive throw."

While the Eagles have seen Milton's strong right arm in practice, it doesn't get old seeing it in games. Milton uncorked a few of those long balls on Thursday afternoon, but he did more than just that. Milton ended up completed 21 of 31 passes for 331 yards with 3 touchdowns and an interception. He had a ridiculous passer rating of 121.8.

"Stuff like that ain't never going to get old," Makai Lemon said. "When he's making great passes, it's like, 'Damn, he did that.' It's still great to see."

But what stood out as much as anything on Thursday afternoon was the poise and confidence Milton showed throughout the comeback. Even when the Eagles were down 21 points late in the third quarter, Milton didn't blink. His confidence didn't waver.

"I would say the confidence for the entire offense starts with that guy," Ertz said. "If he's able to exude confident and instill confidence not only in his play but those around him and the game plan, I think it carries an offense a long way.

"Obviously, it's a great starting point. You have to go out there and execute but when you walk into a huddle, when you walk out to the field with a guy who thinks he can make every throw, which he can honestly, I think it instills a lot of confidence in the guys."

The Eagles fell behind the Cowboys on the very first possession of the game and didn't take a lead until there were 2 seconds left in the game. At one point late in the third quarter, they found themselves down 21 points.

So this ended up being the biggest comeback ever in a Thanksgiving game and one of the biggest comebacks in franchise history.

It was orchestrated by a guy making his third-career NFL start.

"Amazing. Guy's a rockstar, man," Jordan Mailata. "He just keeps playing the next play. True example of being cool, calm and collected. And just keep playing the next play no matter what the outcome was. Joe, hats off to him, man. MVP performance tonight."

The crazy thing about Milton's success is that he wasn't signed off the Dallas practice squad until Oct. 16. He didn't go through OTAs or training camp in this offense, with these players. He's learning all this on the fly.

So there have certainly been some bumps in the road, but it's hard to fathom how this guy was sitting on the Cowboys' practice squad a month and a half ago. Any team could have signed him.

Next Sunday in Arizona will be Milton's fourth start with the Eagles.

"I feel like we're just learning each other more," Lemon said. "He's learning the offense, we're learning him more. As the weeks continue, we're going to be able to be on the same page."

The confidence that Milton has brought to the Eagles doesn't just stay with the offense either. The entire team feeds off of it.

"Joe came in and just kept working his tail off," edge rusher Jonathan Greenard said. "Humble but still had his confidence. He wasn't cocky, he wasn't like 'Aw, this is my show.' It's just more like I'm here to get an opportunity and I can be myself. And I'm seeing him be himself. And that's the part where I love it for him because now he can truly just play.

"We're rallying behind him every single time. I tell him that, he knows that. I just got done talking to him, telling him that, 'It's not your last one.' Get used to this feeling. I think he handled it really well. I'm excited to see what he does next week."

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2852. Mavericks vs Suns Recap: 3 things as Dallas rallies for 113-109 win against Phoenix

The Dallas Mavericks stayed undefeated in NBA Cup group play in dramatic fashion Friday night, beating the Phoenix Suns 113-109 in Phoenix.

Dallas rallied from a 21-point deficit, trailing 92-71 with less than two minutes left in the third quarter. Cooper Flagg led all scorers with a monster 34-point game that might have been his best game of his career.

What's funny is the Mavericks started this game extremely hot. Riding the defensive aggression that has carried them throughout the young season, Dallas jumped on Phoenix early and got out in transition. The Mavericks had an 18-5 in the blink of an eye, and just as quickly as they built that lead, it disappeared.

Kyrie Irving sat out for load management, and his presence was felt almost as soon as Dallas had to dip into its bench. The Mavericks started "Point Coop" tonight, with Flagg at point guard. But instead of Naji Marshall jumping into the starting lineup, coach Dusty May kept the starting lineup he's used over the last few games, but plugged PJ Washington back in. Once Marshall and the reserves checked in, things went downhill as the Suns bench exploded.

The Suns controlled the game from the midway point of the first quarter all the way to the fourth. Throughout the game Flagg was doing everything in his power to keep the team connected, but didn't have anyone running with him so to speak. That changed in the fourth, where Naji Marshall and Max Christie came alive. Marshall scored eight late points in the frame, including back-to-back threes, and Christie nailed a crucial three and secured the game-deciding rebound in the final seconds, along with throwing Flagg an alley-oop earlier in the quarter.

This was a special game from Flagg. With Dallas down 106-103 with 1:37 left, Flagg made three straight buckets, punctuated with a smooth 13-foot turnaround, to give the Mavericks a 109-106 lead with just under 24 seconds left. From there the Mavericks defense held on and the Mavericks are a perfect 4-0 in NBA Cup group play.

I think I've either typed or said out loud at least 10 times this season "Wow, that was the best game of Cooper Flagg's career" and, well, I'm typing that again: That was the best game of Cooper Flagg's career.

Flagg finished with 34 points, seven rebounds, eight assists, five steals, four blocks, and only two turnovers. It's the first time since steals and blocks were record that a player has notched this stat line. He's the third player ever to hit 30 points, seven rebounds, eight assists, five steals, and four blocks. And he did this without Kyrie Irving, who the Mavericks were 0-3 without Irving entering tonight. Oh and he made three straight clutch field goals in the final 90 seconds with the Mavericks down three. Remarkable.

I'm not sure what else to say about Flagg. This is a special, special dude. He was everywhere, with splash defensive plays, transition havoc, and clutch halfcourt shotmaking. He's the real deal and the full package. Can't be said enough how important it was for Flagg to do this without Irving, as the Mavericks desperately have to find ways to win games without their second star so Irving's workload can be managed. Turns out having Flagg turn into basketball god is a way to win those games.

For the first three quarters, this was looking like a waste of a historic Flagg game, mainly because the Mavericks bench couldn't get anything going. Without Irving, the Mavericks just don't have many reliable perimeter players that coach Dusty May trusts.

Naji Marshall and Max Christie came through in the nick of time. Christie scored 16 second-half points, including two three-pointers, while Marshall scored 10 in the fourth, including eight straight down the stretch while the Mavericks were rallying back into the game. Dallas already lacks a lot of pop on the perimeter even with Irving in the game, so without him, it's troubling. Kudos to Christie and Marshall for heating up in the second half, and kudos to May for sticking with Marshall after a rough first half. Dallas just needed any type of spacing at all, and Christie delivered.

The Mavericks offense doesn't look pretty when it's limited to slower, half-court play, but boy when the defense is humming and that aggression pays off, it's fun to watch. Dallas jumped to that 18-5 early lead thanks to that aggressive defense, and got itself back into the game with that same style.

Dallas had 12 steals, outscored Phoenix 15-9 in transition, and scored 21 points off the Suns 18 total team turnovers. The Mavericks just continue to amp the pressure against teams and it's paying off. There were moments in the fourth quarter where it looked like the Suns were completely unprepared for how up on the ball The Mavericks defenders would be — Dallas even forced an over-and-back call against Devin Booker when Zaccharie Risacher hounded him as soon as he crossed halfcourt.

The downside to this style is obvious — teams that can handle the pressure and pass over the top can burn the Mavericks for backdoor cuts, layups, and threes. The Suns shot 15-of-40 from three tonight, which nearly did the Mavericks in. But it wasn't enough and Dallas survived.

#2849. Ty Robinson plays role of unlikely hero in Eagles' Thanksgiving win

Ty Robinson plays role of unlikely hero in Eagles' Thanksgiving win originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

ARLINGTON, Texas — Ty Robinson has been waiting a year and a half to make one impact play to help the Eagles win a game.

That's exactly what happened on Thursday in North Texas as Robinson ended up playing the role of unlikely hero in the Eagles' incredible 39-38 comeback win over the Dallas Cowboys.

"I feel like this is just the start," the second-year DT said. "I've just been waiting for that opportunity. Like I've been saying, here it is. It's time to take advantage of it and make the most of it. I'm going to keep rolling. This is just the start."

Robinson, 25, had two of the game's biggest plays. First, he blocked a punt early in the fourth quarter that was scooped up by Kenny Moore II for a touchdown. Then, he sacked Dak Prescott on 4th-and-1 with under a minute left in the game, which led to the game-winning field goal drive.

There was a point in training camp where it was fair to wonder if Robinson would ever be a contributor in the NFL. Then the former fourth-round pick goes and plays hero in a nationally televised game that might have kept the Eagles' season alive.

"It was awesome. I love that," head coach Nick Sirianni said. "I love when guys like Ty make these huge plays because we see it every day in practice: his fight, how he works, all those things. And then for him to be able to make a play like that in this game … or a couple plays like that in this game are huge."

For a lot of this game, points were hard to come by for the Eagles' offense, so getting a touchdown from special teams was a huge deal. Robinson has become a very good special teams player this year.

This was a rush that Michael Clay brought to his punt block team on a short week and it worked in a crucial moment.

"I just stuck my hand out and tried to get out of the way so I didn't hit the punter," Robinson said. "I didn't even see it hit my hand. I just felt it. As soon as I got up and saw Kenny pick it up right there in front of the end zone, I was pretty pumped."

Robinson might have been pumped then, but he didn't know his biggest play of the game was going to happen later in the fourth quarter.

The Cowboys were trying to ice the game when they elected to go for a 4th-and-1 from the Eagles' 39-yard line with 55 seconds remaining. If the Cowboys picked up the first down, the game would have been over; they would have won 38-36.

While Robinson made sure he didn't jump offsides, he still made it into the backfield after showing patience. And he picked up the first sack of his NFL career.

"There's no better day," Robinson said. "Thanksgiving game against the Cowboys, against a division rival, against a great quarterback like Dak. I got nothing but respect for them.

"To go out there and do that, the adversity we overcame with the guys on the defensive side of the ball, there's no better feeling. I haven't had kids yet, so I don't know what that's like. But as of right now, that's probably No. 1 for me."

Through 10 games this season, Robinson had played just 73 total defensive snaps. But his workload has increased after the Eagles traded Jordan Davis to the Jacksonville Jaguars before the deadline. That trade meant more opportunity for Robinson and he's trying to make the most of it.

Veteran edge rusher Jonathan Greenard said it's easy to root for a guy like Robinson, who has been a positive force in the locker room — "Everybody loves Ty," Greenard said.

The Eagles used a fourth-round pick to select Robinson out of Nebraska last spring and they're hoping he can turn into part of their defensive tackle rotation. He took a big step on Thursday toward being an impact player.

"When you see guys like that finally get their chance and go in and be in that big moment and make that play, because I've been there," Greenard said, "it's a great feeling. I wish everybody could experience that. But for him to make those plays he did today, man, I know he's going to be sleeping really good tonight."

Robinson on Thursday night said he hadn't yet checked his phone to see how many text messages and calls were awaiting him, but he figured there would be a lot.

He planned on responding to a few of those on the flight back to Philly from Dallas, before giving himself a chance to rest.

"I'm sure the adrenaline will come down and the joy will come down," Robinson said. "And I'm probably going to sleep the rest of the flight. But we'll get back home and enjoy the next three days to recover and get back to it next week."

#2846. Yaxel Lendeborg, Warriors fail to overcome abysmal start in loss to Blazers

The Golden State Warriors two-game winning streak came to an unequivocal end on Friday night in a 116-94 loss to the Portland Trail Blazers. After facing a shorthanded and schedule tired Celtics and the Kings in the team's last two contest, the depleted Dubs were steamrolled by a Blazers team that has been playing like one of the best team's in the league early. Despite eventually bouncing back, it was too little too late.

When rookie Yaxel Lendeborg fails to be the dominant offensive force he's looked like at times early in the season, the Warriors offense is essentially entirely reliant on three-point shot. When Golden State is struggling from deep, the team's limited on-ball creation, athleticism, and size makes them look out of place in the NBA. Since no shot sets up an opposing offense better than a missed three, the failing offense makes their job even harder defensively. That's how things started in Portland.

Lendeborg was unusually hesitant to attack offensively while outside shots weren't falling. The Blazers pounced, building a lead beyond 20 points in the first quarter. Before the Warriors outside shooting came alive in the second quarter, the Blazers had amassed an absurd 47-11 lead.

The Warriors offense is at least passable when they can find a rhythm from deep. De'Anthony Melton's scorching hot shooting streak continued, bringing the Dubs to life with three consecutive made threes. While it was nothing to celebrate, the Warriors stopped the bleeding and were able to cut the deficit down to 23 by halftime.

In the third quarter, Lendeborg came to life. After an incredibly tentative first half, he began initiating more offense and forcing the ball downhill. He drew a few fouls and occasionally finished through contact. He scored 12 points in the quarter, including a forceful and-one dunk through firm contact from Jrue Holiday. Not long after, Lendeborg made his first three of the night to cut the deficit to 13.

Once they weren't forced to consistently play in transition, the Warriors defense finally began giving the Blazers problems. Portland was missing their two primary point guards (Damian Lillard and Ja Morant), which forced the bulk of offensive creation into the hands of Deni Avdjia and Scoot Henderson. While both players had plenty of success, they are inconsistent shooters. The Warriors comeback efforts largely relied on those struggles.

Avdjia finished with a game-high 26 points, 7 rebounds, 7 assists, and 2 steals, but was 0-for-6 from three and committed 5 turnovers. Henderson scored 18 points, but was 6-for-15 from the field. In fact, the Blazers were just 12-for-49 (24.5%) from three as a team.

Early in the fourth quarter, Brandin Podziemski missed a corner three that would have cut the deficit to seven. The shot went in-and-out and the Blazers answered with a 6-0 run. The Warriors would pull within 10 a few times in the final quarter, but were never able to cut the deficit to single digits. You pay a price when you fall behind 36 points in the first half.

Lendeborg led the Warriors with 18 points, 7 rebounds, 2 assists, 2 blocks, 1 steal, and 1 turnover on 5-for-14 shooting (1-for-4 from three). Melton had 17 points and was 5-for-10 from three, but committed 5 turnvoers and struggled inside the arc. Podziemski and Charles Bassey were the only other Warriors in double-figures, scoring 16 and 12 points respectively.

With the loss, the Warriors were officially eliminated from the NBA Cup, which will at least give them a lighter schedule in the coming weeks. Although the team would surely prefer the pay checks that would have come with advancing. For now, Golden State will return home and continue the usual regular-season schedule on Sunday. They will host the Los Angeles Clippers.

#2842. Roob's Observations as Joe Milton leads Eagles' miraculous comeback over Cowboys

Roob's Observations as Joe Milton leads Eagles' miraculous comeback over Cowboys originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

I sure hope you didn't turn off the TV and start eating Thanksgiving dinner when it was 14-0 or 21-3 or 31-10.

Behind a spectacular performance by Joe Milton in his third career start and a clutch monster game-winning field goal by Jake Elliott, the Eagles pulled off their biggest comeback ever against the Cowboys – and 4th-biggest against anybody – Thursday night, winning 39-38 after trailing by 31-10 with half a minute left in the third quarter.

Jake Elliott blasted a 59-yard field goal with two seconds left to cap one of the most remarkable comebacks in Eagles history and send the AT&T Stadium fans home devastated.

The Eagles scored more points in the fourth quarter (22) than they had in any of their last eight games.

Here's our 10 Instant Observations off an all-timer.

1. What an astonishing performance by Joe Milton in his third NFL start. Just unreal stuff by Milton against the team he was with until mid-October. Milton's TDs of 50 and 45 yards to DeVonta Smith, a guy he had only played one game with before Thursday night, were a real high point of the season. The first one in particular was magical, and we'll get to that in a moment, but Milton was just masterful throughout, finishing 21-for-31 for 331 yards, three touchdown passes, a 15-yard TD run, one interception and a 121.8 passer rating. Are you kiding me? On top of the two bombs to DeVonta, he added a 24-yard TD pass to Makai Lemon with 2 ½ minutes left that made this a two-point game before a failed two-point conversion. Then he drove the Eagles into Elliott's field goal range in the final seconds. This was pure magic. The inconsistency and inaccuracy we saw in his first two starts turned into high-level playmaking Thursday in Dallas. Milton did so much better taking juice off his throws and giving his receivers more catchable balls, which definitely helped. He threw with incredible touch, spread the ball around, made outstanding decisions, drove the ball down the field and never wavered in his confidence, no matter how big the Cowboys' lead got. The Eagles have a quarterback.

2. Before we go any farther, let's just talk about Milton's 50-yarder to DeVonta. Play of the Year. What a spectacular throw and really demonstrates what Milton is capable of and why his ceiling is so high. Milton was flushed out to his left, Smith was in traffic after running a short in-breaking route, but it turned into a scramble drill and Milton waved Smith down the field and then just heaved the football across the field to the end zone right to a waiting Smith, who got great position in front of Cowboys cornerback Cobie Durant and secured the ball. Milton threw from the Eagles' 42-yard-line and the ball traveled halfway across the field so we're talking about something like 65 yards in the air. This was right out of the Randall Cunningham playbook, Milton using his mobility to buy time and then just throwing a flat-out missile for the 3rd-longest catch of Smith's career and the Eagles' longest TD pass against the Cowboys since Jalen Hurts' 81-yarder to DeSean Jackson late in 2020 – ironically in Hurts' third career start also. What's remarkable is that this is only the second game Milton and Smith have played together and they've barely practiced together and yet they were able to connect on such an incredible play.

3. Can we please talk about DeVonta Smith? Good lord, how good is he? I mean, we've always known it, but he showed everybody watching football all over the country Thursday night what a special player he is. Smith had just five TDs of at least 45 yards in his entire career, including the 46-yard dagger in the Super Bowl. He had just one since the middle of 2024. He just had two in one half from a new quarterback that he played his first game with four days ago. Smith finished Thursday with 11 catches for a career-high 200 yards, most yards by an Eagle since D-Jack had 210 in Dallas in 2010 and 6th-most in Eagles history. It's been a tough year for Smith with a bunch of different quarterbacks and his own hamstring battles that sidelined him for five games and limited him for a sixth. But this was a heck of a reminder of just how great this football player is. Tough to see him hurt his back going for another deep ball in the fourth quarter, but that wasn't enough to keep him out of the game. He was back out there and caught a pass on the Eagles' next series. So tough. So clutch. Absolute beast.

4. And how about Jake Elliott? My goodness. This is a guy who was statistically the worst kicker in the NFL over the 2024 and 2025 season and the Eagles stuck with him and he's just been money all year but never more than he was Thursday night with a 59-yard game-winner. Elliott is now 4-for-5 in his career with 59-yard attempts, and his four makes from 59 and out are tied for 5th-most in NFL history. He was huge on Sunday with 53- and 55-yard field goals in the win over the Steelers, becoming the first kicker in Eagles history to make two field goals of 53 yards or longer in the same game. And now this. The 59-yarder meant so much to this football team because the whole team scraped and clawed just to be in position for him to try a long field goal for the win, and he came up huge. Elliott made two field goals of at least 55 yards the last two years combined. He now has three this year. His kick Thursday was the longest game-winning field goal ever against the Cowboys, which only makes it sweeter. It was easy to think the Eagles needed a kicker after last year ended when it turns out they had exactly the one they needed all along.

5. It would be easy to rip on the defense for allowing 38 points and 416 yards. But considering how bad the first half was and how hot the Cowboys have been, they really ought to be commended. Because after allowing 28 points, 245 yards and 17 first downs in the first half, they held Dak Prescott and Company to 10 points, 171 yards and seven first downs in the second half. They didn't get a ton of stops, but they got enough to give the offense – and special teams – a chance to get back into the game. Jeremiah Trotter Jr. once again was all over the place with 12 tackles, a sack, two tackles for loss and a quarterback hit. Ty Robinson – who wouldn't have been playing if Jordan Davis was still here – not only blocked a punt that Kenny Moore returned for a touchdown, he had a sack, two tackles for loss and four tackles. Jonathan Greenard, Jalen Carter and Byron Young keyed a 2nd-half defensive effort that finally slowed down the Cowboys. Even practice squad safety Cole Wisniewski, only playing because Marcus Epps left the game, made a couple big plays. You're not going to call many defensive efforts outstanding when you allow 38 points, but this one was. The stats might not say it, but when they had to make plays, they made plays.

6. We haven't had many opportunities this year to say nice things about special teams, but Michael Clay's unit was fantastic Thursday night. Start with Robinson's blocked punt that Moore picked up and returned three yards for the touchdown that really gave the Eagles some momentum early in the fourth quarter. Then there was Britain Covey, who had a career-long 55-yard kick return to set up Milton's 15-yard touchdown run and averaged 12.3 yards on punt returns, Braden Mann, who averaged a ridiculous 62.7 yards per punt and for the third straight week had at least one punt of 68 or more yards and then add in Elliott's ridiculous game-winning field goal. The Eagles also kept the dangerous Kavontae Turpin in check. Although he had a 33-yard punt return, he only averaged 20.7 yards on his kick returns. Special teams has contributed to plenty of losses this year. Thursday night they had a whole lot to do with a win.

7. Once again Saquon Barkley was unable to finish a game, and it's been tough to watch Saquon this year because he just can't get through a game healthy, and he's such a competitor you know it's killing him to have this kind of season. He ran five times for just seven yards before leaving the game. But you know what happened after that? Will Shipley had himself a low-key very good game. The Eagles have cycled through so many running backs this year, but Shipley is finally back healthy and he gave the Eagles 10 carries for 46 yards, a regular-season career high and probably more meaningful than the 77 yards he had late in the NFC Championship Game two years ago. Shipley runs hard and he sees the holes well and he carries tacklers a couple extra yards, and I'm not sure how high is ceiling is, but as sharp as Milton was in this game you don't want to throw every snap, and Shipley came up big with the most high-leverage carries of his career.

8. One cool byproduct of this win is that the Eagles ended Dak Prescott's 20-game home winning streak against the NFC East, a streak that tied the longest in NFL history by any quarterback, a record set by Tom Brady (who was calling the game for FOX). Prescott has given the Eagles nightmares over the years. He had won 11 of his last 13 meaningful starts against the Eagles with 28 touchdowns and five interceptions. In his last 10 starts against the Eagles he had 21 TDs and two INTs. And he made some plays Thursday night, especially in the first half. He threw for 291 yards with two touchdowns – both in the first half – but it's big for the Eagles to get a win over the quarterback who's given them the most trouble over most of the past decade. Drew Mukuba even picked him off on the final play of the game, only the Eagles' fourth interception and sixth takeaway all year. Prescott has given the Eagles nightmares for years. After this game, tonight he'll be the one having nightmares.

9. A lot of Eagles fans don't want to hear this because it's as simple as, "I hate Nick," and, "Nick sucks." And Nick has his faults and has not coached particularly well most of the year. But I'll tell you what. This is the kind of game that saves coaches' jobs. It really is. We said it last week when the Eagles battled the Steelers and came out with a win, and we'll say it again. When an owner sees a team fighting the way the Eagles fought Thursday night in a stadium where the Cowboys don't lose many games, that speaks volume about any coach. It's so easy to give up when you're down 31-10 late in the third quarter, the crowd is going nuts, Dak is making plays all over the place, CeeDee Lamb is going off, Saquon Barkley, Dallas Goedert, Zack Baun and Cooper DeJean are all out, and it seems like nothing is working. This football team didn't buckle. They never stopped battling. You learn so much about a team when it's facing adversity and down 31-10 in Dallas late in the third quarter is plenty of adversity. This might not be a good football team and it might not be a good season, but what the Eagles achieved Thursday night on national TV says a lot about their character and the culture that Sirianni has built over the last six years.

10. Let's talk about comebacks. This was the fifth time in franchise history the Eagles have won a game after trailing by 21 or more points. The first two were before 1960 – they trailed Washington 24-0 at Griffith Stadium in 1946 before winning 28-24 and they trailed the Chicago Cardinals 24-0 at Metropolitan Stadium in Minneapolis in 1959 before winning again 28-24. So this was just the third comeback of 21 or more points since 1960. In 1991, they trailed the Browns at Municipal Stadium 23-0 before rallying behind Jim McMahon for a 32-30 win. And of course the historic comeback vs. the Giants in 2010, when they trailed 31-10 halfway through the fourth quarter before winning 38-31 behind Michael Vick and DeSean Jackson's walk-off punt return. That's five times in 94 years. The Eagles' biggest previous comeback over the Cowboys came in 1988, when they trailed 20-0 at the Vet and won 24-20 on a couple late Anthony Toney touchdowns. The 21-point blown lead is tied for 3rd-biggest in Cowboys history and their biggest since they blew a 23-point lead to the Packers in 2013.

#2841. Spurs erase 20-point deficit to eliminate Lakers in NBA Cup action, advance to knockout round as wild card

The most wide-open division of the 2026 NBA Cup entering Friday, the final day of group play, was West Group C, which had four different teams capable of clinching a spot in the knockout rounds. Two of those teams were the San Antonio Spurs and Los Angeles Lakers, and they delivered one of the night's wildest games.

The Spurs erased a 20-point deficit on their way to a 99-96 win over the Lakers, riding another huge game from Victor Wembanyama. The win officially put them in the knockout rounds as the West's wild card, thanks to a plus-57 point differential in four games.

Simultaneously, the Portland Trail Blazers won the group by defeating the Golden State Warriors and holding the tiebreaker over San Antonio. The Lakers and Warriors were eliminated with the losses.

Wembanyama finished with 25 points, 16 rebounds, 4 steals, 2 assists and 2 blocks, but his 3-for-8 night at the line also invited disaster toward the end of the game, when the Lakers were trying to inch their way back into it.

Los Angeles got the ball down three with a chance to tie the game twice in the final 10 seconds, but the Spurs fouled both times and managed to hold on for the win.

It was a brutal first half for the Spurs, who entered halftime down 54-38, but they proceeded to outscore the Lakers 30-14 in the third quarter and continued that momentum into the fourth quarter to take control.

It's a return to knockout-round action for the Spurs, who reached the NBA Cup championship game last year before losing to the New York Knicks (a result that was repeated in the NBA Finals). They will now face the Dallas Mavericks, the only team to go undefeated in group play, in the quarterfinals next week.

#2839. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

#2836. Jake Elliott proves again he's one of the great long-distance kickers in history

Jake Elliott proves again he's one of the great long-distance kickers in history originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

You want clutch? Here's clutch: There have been nine last-minute game-winning field goals of 59 yards or more in NFL history.

Jake Elliot is now responsible for two of those nine.

Elliott continued his late-career resurgence Thursday night with a miracle 59-yard game-winner in the Eagles' 39-38 comeback win over the Cowboys at AT&T Stadium in Arlington, Texas.

Along with his 61-yard game winner to give the Eagles a 27-24 win over the Giants at the Linc as a rookie in 2017, Elliott joined Justin Tucker as the second kicker in NFL history with two last-minute 59-yard game winners with their team trailing.

Tucker made a 61-yarder with 38 seconds left in the Ravens' 18-16 win over the Lions at Ford Field in 2013 and 66-yarder back at Ford Field as time expired to give the Ravens a 19-17 win over the Lions in 2021.

The only other kickers to make game-winning field goals in the last minute of a game from 59 yards and out are Brandon Aubrey, Matt Prater, Chris Boswell and Ka'imi Fairbairn.

Of those kicks, only Tucker twice and Elliott Thursday made their game-winning kicks with their team trailing. Boswell made a game-winning 60-yarder in the Steelers' 34-32 win over the Jets last year, but that came with 63 seconds left so doesn't technically qualify as a last-minute game-winner.

However you measure it, Elliott reaffirmed his status Thursday as one of the greatest long-distance kickers in NFL history and one of the greatest clutch kickers ever.

"I mean, yeah, I'm human," Elliott said after the game. "Definitely nervous, but you you just go back to your process. I mean, everything, every (kick) is the same. I treat it the same and just kind of have my process: What balls I'm hitting to the nets, at what times, and when I feel comfortable about about the timing of everything, everything usually goes well."

It has lately. Elliott has now made three field goals of at least 55 yards in three attempts this year and his four career makes from 59 yards and out are tied for 4th-most in NFL history.

This is a guy who was statistically the worst kicker in the NFL over the 2024 and 2025 seasons.

Elliott is now 4-for-5 in his career from 59 yards, tied with Little for most accurate ever from that distance.

Elliott thought he had a game-winning 48-yarder with eight seconds left against the Jaguars in London, only to watch Little turn the tables and make a 57-yarder as time expired.

He finally got to experience a game-winner for the first time since October 2023, when he made a 54-yarder in overtime to beat the Commanders 34-31 at th Linc.

"It's awesome," Elliott said. "We haven't had one for a little bit. We almost had one in London, but it's always good to be in those situations."

It speaks volumes that for all the big throws Joe Milton made Thursday night he said his favorites were the two short passes that got the Eagles into position for a long-distane game winner.

"He's a great kicker," Milton said. "All three of those guys, but just to put him in position to ice the game. That's something he's great at. That's probably one of my favorite moments, just icing the game and letting the kicker go be him."

Elliott got the opportunity to attempt a game-winner after defense got a stop on Ty Robinson's first career sack on a 4th-and-1 with 55 seconds left.

"Once the defense got a stop, huge stop by the D, then it was kind of writing on the wall," punter and holder Braden Mann said. "We were like, 'We're going to have a chance. Might be long, might be short, who knows?' But Jake's on the other end just locking in and Rocco's giving me great snaps the whole time. So just exciting."

Elliott should have only had to make a 54-yard field goal, but a false start on rookie right tackle Markel Bell with 11 seconds left moved the ball back from the Cowboys' 36 to the 41.

But in the end it didn't matter. Elliott's monster kick would have been good from 65.

"One of the coolest moments I've been a part of," Mann said. "You know, everyone's got full confidence (in Jake). It was super sick to have him go out and nail it. And he's just been that guy for a while, so it's cool to see him just do it again."

Elliott, who was just 5-for-15 from 50 yards and out in 2024 and 2025, is 5-for-5 this year.

He's now made as many kicks from 53 yards and out since Sunday – three – as he made the last two years combined.

Elliott now has eight career field goals of 58 yards or more. Only Aubrey and Prater have made more. His 80 percent from 58 yards and out is highest ever among kickers who have attempted more than two.

"This is the first Thanksgiving game we've ever played, so those ones feel good," Elliott said. "Those are ones that I'll remember for the rest of my life."

#2834. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2833. Lakers crumble in second half, fall to Spurs in marquee showdown

After building a 20-point lead in the first half, the Lakers were walked down by the Spurs in the second half as they fell to San Antonio, 99-96.

The loss means they will not advance to the knockout rounds of the NBA Cup.

It was a tale of two halves. The Lakers shot 51.2% in the first half before shooting just 34.5% in the second half. LA was just 12-42 from three with Luka going 1-13.

The Lakers were active early, coming away with steals on the first two possessions. However, the Spurs jumped ahead through an alley-oop finish from Victor Wembanyama and a Stephon Castle fastbreak dunk.

LA responded with a fadeaway mid-range jumper from Luka Dončić and back-to-back threes from Jake LaRavia, putting the visitors up 10-6 and forcing an early timeout.

Out of the break, Dylan Harper got to the rim to stop the run as the two teams began trading buckets. Luka finished a floater and scored off the glass trying to draw a foul while Castle had another fastbreak dunk and found Luke Kornet for an oop.

Quentin Grimes knocked down a three before Harper and Luka traded baskets, keeping the Lakers ahead 21-14 with three minutes remaining. LA closed the quarter strong with four straight from Luka, giving him 14 in the quarter, and a Grimes lefty layup as LA led 27-14 after one.

San Antonio could get nothing going offensively as they were 0-8 from three. The Lakers, meanwhile, were cooking. Reaves found Kessler for a layup a handful of possessions before LaRavia drew an and-one on a foul-line jumper late in the shot clock.

After missing the freebie, LaRavia chased down his rebound and found Collin Sexton for a three, making it a 34-17 lead and forcing another Spurs timeout. Fox got a layup over Kessler to fall after the timeout but, again, the Lakers remained in full control.

Sandro Mamukelashvili was making plays on both ends, blocking a layup off the glass and even knocking down a three. Sandwiched between those plays was an and-one putback from Collin Sexton as the lead kept growing.

Sexton kept rolling, getting a floater to fall before hitting another layup at the rim and hitting the "too small" gesture.

Wemby stepped up to try to pull the Spurs back into the game, but Reaves and Luka answered every basket as both knocked down absurd shots. The Spurs never pieced together a run to close the half and the Lakers went into the half up 54-38.

The Spurs tried to come out with energy to cut into the lead again to start the third, but Luka and Austin again had replies. After Reaves hit his first three of the game, Luka converted on back-to-back baskets to make it a 61-45 lead.

Castle drew an and-one for the Spurs, made the free throw and then stole the ensuing in-bounds pass and knocked down a shot with his foot on the 3-point line, quickly cutting into the lead and forcing head coach JJ Redick to use a timeout.

A missed LaRavia three led to a Wemby oop to make it a single-digit game again. Wemby then banked in an off-balance shot, but the Lakers finally responded through a Kessler rebound putback and a Reaves three, prompting a timeout from San Antonio.

The Spurs regained momentum with a Harper putback of his own miss and a Champagnie three. Luka drew an and-one on a jumper to stem the tide. As well as Luka was playing, he was still struggling mightily from three as he was up to 1-11 shooting from range.

The Lakers were struggling for offense as Ziaire missed back-to-back looks from range and Luka airballed another. Fox drew a foul on a layup for an and-one. While he missed the free throw, Wemby grabbed the rebound for a layup. Another Wemby layup and Keldon Johnson three in the final seconds tied the game at 68 heading into the fourth.

Wemby opened the fourth with a pull-up jumper and an assist to put the hosts ahead. Sexton drew an and-one to temporarily stop the run, but Carter Bryant hit a three on the other end. Castle then connected on consecutive threes to make it an 81-73 lead and force an LA timeout.

The Lakers used a Grimes three and Luka off-balance shot to make it a one-possession game again. Wemby responded with an absurd driving reverse dunk before Vassell knocked down a three, extending it back to an eight-point lead at 86-78.

Reaves knocked down back-to-back threes of his own, pulling LA back within two with 3:16 remaining. Harper responded by converting a pair of freebies and hitting a runner. Luka hit his own runner before Austin finished on a fastbreak, keeping it a 90-88 Spurs lead with just under two minutes left.

Wemby converted on a layup after a series of misses, but Luka hit yet another off-balance shot on the next possession. Wemby was then fouled on a jumper and split the pair of free throws, but Fox chased down the rebound, leading to a dagger three from Castle with 43 seconds remaining.

Luka missed a three on the next possession, but just as it looked like all hope was lost, Matisse Thybulle forced a turnover and Reaves immediately hit a three, making it a one-possession game with 33.9 left.

Fox then turned the ball over on the next possession, giving LA a chance to tie the game. The Spurs, however, fouled LaRavia to deny the Lakers that attempt to tie the game. LaRavia made both with 7.7 seconds left, making it a 96-95 Spurs lead. Fox was fouled on the other end and made both with 6.6 seconds left.

Grimes was fouled at midcourt with 3.9 seconds left. He made the first and intentionally missed the second, but Wemby grabbed the rebound. He split the pair and Austin's heave at the buzzer was well short.

Luka had another superstar performance, pouring in 31 points with six rebounds and eight assists. In his return, Reaves tallied 21 points and grabbed eight rebounds.

Kessler finished with 10 points and 13 rebounds matched up against Wemby. Sexton provided a lift off the bench with 12 points.

LaRavia had 10 points. Grimes had nine points as he moved back to the bench. No other Laker had more than three points.

The Lakers will be back in action on Sunday when they play the Grizzlies in a matinee clash, slated to tip at 1 p.m. PT.

#2831. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guidance; Extension of Comment Period

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations

Agencies seek comment on proposed third-party risk management guidance and issue statement on community bank engagement with core service providers

#2830. Inside the Tape: Joe Milton makes the impossible look routine against Dallas

Inside the Tape: Joe Milton makes the impossible look routine against Dallas originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Box scores give you the what. Film gives you the why.

Welcome back to our weekly film series, where we bypass surface-level stats to isolate the schematic tweaks, matchups, and micro-details that define the Eagles from week to week.

After diving into Drew Kendall, Willie Lampkin's first NFL start against the Jaguars in London, rookie wideout Makai Lemon's expanded opportunity against Dallas, and Jeremiah Trotter Jr.'s continued emergence against Pittsburgh, the focus today shifts to Thursday's Thanksgiving shootout that delivered one of the most remarkable performances from an Eagles signal-caller in some time.

In just his third career start, Joe Milton was simply sensational in Dallas accounting for four touchdowns as he led the Eagles back from a 21-point deficit in historic fashion. From the explosive plays downfield to the individual matchups and schematic adjustments that helped fuel the comeback, there was plenty to unpack from a Thanksgiving classic that we'll all remember for a long, long time.

I mean are you kidding? We've talked about Joe Milton's out-of-this-world arm talent for some time, and this is a type of throw not many humans on this earth could even try to make, let alone execute it in an NFL game.

The play really is Milton's explosive skill set on full display. The ability to avoid pressure after Jordan Mailata initially gets pushed back into the pocket, the instincts to keep his eyes downfield as the play develops, and then the absolute howitzer to rip a throw to the opposite side of the field some 60+ yards away on a frozen rope right into the hands of DeVonta Smith is simply incredible. Also, a tip of the cap to Smith in this ballgame. Away from his 200-yard receiving day, the grit and fight he showed to stay out on the football field in what has been a roller coaster of a season thus far tells you everything you need to know about one of the best wideouts to ever put on an Eagles uniform. Smith could barely walk late in that football game after landing on his tailbone, yet he dug deep and finished the ballgame upright for his young quarterback.

Back to Milton, and a quick story. When I first saw him live at the Senior Bowl back in 2024, the buzz down in Mobile, Alabama (where the event is held) centered around Milton's arm. It was almost talked about in the same vein as one of the Wonders of the World – "you just have to see it live," I remember a scout saying before Milton's first practice. And my goodness was it true, and then some. Far hash comeback throws with ease, 60-yard throws off of his backfoot, I remember him completing a pass in warmups off of a rollout from his own five-yard line to the opposite 10. No crow hop, nothing. Just 85 yards on a rope – it was eye-popping to see in person.

To see the confidence he is playing with now, in just his third start, you can't help but be excited for what's next.

Dallas tried to cook up a few pressures as the game worked into the second half, but Milton's athleticism and ability to deliver with zip and accuracy on the run were the ultimate equalizer on Thanksgiving.

This is a Cover 0 look here from Dallas meaning only three players are left to cover on the backend. Although LB Shemar James (50) drops out as the spy, seven players are left for the Eagles to account for. For OC Sean Mannion, this play from Milton showcased the steps he's taken since being named the starter just a few weeks ago. In his first start against New York, this was a look he struggled against. Whether it was understanding who's blitzing from where, identifying his hot (outlet against pressure), or finding a rush lane to create, this type of play often resulted in a negative for Philadelphia.

Rolling to his right throwing back against his body, Milton couldn't have walked the ball up and placed the ball in a better spot for Makai Lemon. And Milton let it loose at Dallas' 35 yard line. Not the 15, not the five, the 35 (!!!) yard line, folks. It was a flat out frozen rope to the hands of Lemon, exactly where you're supposed to place the football on the backline – helmet or higher where only your guy can go and get it. There's keyhole accuracy, there's pinhole accuracy, and then there's this kind of long range bullseye from Milton into the hands of the Eagles' 2026 first-round pick in Lemon. Sensational play from everyone involved.

This is a similar play via a rollout from Milton here, which should be something we see more of moving into the Arizona and Indianapolis games – relocating the launch point. In layman's terms, allow Milton to be an athlete under center and good things will happen. Pigeonholing him into being solely a pocket passer does nothing for his game, as he's best when he's able to be an athlete in the pocket where he can activate his legs before uncorking passes downfield at a variety of depths. The way the football accelerates off of his hand yet gracefully moves through the air is so fun to watch. It just glides here from the opposite 47 down to the Cowboys' 3-yard line with a flick of the wrist.

Overall, it was an explosive and overwhelmingly dynamic afternoon for Milton showcasing virtually every element of his skill set that makes him such an intriguing young talent under center. The arm talent is obvious, it's world-class, but it's the combination of all things – athleticism, awareness in the pocket, ability to create outside of structure, and willingness to keep his eyes downfield that makes him so difficult to defend.

There are still areas of his game that will continue to develop with experience, but the confidence he's playing with right now is undeniable. More importantly, the Eagles are beginning to find ways to put him in positions to succeed before attacking defenses at every level of the field. Thursday was a masterclass from Milton on how to play quarterback at an elite level in the NFL, and a glimpse of why it's hard not to be excited about what he could bring to the organization moving forward.

#2829. San Antonio vs. Los Angeles, Final Score: Spurs’ epic comeback holds off Lakers, 99-96

The San Antonio Spurs survived a nail-biter against the Los Angeles Lakers in the last game of Emirates Cup Group Play. With four of the teams in West Group C tied before Friday night, the Spurs needed a win to advance to the next round.

They started in the mud as they struggled taking care of the ball and 3-point shots weren't falling, soiling their rhythm as they went down 16 points to end the first half. Luka Dončić was seeing all the schemes but making mince meat out of them, and he had 19 points in that span.

Their other issue was fouling too much on defense, which gave the Lakers extra time and had them in the bonus for the last eight minutes of the half. It took until late in the period for the Spurs to get out of their offensive funk and it carried over after intermission. Stephon Castle's hustle and playmaking helped change the game in the third quarter, and Victor Wembanyama stepped up as well, picking up his scoring at close range.

It took the Spurs forcing five turnovers, outgunning the Lakers in a fourth-quarter shootout and intentionally fouling LA twice to put them on the line for two, preventing them a chance to tie with a 3-pointer, to pull away.

Aside from Emirates Cup play, this was a big game because of Dončić and Wemby's status, which includes them being first and fourth in scoring average. Dončić went on a tear in the first half with Castle spending a ton of time and energy guarding him. Wembanyama was not up to his standards early but later got cooking and it taking longer had to do with all the bodies he was seeing. The Lakers would even front on passes to him so he'd have to work harder on his dribble moves to get closer to the basket.

The first non-Wembanyama minutes were not so bad, getting outscored by two points, but keep in mind that Dončić stayed in during that time. The next one was earlier than usual in the second quarter and they were able to tread water. It possibly happened because coach Mitch Johnson needed to press a button to change the flow of the game.

The defense had its uncharacteristic moments but it had seven steals and held the Lakers to 28.6 percent 3-point shooting in the first half. The inability to hit more than two shots outside of the lane was a larger factor in the half-time deficit.

The team slowing down Dončić to 33 percent shooting in the third quarter also changed the game.

De'Aaron Fox and Dylan Harper returned. The latter had some shaky moments, but made his first four attempts on the move and was the most productive player off the bench. Fox was not asserting himself as an inside threat like he usually does for long stretches and had a terrible turnover with 14.3 seconds left when it was a one-possession game. Still, he contributed on defense.

The Spurs were having one of their worst 3-point shooting nights of the season, yet were able to pick it up in the second half, making 47.1 percent. Notably, Castle hit three treys in the fourth quarter when the game was for the taking.

The Spurs were great in the hustle department, taking away the transition game, which allowed three LA fastbreak points, and San Antonio logged 20 second-chance points. The Spurs are now 7-1 this year when scoring at least 20 off second chances.

With the win, the Spurs advance as a wildcard team (the Blazers won the group)bto the quarterfinals of the Cup they will face the Mavericks in Dallas on Friday, December 4.

#2827. This Anthropic proxy play is getting traders' attention – here's where they see it going

Who says you can't tailgate an IPO?Traders are preparing for this week's blockbuster Anthropic IPO in different ways, including buying options on one publicly-traded fund that invests in private assets.The Destiny Tech100 (DXYZ) is a publicly-traded, closed-end fund that invests in equity, equity-linked instruments, or shares of private funds and venture companies to gain exposure to private companies. Currently, 14% of the fund's assets are tied to Anthropic, according to the company's website.Options volume on the fund jumped to more than 10x the 30-day average Monday, according to Cboe LiveVol data. Of the 42,300 contracts traded by midday, 41,800 were calls, with just 141 puts likely opened by buyers.

Just shy of $5.5 million in options premium on DXYZ traded on Monday, with almost all of it tied to calls, but just marginally more spent buying than selling calls, according to SpotGamma. The highest-volume contract is currently the 30-strike call expiring Friday, a 90-cent contract that needs the fund to rally 19% into the weekend to pay off.Traders may want to be wary of how the fund traded around the SpaceX IPO: DXYZ surged almost 150% in May before unwinding all the gains in the weeks ahead of SpaceX's debut. Since then, the fund has barely moved, trading around $26.There are other bullish signs surrounding Anthropic's IPO in the derivatives markets. Current prices for perpetual futures on the blockchain exchange Hyperliquid are implying an opening price of $98, a big pop from the $70-$78 price range quoted in the company's amended S1 filing. The AI giant is expected to price Wednesday evening and begin trading on the Nasdaq on Thursday under the ticker ANT.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released results from a survey of senior financial officers at banks about their strategies and practices for managing reserve balances. The Senior Financial Officer Survey is used by the Board to obtain information about banks' reserve balance management strategies and practices, their expectations around the size of their balance sheets and deposit balances their activity in money markets, and their views on other matters, such as operations on weekends and holidays.

The survey was conducted in collaboration with the Federal Reserve Bank of New York between September 18, 2026, and September 28, 2026, and includes responses from banks that held around three-fourths of total banking system reserve balances at the time of the survey.

#2822. ‘I know the talent he has': Dak Prescott reflects on his time with Joe Milton

'I know the talent he has': Dak Prescott reflects on his time with Joe Milton originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Joe Milton will look across the football field Thursday at AT&T Stadium and see his friend and his mentor on the other sideline.

Milton makes his third NFL start Thursday when the Eagles face the Cowboys, the team he was with until six weeks ago.

It's a classic case of student faces teacher because it was Dak Prescott who had so much to do with Milton's development to the point where he could become a functional starting quarterback in the NFL.

"I can't thank him no more than I already have," Milton said of Prescott. "I mean, he's a great leader. He's a great big brother. He took me under his wing, and that's something that he told me from day one when I got there. Just trust the process. And for Dak and me and our relationship, I mean, taking me on his wing, but also just way that he carries himself.

"Whatever he does and whatever he says, he does that day in, day out. And that's the stuff that I took from him. And I just like the way that he carries himself. That's something that I respect about him the most. But other than that, shoot.

He made sure that I was in the right position as far as understanding plays, understanding coverages, how to lead a team, how to carry yourself outside of the building."

Milton began his career with the Patriots, who drafted him in the sixth round in 2024. They traded him to the Cowboys in April of 2025 and he was with Prescott through the 2025 season and this past offseason and training camp until the Eagles signed him off the Cowboys' practice squad in mid-October.

Milton is 1-1 in his first two starts, losing to the Giants and beating the Steelers, a win that ended the Eagles' seven-game losing streak and may have ended future Hall of Famer Aaron Rodgers' career. He has yet to throw a touchdown or interception.

"I'm not going to pay attention to the guy on the other side of the field," Prescott said. "But credit Joe and his development and his maturity over the last couple of years and just in his time here in Dallas and excited that he's got this opportunity and what he's done the last couple of weeks, getting a big win last week.

"I know the talent that he has and the guy and the quarterback that he is and can be, so it was no surprise when he wasn't on our active (roster) that teams were going to be trying to get him. And then the moment Philly signed him up, I said Joe will be starting by the end of the year, and it's really just a credit to him and the strides he's made and the player that he is and obviously the arm talent."

The Eagles, 3-7, face the 5-6 Cowboys at 4:30 p.m. Thursday at AT&T Stadium in Arlington, Texas.

Milton will become the first Eagles quarterback to face his former team since Nick Foles beat the Rams at L.A. Coliseum late in the 2018 season. Foles spent 2015 with the Rams in between his two stints with the Eagles.

Prescott said he saw tremendous growth from Milton during their time together understanding the cerebral side of the game.

"Just the mental side of the game and just the preparation and just whether it be all the different nuances that we're asked to do that aren't really ever seen whether it be protection, adjustments, and things like that, or just getting into the right play," he said.

"And then ultimately, your reads and anticipating as the play goes on. He made big strides. And it's the reason why he's a starting quarterback in this league."

Milton will be facing a defense Thursday that he faced only a few weeks ago running scout team for the Cowboys, so he's got a good working knowledge of what he's about to go up against, definitely an advantage on a short week when film and practice time are at a premium.

"Whenever you're doing scout team there's those are not reps that you take off," he said. "Those are reps where you can get better, work at your craft and also kind of get a tell from a defense on what they're doing that play against that team, whatever week you're the scout team quarterback.

"So there's times where in the scout team when I was there, just getting those reps and seeing it, looking back over the tape and just stealing reps. The best way I can to get better at my position, even though I'm getting a look for them. But it was just pretty much just mainly, 'Am I taking the right drops,' or, 'Am I seeing this play correctly even though it wasn't a play from Dallas playbook at the time.' But still reps of getting better and recognizing defenses."

#2820. Lakers blow 20-point lead, lose to Spurs and miss NBA Cup quarterfinals

Victor Wembanyama slammed a reverse two-handed dunk over Walker Kessler. On the next play, he volleyball spiked Collin Sexton's layup attempt into the front row at Frost Bank Center. San Antonio fans wagged their index fingers toward the court.

The "MVP" chants that so often greet Luka Doncic even in visiting arenas belong to the Spurs' French superstar here.

Wembanyama's 25 points and 16 rebounds led the Spurs to a 99-96 comeback win over the Lakers on Friday in a thrilling matchup between the top two teams in the Western Conference. The Lakers (15-4) squandered a 20-point first-half lead that cost them a spot in the NBA Cup knockout round. Instead of advancing to the quarterfinals of the in-season tournament, the Lakers will play regular-season games at Oklahoma City on Dec. 6 at 4 p.m. PST and at Crypto.com Arena on Dec. 10 at 7 p.m. against the Utah Jazz.

Read more: 'All about giving': Why the Lakers' Ziaire Williams enjoys giving back

Doncic had 31 points, eight assists and six rebounds, but he continues to struggle on three-point shots. He entered Friday's game shooting 26.6% from three during the last five games and went just one for 13 from beyond the arc in the much-anticipated showdown. He and Austin Reaves, who returned from a five-game absence with a left heel bruise, were the only Lakers to make multiple field goals during the second half when the Spurs (16-4) erased a 16-point halftime deficit during the third quarter and started the fourth on a 13-5 run.

Reaves finished with 21 points and eight rebounds, but seven turnovers. He scored 13 in the fourth quarter, including a three with 34 seconds to go that brought the Lakers to within three before the Spurs inched away from the free-throw line in front of a raucous home crowd that the Lakers compared to a playoff atmosphere.

"They're disappointed right now and that's a natural human emotion from losing a game," Lakers coach JJ Redick said. "I told them this morning and I said again after the game, 'We're gonna get feedback. And we wake up tomorrow if we won the game or we lost the game, we're still a good team on Saturday morning.' So we'll keep getting better. That's the goal of the season."

Lakers guard Luka Doncic, putting up a shot against Spurs guard Dylan Harper, finished with 31 points on Friday night but only made one of 13 three-point shots. (Ronald Cortes / Getty Images)

The Lakers had the league's best record entering the game but it came with outside skepticism. They had only played eight games against teams currently better than .500. They hadn't played a winning team since narrowly surviving a comeback attempt from the Atlanta Hawks on the road on Nov. 9. Three games — at San Antonio, Memphis and Houston — on their current four-game trip could serve as early statement opportunities.

The Lakers looked every bit the championship contenders they hoped to be during the first half. They ended the first quarter on an 8-0 run that chewed up more than four minutes.

The Lakers fought to keep the Spurs (16-4) scoreless even when playing without Kessler against Wembanyama. The French superstar got swarmed by Jarred Vanderbilt, Sandro Mamukelashvili and even switched onto 6-foot-5 guard Quentin Grimes yet was held to just two points and three rebounds during the first quarter in a too-early most valuable player, Doncic led the Lakers to a 13-point first-quarter lead.

"I was surprised of the quality of the game that they offered and he offered, collectively," Wembanyama said. "I mean, I know what to expect of Luka, but as a team — I wasn't sure what they were built of just from the start of the season, but it's real. It's for real, they're good."

The Lakers orchestrated a massive sign-and-trade to get Kessler this offseason for matchups like this one. Kessler finished with 10 points and 13 rebounds while Wembanyama was an inefficient 10-for-25 shooting from the field.

"It's not easy to guard [Wembanyama]," said Doncic, who told Lakers executives he wanted to add Kessler. "I think he did a fantastic job, and he's becoming the Walker we need."

The reigning Western Conference champions led the league with 48.4 rebounds per game but got outrebounded during the first half 24-15. The Spurs won the rebounding battle 47-43 and got the dagger three-pointer from Stephon Castle with 43 seconds remaining off an offensive rebound after a missed free throw by Wembanyama. Instead of a chance to tie the score, the Lakers got the ball back down by six.

The Spurs had 20 second-chance points and 28 points off 17 Lakers turnovers.

"There's a lot to learn," Kessler said. "A team as good as San Antonio … you can't waste possessions because they're gonna capitalize on that."

After never coming close to the playoff hunt during four seasons with the Utah Jazz, Kessler marveled at the playoff-like atmosphere in the sold-out Frost Bank Center. The Jackals, the team's Wembanyama-backed super fan section, chanted and pounded on drums. The crowd roared when Keldon Johnson tied the score with a corner three at the end of the third quarter.

Emotions were still high after the game when Doncic routinely threw his armband into the crowd and got into an altercation with a fan. After he turned back to retrieve the armband, a fan shoved Doncic away. Lakers security and forward Jarred Vanderbilt stepped in.

"I just heard somebody say they didn't want [my wristband]," Doncic said. "I went back to get it, and then he pushed me, and I just gave the wristband to somebody else."
