#2819. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

#2816. Historic comebacks and much more in this week's Roob's Eagles Stats

Historic comebacks and much more in this week's Roob's Eagles Stats originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

If you want Joe Milton stats, and I'm sure you do, click here. And if you're looking for Jake Elliott stats, and why wouldn't you, they're all here.

For all other unique and fascinating stats off the Eagles' remarkable comeback win over the Cowboys, you're in the right place!

And I'll tell you one thing: This weekly stats piece is a heck of a lot more interesting when the Eagles win.

MIRACLE COMEBACK: We noted after the game the comeback from 21 points down tied the Eagles' 4th-biggest all-time. But it gets better. The Eagles had won just three of 209 previous games when trailing by 14 or more points through three quarters. Yep, they were 3-206. The only previous wins came in 1978, when they beat the Colts 17-14 at Memorial Stadium after trailing 14-0 after three quarters; in 1985, when they beat the Bills 21-17 at the Vet after trailing 17-0 starting the fourth quarter; and in 2010 with the 38-31 win over the Giants at MetLife after trailing 24-10 going into the fourth quarter. The Cowboys had only lost one game in franchise history after leading by 14 going into the fourth quarter and that was in overtime. In 2022, they led the Packers 28-14 after three quarters at Lambeau before losing 31-28 in OT. So this was the first time in their 67-year history they've blown a 14-point lead after three quarters at home.

THE MAGIC OF DEVONTA SMITH: DeVonta Smith became the sixth Eagle with a 200-yard receiving game, the first since DeSean Jackson had 210 yards against the Cowboys at AT&T Stadium in 2010. Those are the only road 200-yard games an Eagle has ever had, both in the same stadium. The only other 200-yard games were by Bud Grant (203 vs. Texans at Shibe Park in 1952), Tommy McDonald (237 vs. Giants at Franklin Field in 1961), Pete Retzlaff (204 vs. Washington at Franklin Field in 1965) and Kevin Curtis vs. the Lions (221 vs. Lions at the Linc in 2007). Smith is the sixth receiver with 200 yards against the Cowboys and first since Calvin Johnson had 329 yards in the Lions' win at Ford Field in 2013. Smith and Jackson are the only opposing receivers with 200 yards in Dallas since Johnny Morris of the Bears had 201 in 1962. Smith increased his career total to 5,570 yards and passed McDonald (5,499) to move into seventh in Eagles history. With 11 catches, he increased his total to 470 and passed Brian Westbrook (469) to move into fifth. His 17th career 100-yard game moved him into a tie for 6th-most in Eagles history with Hall of Famer Tommy McDonald. Next on the list are Harold Carmichael, Mike Quick and DeSean Jackson with 21.

A BIG ONE AND ANOTHER BIG ONE: With his 45- and 50-yard TD catches, Smith became the sixth player in franchise history with multiple touchdowns of 45 yards or more in a game. Hall of Famer Tommy McDonald was the first Eagle todo that with TDs of 52 and 64 yards from Norm Van Brocklin at Washington in 1960. In a 45-35 loss at St. Louis in 1962, both McDonald and Timmy Brown had multiple TD catches of at least 45 yards from Sonny Jurgensen in the same game – 56 and 60 yards for McDonald and 60 and 82 yards for Brown. In 1967, Norm Snead threw TDs of 63 and 69 yards to Ben Hawkins in a 35-35 tie in Washington and it didn't happen again until 1994, when Fred Barnett caught TDs of 47 and 50 yards from Randall Cunningham in a 17-7 win over the Cards at the Vet. And in 2019, Carson Wentz threw TD passes of 51 and 53 yards to Jackson in a 32-27 win over Washington at the Linc. Smith is only the eighth player with two 45-yard TDs in a game against the Cowboys but the second this month. Eleven days earlier, Christian McCaffrey had TDs of 66 and 74 yards from Brock Purdy in the 49ers' 46-40 win over the Cowboys. They are the first against Dallas since Randy Moss of the Vikings had three long TD catches from Cunningham in 1998 – 51, 56 and 56 yards – in a 46-36 Vikings win. Smith's 37th career TD catch tied Jeremy Maclin and Jackson for 9th-most in Eagles history. Next on that list are Dallas Goedert (42) and Zach Ertz (40).

FIRST TIME IN 35 YEARS: The win was the Eagles' first in 35 years when allowing 28 or more points before halftime. Last time that happened was Week 11 in 1991, when they trailed the Browns 30-17 at halftime before rallying for a 32-30 win. They're now 3-29 in franchise history when allowing 28 points in a first half. The Cowboys fell to 32-2 all-time when scoring 28 first-half points.

TWO LONG KICKS: With Jake Elliott making a game-winning 59-yard field goal and Brandon Aubrey of the Cowboys kicking a 59-yarder in the third quarter, this was the second game in NFL history where kickers from both teams made field goals of at least 59 yards. Aubrey of course also missed a 40-yarder, opening the door for the Eagles' comeback. The only other time both kickers made field goals of 59 yards or more was last November, when Chargers kicker and former Eagle Cameron Dicker and Steelers kicker Chris Boswell both made 59-yarders in a 25-10 Chargers win at SoFi Stadium. Opposing kickers have made more field goals of 59 yards or more against the Eagles over the last 3 ½ years (6-for-8) as they made in the Eagles' first 90 years (4-for-6).

MAKAI LEMON CLIMBING THE CHARTS: With five more catches, Makai Lemon increased his season total to 43, which leads all NFL rookies. The Browns' K.C. Concepcion is second with 40. With six games to go, Lemon's 43 receptions are 10th-most in Eagles history by a rookie, seven behind Miles Sanders and Lee Bougess for eighth place. He's on pace for 73 catches, which would be the most ever by an Eagles rookie wide receiver. Jordan Matthews had 67 in 2014. His 33 catches in his last seven games are 3rd-most ever by an Eagles rookie in any seven-game stretch, behind Matthews' 36 in 2014 and Don Looney's 36 in 1940.

THE AMAZING BRADEN MANN: With an 89-yard punt against the Giants, a 70-yarder against the Steelers and a 68-yarder Thursday in Dallas, Braden Mann is the only player in NFL history with a punt of at least 68 yards in three consecutive games. Mann has always broken the franchise record for highest punting average (minimum three punts) twice in five days. Until the Steelers game, Mat McBriar held the franchise record at 56.6 in an overtime loss to the Lions in 2012. Mann broke that by averaging 59.3 against the Steelers, then broke that record by averaging 62.7 Thursday night in Dallas. Mann's 62.7 is 11th-highest in NFL history.

JALEN CARTER PILING UP THE SACKS: Jalen Carter's sixth sack matched his career high set as a rookie in 2023. Carter is third among interior linemen in sacks this year behind only Jeffrey Simmons and Quinnen Williams, who each have nine. Since 2023, his 19 ½ sacks are 5th-most among interior linemen, behind Simmons (30 ½), Chris Jones (26.0), DeForest Buckner (22 ½) and Maliek Collins (20). With 19 ½ sacks, he moved into a tie with Mike Pitts for 8th-most all-time by an Eagles' interior lineman. He needs four to pass Javon Hargrave and move into seventh. After recording just 10 sacks in their first eight games, the Eagles have 14 in their last three.

SACK LEGENDS: Gabe Hall recorded his first career sack against the Steelers and Jeremiah Trotter Jr. and Ty Robinson each had their first career sack against the Cowboys. This is the first time two Eagles have recorded their first career sack in consecutive games since 2018, when Kamu Grugier-Hill recorded his first career sack against the Rams and Jared Goff in Week 15 and Treyvon Hester picked up his first a week later against Deshaun Watson and the Texans. Trotter and Robinson are the first Eagles teammates to record their first career sack in the same game since 2011, when Phillip Hunt and Casey Matthews both had their first sacks in a 26-10 win over the Dolphins, Hunt on J.P. Losman and Matthews on Matt Moore.

6TH-ROUND HISTORY: With 2023 Eagles 6th-round pick Tanner McKee and 2024 Patriots 6th-round pick Joe Milton each starting three games, the Eagles are the eighth team in NFL history with two 6th-round quarterbacks each starting at least three games. The only other teams with two 6th-round QBs in the last two decades are the 2009 Rams (Marc Bulger, Keith Null) and 2020 Jaguars (Jake Luton, Gardner Minshew III). Other teams to start two 6th-round picks: 1979 Bears (Vince Evans, Bob Avellini), 1987 Raiders (Evans, Rusty Hilger), 1990 Washington (Stan Humphries, Mark Rypien), 1993 Chargers (Stan Humphries, John Friesz), 1997 Chargers (Humphries, Craig Whelihan).

BITS AND PIECES: Drew Mukuba's four interceptions are the most by an Eagles rookie in his first 22 career games since Jordan Hicks had five over the 2015 and 2016 seasons and ties the most by a defensive back since Hall of Famer Eric Allen had eight in his first 22 games in 1988 and 1989 … The Cowboys outgained the Eagles by 47 yards (416-369), the ninth straight game the Eagles have been outgained by at least 20 yards. That's the 2nd-longest streak in franchise history. They were outgained by 20 or more yards 10 straight games in 1971. The Eagles have been outgained by an average of 111 yards in their last nine games, the largest yardage margin against them in any nine-game span since 1999, when they were outgained by 121 yards per game … The Cowboys rushed for 204 yards in the first game and 151 yards Thursday. Their 355 total rushing yards are their most against the Eagles since 1993, when they had 453 … Robinson is the first player in Eagles history with a blocked punt that was returned for a touchdown and a sack in the same game … Milton has been sacked 15 times in his three starts, matching the most sacks by any Eagles QB in a three-game span since Donovan McNabb was sacked 18 times by the Giants, Jets and Bears in 2007 … Milton is only the third Eagles QB with 300 yards and three TDs in a game against the Cowboys. Jurgensen had a 351-yard, five-TD game in 1961, and McNabb had a 345-yard, four-TD game in Dallas in 2004 … With Jalen Hurts, McKee, Andy Dalton and Joe Milton now all with at least one interception, this is the first time since 1991 and the second time since 1965 that the Eagles have had four different players in a season with an INT. In 1991, Jim McMahon threw 11 interceptions, Brad Goebel threw six, Jeff Kemp five, Pat Ryan four and Keith Byars one … The loss was the Cowboys' fourth this year when they've scored at least 30 points, tying the most in NFL history. The 2002 Chiefs and 2024 Bengals also did that … The Eagles had 86 penalty yards Thursday. Seven of their last 11 games with at least 85 penalty yards have come against the Cowboys … The Eagles had lost 22 consecutive games when they've allowed 38 points since a 45-38 win over the Giant in 2009. They're now 4-78 in franchise history when allowing at least 38 points.

#2815. Agencies announce dollar thresholds for smaller loan exemption from appraisal requirements for higher-priced mortgage loans

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 3:00 p.m. EST Share

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau, the Federal Reserve Board, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that the 2027 threshold for higher-priced mortgage loans that are subject to special appraisal requirements will increase from $34,200 to $35,500.

The threshold amount will be effective January 1, 2027, and is based on the 3.9 percent annual percentage increase in the Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers, known as CPI-W, as of June 1, 2026.

The Dodd-Frank Act added special appraisal requirements for higher-priced mortgage loans to the Truth in Lending Act, including that creditors obtain a written appraisal based on a physical visit to the interior of the home before making a higher-priced mortgage loan. The rules implementing these requirements contain an exemption for loans at or below a threshold amount that is adjusted annually to reflect CPI-W increases.

#2814. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2813. Stats Rundown: 7 numbers to know from the Mavericks 113-109 win over the Suns

The Dallas Mavericks (12-7) beat the Phoenix Suns (9-9) Friday night in Phoenix on the final night of Emirates Cup group play. Dallas was the first team to qualify for the knockout rounds thanks to their 3-0 record in Cup action entering the night and a Phoenix loss to the Houston Rockets earlier this week. How they exited the night was even better, despite a shaky start.

The Mavericks held an 18-5 advantage to start the game, but when the Phoenix bench unit joined the action, they turned the game around. By the end of the first quarter, Dallas was trailing by a point in no small part due to a minus-17 differential in bench unit scoring.

The Mavericks had a dreadful stretch from the three-point line in the first half, at one point missing 12 straight attempts. Meanwhile, a total lack of three-point defense made for a terrible combination. In the first half alone, Dallas was outscored by 21 points from beyond the arc. The Mavericks were 3-of-16 from downtown across the first 24 minutes of action.

The Mavericks were down by 21 points and looking listless, but then some sticky-fingered steals and locked-in defense helped the Mavs outscore the Suns by 15 points across a 23-8 scoring run. Dallas looked like a completely different team, particularly to start the fourth quarter.

Following on that scoring run, Dallas really locked in and held Phoenix to only 14 fourth quarter points. Meanwhile, they dropped 35 points of their own for the highest scoring quarter of the night for either team.

Cooper Flagg was the super star, but Christie got in on the hero action as well to ice the game. Christie nailed two free throws after soaring for a rebound, giving the Mavs a five-point lead and a near death-grip on the game. Moments later with the lead cut to two, Christie again stepped up and swished two more free throws to seal an incredible comeback.

After an 'oh-for' from downtown last game, Flagg finally broke through to join Kobe Bryant as the only other teenager in league history to score 1,500 or more points and hit 100 or more three-pointers before turning 20 years old.

With the win, the Mavericks finish group play with a 4-0 record, getting them the top overall seed as things move into the knockout round.

#2812. Agencies announce dollar thresholds for applicability of truth in lending and consumer leasing rules for consumer credit and lease transactions

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 3:00 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board and the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau today announced the dollar thresholds used to determine whether certain consumer credit and lease transactions in 2027 are subject to certain protections under Regulation Z (Truth in Lending) and Regulation M (Consumer Leasing).

By law, the agencies are required to adjust the thresholds annually based on the annual percentage increase in the Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers, known as CPI-W. Transactions at or below the thresholds are subject to the protections of the regulations.

Specifically, based on the 3.9 percent annual percentage increase in the CPI-W as of June 1, 2026, Regulation Z and Regulation M generally will apply to consumer credit transactions and consumer leases of $76,300 or less in 2027. However, private education loans and loans secured by real property, such as mortgages, are subject to Regulation Z regardless of the amount of the loan.

Federal Register notice: Consumer Leasing (Regulation M) (PDF)

Federal Register notice: Truth in Lending (Regulation Z) (PDF)

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

#2810. Miami Dolphins offensive line review: Tracking the guards in Week 11

Folks, this was not a good game on offense. For one, it was the sixth time this season the Dolphins were held under 14 points, leaving them last in the NFL in scoring. For another, Kyle McCord completed just 12 of 33 passes for 86 net yards. The offensive line was far from solely responsible for the struggles, but it certainly didn't help matters.

Still, rookie left guard Kadyn Proctor and second-year right guard Jonah Savaiinaea remain key pieces of the Dolphins' future. Each week, we take a closer look at Proctor and Savaiinaea and examine what analysts are saying about their most recent performances.

The game against the Bills showed a lot of the same problems we've seen the last few weeks. Austin Jackson (PFF grade: an offense-worst 34.6!) put up very little resistance against Bills defensive lineman Greg Rousseau, who logged two sacks of Dolphins quarterback Kyle McCord. McCord also continued to hold the ball for too long, and the team's pass catchers need to keep working on their blocking.

Kadyn Proctor, fresh off of one of his best games this season against the Colts (by PFF standards), had his worst against the Bills (by PFF standards). While he didn't give up any sacks, he did allow 1 quarterback hit, 3 hurries and 4 pressures. His issues appeared to stem largely from rookie mistakes: hesitation, slow recognition when helping teammates and getting caught by Deone Walker's swim move. That said, in taking a spin around Dolphins analysts looking at the offensive line, most either viewed Proctor's performance as adequate or spent more time debating whether he should finish the season at right tackle (more on that later).

The same was true for Jonah Savaiinaea, but in reverse. By PFF standards, his performances against the Bills and Colts were his best since Week 2, and he also allowed only 1 pressure and 1 hurry, with no QB hits or sacks. Still, there were a fair amount of mistakes that need to be cleaned up. He still had his share of losing reps, including several against Deone Walker, and at one point he and Austin Jackson opened up a huge running lane for a defensive tackle to record a tackle for loss. I'm hopeful that six weeks of Savaiinaea's well-below-average play is obscuring his more recent only-below-average play, and he can keep turning around his on-field reputation. It's worth mentioning that Savaiinaea missed a few plays at the end of the first half due to an ankle injury, but he played the entire second half.

As to the "Proctor playing right tackle" question, it's worth mentioning that Mel Kiper (whose reputation these days feels on par with PFF's) labels guard and tackle among the weakest in the 2027 draft. Maybe that's an argument for moving Proctor outside. Then again, given how difficult it has been to find quality guard play, I could be convinced the Dolphins should leave well enough alone.

The tracker will be updated each week throughout the season. For a more in-depth breakdown of Proctor's and Savaiinaea's performance, check out offensive line Coach Eric Smith's film analysis of the game, which you can find on his YouTube channel, Focused on Fins.

#2808. Switzerland’s biggest bank set for showdown with lawmakers as capital fight intensifies amid crisis planning

UBS's crisis planning has won qualified support from market regulators, which called its plan "executable" in principle. But Switzerland's biggest bank still faces a major fight over sweeping plans that would force it to hold more capital against its foreign subsidiaries.

FINMA, the Swiss markets regulator, said Wednesday that UBS's resolvability — or its ability to fail safely, without wreaking havoc on the economy — has improved since it integrated rival Credit Suisse, with progress made in implementing the emergency plan across group-wide resolution planning.

The preferred resolution strategy — involving plans to allow for recapitalization and restructuring through the use of loss-absorbing bail-in bonds — is "executable," FINMA said. The regulator formally approved UBS's recovery plan for the first time since its ​Credit Suisse integration.

How UBS would withstand a future crisis remains in sharp focus among Swiss authorities ever since the lender's emergency takeover of stricken rival Credit Suisse in 2023.

UBS is now the country's only global systemically important bank, and the Swiss government wants it to hold stronger capital cushions, specifically in relation to its foreign subsidiaries, which would help it withstand another banking collapse.

UBS has pushed back against the overhaul, saying the measures are disproportionate and would damage its international competitiveness, forcing it to hold an extra $16 billion in capital.

A parliamentary showdown now looms, with the National Council, Switzerland's lower house, scheduled to debate the proposal Dec. 16.

On Tuesday, the lower house's economics committee backed plans to force the bank to support its foreign subsidiaries with 90% high-quality core Common Equity Tier 1, or CET1, capital. The Council of States, the country's upper house, approved the 90% plan in September.

The Swiss government had earlier proposed a 100% threshold. UBS wants it to be no more than 50%.

UBS CEO Sergio Ermotti told CNBC in an interview Oct. 28 that 90% would not allow the company to be a "competitive player" long-term. But he would not be drawn on speculation that the measures could push the bank to relocate out of Switzerland.

In a statement, the bank said the economics committee's recommendation would herald further excessive tightening of Swiss capital requirements, "which are already among the most stringent globally."

FINMA said in its Nov. 25 update that the goal is to avoid government solvency support and emergency law.

"The aim is to enable the authorities, in the event of a crisis, to choose the option that offers the best prospects of success in safeguarding financial stability and ensuring the continuation of systemically important functions," the regulator said.

#2807. Things get physical as Spurs fan shoves Luka Doncic in wild exchange with Lakers star

The Los Angeles Lakers couldn't hold on to a 20-point lead Friday night, and Luka Doncic couldn't hold on to his temper.

Moments after the San Antonio Spurs stunned the Lakers with a 99-96 comeback victory in their NBA Cup Group C matchup, Doncic got into a heated altercation with two courtside fans that required security to intervene.

Video captured by Amazon Prime Video's postgame broadcast showed Doncic walking toward the visitors' locker room when a woman sitting courtside appeared to say something to the Lakers superstar.

Luka Doncic of the Los Angeles Lakers reacts during a game against the San Antonio Spurs in San Antonio, Texas, Friday, Nov. 27th, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Doncic routinely tossed his white wristband at the woman before continuing toward the tunnel. Seconds later, he doubled back to retrieve it.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic #77 is shoved by a Spurs' fan sitting courtside in San Antonio. Amazon Prime

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic #77 is shoved by a Spurs' fan sitting courtside in San Antonio.

The Los Angeles Lakers blowing a 20-point lead against the San Antonio Spurs on Friday was bad. What happened immediately after was ugly, with Lakers star Luka Dončić getting shoved by a courtside fan.

#2799. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2796. Timberwolves 109, Grizzlies 114: No Ball, No Business

The Minnesota Wolves will miss their chance to hang a banner before they retire Kevin Garnett's jersey, as the NBA Cup and its point differential rules have booted the Wolves from the tournament.

The Memphis Grizzlies and their second-ranked offense overcame the Wolves' stout defense. Combine that with the Portland Trail Blazers routing the Golden State Warriors and the San Antonio Spurs defeating the Los Angeles Lakers in a nail-biter, and Minnesota not booking airfare to the next round.

Instead, they'll meet two other non-tourney teams in mid-December (which may help their record quite a bit; they're 6-0 in those makeup games since the addition of the NBA Cup).

In the first two quarters, the Grizzlies came out as the team that wanted it more. They turned Minnesota over 10 times, scoring 14 points off Wolves mistakes, and out-rebounded Minnesota 25-22 despite starting center Zach Edey picking up 4 fouls in seven minutes and his fifth less than a minute into the third quarter.

On the night, the Grizzlies won the second-chance points battle 25-11 and the points off turnovers 27-19. That was all the difference in what ended up being a close game

This Wolves team looks tired. Their usual rebounding prowess looked a step slow the whole night. On multiple occasions, the Wolves were stripped under their own hoop for easy Memphis buckets. These types of mistakes often show a team losing focus.

Grizzlies head coach Tuomas Iisalo loves to make quick rotations, and his bench often runs 11 deep. As such, Memphis plays with a seemingly boundless supply of energy, with legs used to quicker sprints.

Combine all of this with the Wolves' persistent free-throw shooting problems, and it's impressive how close a finish it was.

Minnesota has a banged-up squad right now. Their four injured players are exclusively in the guard rotation, and that was felt in the Wolves' inability to initiate offense cleanly. When their passes weren't getting picked off (Memphis had 12 steals on the night), they were getting tipped en route, causing the intended target to scramble to recover the ball with all energy removed from the play.

This was the first game this season the Wolves played without LaMelo Ball. They missed his passing, his organization, and his ability to be a threat from deep.

The injury report is no place to rely on excuses, though, as seven of Memphis' players were also out tonight. Early in the first half, Iisalo turned to two-way player Javon Small, who brought boundless aggression looking for his own shot. He scored 12 straight points in the first quarter and finished the night with 19 in his nine minutes of play.

When Finch tried to match and turned to his deep bench, he found little there. Two-way guard Zyon Pullin saw some of his first meaningful minutes of the season and did little to earn himself glory. In five minutes, he recorded a single turnover and no other stat. He did not have the sense of urgency to match the moment and, at one point, was chided by Rudy to move quicker to get the ball into play.

Rounding it all out, Ty Jerome continued his Most Improved Player campaign with efficient shooting and few mistakes for the Wolves to capitalize on.

Jaden has been sensational these past few games. He started this bout off with a couple of blocks and saw his matchup dribble all the way back out to the perimeter for fear of getting swatted again. Three fouls, at least one of them a cheap shove far from the basket, kept him on the bench to finish the first half.

His offense got going in the third quarter as the Wolves took a short lived lead. His three ball was not falling so he turned to his midrange game, continuing to use his change of pace to throw his Grizzlies defenders off balance. In the fourth quarter the kettle bubbled over.

It wasn't all bad. The Wolves overcame a 16-point first-half deficit. When the Grizzlies ballooned their lead back to a dozen in the fourth, they scrapped and clawed until the final buzzer to close that gap to five points.

While I was watching the game tonight, my wife was watching Indiana Jones: The Last Crusade. I espied a few moments of the classic flick and was struck by the classic trope that when a hero, in this case Indy, punches a villain, in this case a Nazi, they crumple. However, when Indy takes a punch – sometimes several of them in a row, right to the kidney – while holding onto the whip wrapped around his father's legs, preventing him from being crushed by a tank, he weathers all of them and doesn't let go of the whip.

In every game this season, the Wolves have clawed back to make it competitive in the fourth quarter. They have lost their six games by a grand total of 36 points combined. For years, this was a team that played with arrogance any time they had a decent lead, like a villain thinking there was no way their plans would be foiled. And then the opponent would throw a punch, and the Wolves would be left weak-legged and stumbling.

This team does not quit. They are the heroes in their own story. If you knock them down, they will get up again.

The Wolves head on to face the Sacramento Kings with all of the pent-up aggression from letting this game slip away. The Kings have been miserable this season, though they do have a recent win over the Boston Celtics that reminded fans that the Beam does not need to have its bulb replaced. That will be available on DAZN.

#2793. Private markets are more accessible than ever for investors. Getting out is another matter

Retail investors are lining up to back private funds. But education on how restrictive these funds can be, particularly on when and how much money they can get back, may not be keeping pace with the influx of new capital.

PwC research has found that 77% of fund distributors surveyed said access to private markets is becoming important or very important to what they plan to offer clients, but just 15% of asset managers said they provide comprehensive education for retail investors.

Unlike the public markets retail investors are more used to, some private-market vehicles limit when investors can get their money back to set repurchase windows, and even then may return only part of what they request.

A spike in redemption requests at several private-credit funds earlier this year exposed the tension between giving investors regular opportunities to cash out and holding assets that can be difficult to sell quickly.

Blackstone restricted withdrawals from its $79 billion flagship nontraded BCRED private credit fund to 5% of shares in June, after redemption requests hit 10% during the second quarter. Apollo also capped withdrawals in its Apollo Debt Solutions vehicle at 5% after second-quarter redemption requests surged to almost 17%. Blue Owl, Morgan Stanley and Partners Group also limited investor withdrawals from some of their funds.

Limitations on redemptions are a key trade-off in structures such as LTAFs in the U.K., ELTIFs in the EU and interval funds in the U.S., which broaden access to less-liquid assets.

It's emerged as a major pressure point as those structures come under closer scrutiny.

"Private markets can have very different characteristics from the products many wealth clients are used to, particularly around liquidity, valuations and holding periods," said Albertha Charles, global asset and wealth management leader at PwC.

"Investors and advisers need to understand how these products work, including their liquidity and valuation characteristics, so they can make informed decisions about whether they are appropriate."

PwC's 2026 Asset Management Revolution report, published Nov. 23, surveyed 250 asset managers, 100 institutional investors and 100 distributors across 36 countries and regions. More than half of respondents represented firms with over $20 billion in assets under management.

Among the study's key findings, education and liquidity are seen as the main hurdles as private markets reach a broader investor base. Distributors said better advisor and client education would help expand access, while liquidity, lock-ups and suitability requirements remain major barriers. Private equity and private credit were also the areas where distributors saw some of the biggest gaps in advisor knowledge.

Private credit appears likely to draw the strongest demand — two-thirds (66%) of distributors expect it to lead client interest in private markets, compared with 46% for private equity.

Regulators in several major markets are creating structures to widen access to long-term and illiquid assets. The U.K. uses long-term asset funds, or LTAFs, while the EU's European long-term investment funds, known as ELTIFs, can be marketed across member states.

In the U.S., interval funds provide periodic, rather than daily, liquidity. Singapore, Japan and Hong Kong have also taken steps to broaden retail or listed access to private assets. The frameworks differ, but all aim to make less-liquid investments workable within existing distribution, suitability and liquidity requirements.

Yen Sum, global chair of the private capital practice at Latham & Watkins, and a member of its executive committee, said access to private-market funds is generally limited to investors who certify they are qualified or sophisticated.

She told CNBC on Thursday that the key issue for investor education centers on how private assets differ in liquidity, maturities and return-of-capital terms, and how higher rates can pressure leveraged companies — rather than simply whether regulation should curb access.

#2792. Week 12 NFL picks against the spread, predictions for every game: Can the Seahawks, or anyone, stop the 49ers?

Last season, the San Francisco 49ers won a close game at the Seattle Seahawks. And then after that the Seahawks became a dominant Super Bowl champion, including two blowout wins of the 49ers.

We have the 49ers' road win over the Seahawks out of the way already this season. But this season San Francisco is the team that looks like a future Super Bowl champion.

The 49ers have been great this season, including a dominant win over the Minnesota Vikings last week. The offense has been spectacular. And the defense, ranked seventh in DVOA, has been one of the biggest surprises of the season. That defense has brought the 49ers from being a good team to a potentially great one. Kyle Shanahan, who should be NFL Coach of the Year, was going to field a potent offense. The defense completes the circle.

However, we can't count out the Seahawks quite yet. The defense has been tremendous again. The offense hasn't been great, but a lot of that is attributed to Sam Darnold playing through injuries almost all season, when he has been able to play. Darnold should return this week, off the Seahawks' bye, and if he's a little healthier and a bit more protective of the ball, Seattle can still play at a high level.

That's why the Seahawks are not a big underdog this week, even though they play at a 9-1 49ers team. The 49ers are just a 3-point favorite. Not setting the spread above a field goal shows a lot of respect for the reigning champions. But maybe it's not showing enough respect for the 49ers.

Brock Purdy and the San Francisco 49ers beat the Seahawks 32-29 back in Week 5. (Photo by Alika Jenner/Getty Images)

San Francisco has shown no signs of vulnerability. It had no issues with the Vikings defense, which is the only defense on the same level as Seattle. This looks like a great San Francisco team, and that's why 49ers -3 is the pick. And if the Seahawks can't slow down the 49ers, nobody else in the NFC might be able to do it either.

Here are the rest of the picks against the spread for Week 12 of the NFL season:

The Bengals should be upset after losing in the final seconds at Washington on Monday night. And they can take it out on the Saints, who have lost three of four with the only win being when they held off an overtime two-point conversation at home against Shedeur Sanders and the Browns. Joe Burrow should have another monster game.

Kyle McCord was reasonable in his first start, but that was against the Colts. Last week at Buffalo the Dolphins had what was probably their worst game of the season, and that's saying something for a 1-9 team. The Jets aren't good, but they are coming off a win over the Chargers, and they'll look better when they're playing teams down at their level.

The worst thing to happen to Jesse Minter is the defense struggling as badly as it has. If the Ravens were losing because the offense was inept without Lamar Jackson, it would be easy to give him a pass. But Jacoby Brissett has been reasonable and the defense, which is Minter's specialty, has been awful. Baltimore has allowed 32.4 points per game over the past five weeks. The Texans can move the ball and now see an opening for a wild-card spot with the upheaval in Pittsburgh.

It's hard to take any underdog lately, with the favorites on a roll the past few weeks. And the Falcons aren't a comfortable underdog to take, but Atlanta has enough talent that it's still the only team to beat the 49ers. The Falcons are coming off a bye, and maybe that helps them keep it close.

Switching quarterbacks can give a team a short-term boost, especially when that quarterback was the worst in the NFL this season. Daniel Jones wasn't giving the Colts a chance. Anthony Richardson Sr. hasn't been good in the NFL, but he can't be worse than Jones has been. Maybe there's a jolt that gives Indianapolis a chance to keep this one close.

It would have been nice to see the Titans play better at Dallas last week, but they did end up scoring 30 points. The offense has been decent lately. The problem is the defense, and that's why it's scary to take them even getting more than two touchdowns. But that's a big spread in the NFL.

This game could be Tommy DeVito vs. D.J. Uiagalelei, if Justin Herbert doesn't clear concussion protocol. Since there's no reason to rush Herbert back we'll assume he won't play, and then this could turn into one of the ugliest games in the NFL this season.

The Cardinals have shown no signs of life lately, after trading Jacoby Brissett, watching Gardner Minshew II go down to injury, losing all their receivers due to injury or trades and then firing their defensive coordinator this week. Whether rookie Carson Beck returns from a back injury or Josh Johnson has to start again, it doesn't really matter. The Commanders aren't great but we can't trust Arizona.

Fernando Mendoza didn't look bad last week. The Raiders' defense is still terrible and maybe the Browns break out against it, but Cleveland's offense has its issues too. The Browns play close games, that's what they do, and we'll assume another close one on Sunday night.

Tampa Bay is getting very little out of Baker Mayfield. But the Buccaneers can still run the ball and play very good defense. A win for Tampa Bay would still leave them at 4-7, but they'd be just a game behind the Panthers for first place and clinch the head-to-head tiebreaker. The first meeting was an absolute blowout, and while that won't repeat, it showed that Tampa Bay is a hard matchup for Carolina.

#2790. Game Recap: Suns squander 21-point lead in 113-109 loss to Mavericks

The Phoenix Suns, after leading by as many as 21 points, coughed up a 15-point fourth-quarter lead to the Dallas Mavericks, losing 113-109 on Friday night in NBA Cup Group play.

Cooper Flagg was as advertised, posting 34 points, 7 rebounds, 8 assists, 5 steals, and 4 blocks. Video game numbers. Despite that, outside of a predictably slow start courtesy of the starting lineup, the Suns were in control for most of this game, carrying a commanding 90-71 lead into the fourth quarter, which is when they ran up against *dun, dun, dunnn* a zone.

The shooting went cold, and the Suns got sloppy, allowing the Mavericks (on the efforts of Christie and Marshall) to shoot their way back into contention, and by the 5-minute mark the lead was down to six. The shooting never returned for the Suns as the Mavericks turned up the defensive pressure, and even as the Suns managed to keep their head just above water, it was clear to anyone watching that it was only a matter of time. A late offense rebound giving Cooper Flagg and one bucket, on a night when it appeared as if the Suns had finally figured the rebounding thing out, put the Mavericks up three and for all intensive purposes sealed the win.

Collin Gillespie had another monster night for the Suns, posting 14 points, 9 rebounds and, along with Jordan Goodwin, was the catalyst to pulling the Suns free from an early 18-5 hole.

Oso really struggled and had 4 careless turnovers in the first quarter. He looks confused. The fluidity that he used to bring on the offensive end isn't there. Meanwhile, Malauch continues to look good in the minutes he gets off the bench. I'm a proponent of protecting Malauch's long-term health by slow-playing his development, managing his minutes as he gets adjusted to the grind of an NBA season, but it might be time to reinsert him back into the starting lineup; however, the minutes get worked out.

What's frustrating about a night like tonight is that a lot of things went right for the Suns. After stumbling out of the gate, Collin Gillespie and Jordan Goodwin provided the juice the Suns needed off the bench closing the gap and taking coming out of the first quarter with a 28-29 lead.

The Suns bounced back from their poor shooting night in Houston, going 15-of-40 from three-point range and shooting 48% from the field. They won the rebounding battle 43-42, grabbing 12 on the offensive end. They led the game by 21 points and were on the brink of Dallas waving the white flag and pulling their starters until they weren't.

Dallas hammered the Suns inside, holding a 60-38 edge in points in the paint. They also won the turnover battle 18-11, and while as a team they shot 9-of-20 from three-point range, Christie went 2-of-4, and Marshall went 2-of-5 and hit some big shots that made the comeback possible.

This was a Mavericks team that came into the game as the 8th seed in the West, a play-in team down its second-best player with Kyrie taking the night off. It's nights like these that remind fans that no lead is safe in the NBA, but it's games like these that give me reason to pause and think. Where does this Suns team fall in the Western Conference hierarchy? The road trip proved we weren't a bottom dweller, but the last two games make it feel like landing a 5th or 6th seed in the playoffs is a long shot. If the Suns seem destined for the play-in tournament, what does that mean for this season and roster going forward? It's still early in the season, but not that early, and as the Suns finally gelled over the 4-1 road trip, it's maybe clearer than ever that something is missing.

The starting unit opens up the game for the Suns by digging a huge hole. A series of missed shots, second opportunities and turnovers put the Phoenix Suns into a quick deficit, forcing Ott to turn to the bench early as the Suns fall behind the Mavericks 18-5 with 8:05 seconds left in the first quarter.

The bench answers the call with Jordan Goodwin driving the lane for a quick bucket in the paint and Collin Gillespie going nuclear, running up a quick seven points in 2 minutes on the court, bringing the Suns within 4 points at 18-14. Malauch adds a dunk, and Dunn hits his first three of the night and hits two three-pointers to contribute 5 to the cause as the Suns charge back to reclaim the lead at 29-28 heading into the second quarter.

There continues to be a clear difference in activity, on the offensive end through ball movement, on the glass through offensive rebounding, and on the defensive end in an immediate uptick in deflections when Collin Gillespie and Jordan Goodwin hit the floor.

The Suns open the half with Jalen Green and Dillon Brooks playing with the bench as Collin Gillespie sits after Jordan Ott brought him in early in the first quarter. The Suns continue to see dividends, opening the second quarter with Brooks playing with the bench.

After giving up an opening layup, the Suns pour it on with a three pointer from Kennar, a jumper from Brooks, a three pointer from Green, and another three pointer from Goodwin, ballooning the lead from 1 to 10 and forcing a Dallas timeout.

Dallas battles back to 47-42, but a scoring explosion from Ryan Dunn and Devin Booker push the lead back out and the Suns head into the locker room at halftime leading 63-53.

#2787. Mike McCarthy admits he neglected key Steelers area after Aaron Rodgers criticism

Mike McCarthy appears to have done some self-reflection amid the Aaron Rodgers firestorm.

The Steelers coach admitted he neglected the team's defense a little too much during his first season with the team following a 27-5 throttling of the Broncos at Acrisure Stadium on Black Friday.

"I have not spent enough time with the defense," McCarthy told reporters after the win. "You know, and that's something that, as far as job responsibility, job description, you know, these are things that'll be looked at in the offseason. But, you know, it's a point in our year where we're making adjustments and definitely conscientious of it.

Pittsburgh Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy walks across the field after an NFL football game against the Denver Broncos in Pittsburgh, Friday, Nov. 27, 2026. AP Photo/Gene J. Puskar

"But at the end of the day, we have to all do our jobs and make sure we're giving the players the plan and all the other things that they need that we can perform and play with that type of urgency, physicality that we played today."

Rodgers, who was benched late Tuesday night and released by the team on Wednesday, was critical of McCarthy, an offensive coach, during his appearance on "The Pat McAfee Show" on Wednesday for not forging enough of a relationship with the team's defense players

"There were some guys early on that were … didn't like the way things were going, especially defensive guys. Mike didn't want to get to know the defense," Rodgers claimed. "At one point, I told him, 'You should really lean into the defensive guys a little bit more and talk to those guys,' and he said, 'Well, I don't think I need to.' And I said, 'Well, I disagree with that.' He already kind of lost a lot of those guys."

Rodgers was heavily critical of McCarthy during his appearance on the McAfee show, accusing him of missing offensive meetings, falling asleep in them and being behind on film work by his standards.

#2785. Rheinmetall reins in growth ambitions as Europe's rearmament boom hits hurdles

Rheinmetall is adopting a more "cautious" approach after the German arms maker lost a major contract to build F126 warships for the country's military, in what would have been Rheinmetall's biggest order since World War II.

The tank and ammunition maker now expects sales of between 33 and 37 billion euros (between $36.2 billion and $40.6 billion) by 2030, down from a previous forecast of about 45 billion euros, it told investors at its Capital Markets Day on Friday.

That guidance incorporates a "more prudent approach" for its large vehicle systems and weapons and ammunition businesses.

CEO Armin Papperger said a 2-billion-euro reduction in the 2030 sales target for its vehicle systems business was a strategic decision to build in a "buffer" against potential project delays and supply chain disruptions, rather than a reflection of further lost contracts or weakening demand.

It follows a full-year 2026 guidance cut earlier this month, which sent shares down 16% as the company cited delays to military contracts and ammunition deliveries.

"The opportunity has not changed; the way we plan for it has," Rheinmetall said in a company presentation.

"We are becoming more cautious whilst retaining our agility in a dynamic market," it said, highlighting a "new normal level of conservatism and recalibrated ambition" in the mid-term.

Shares rose 4% in mid-morning trading amid a European defense sector firmly in the green.

Rheinmetall and the wider defense industry have benefitted massively from European governments' efforts to rearm amid the war in Ukraine and uncertain security guarantees from the U.S. As governments ramp up military spending to meet NATO's hiked spending targets, defense companies see swelling order books but also struggle to meet soaring demand.

While shares of Rheinmetall have averaged triple-digit gains between 2022 and 2025, they are down 49% year-to-date. Addressing capital markets specifically, Papperger said he understood the disappointment, but that the loss of the major F126 was "a political thing."

"The government made this decision, and the minister made that decision," he said.

Other headwinds for Rheinmetall in 2026 were an explosion at an ammunition factory in Murcia, Spain, in January, and delays to Germany's Arminius project, a major military procurement program for Boxer armored vehicles, which is now expected to be commissioned next year.

Papperger said delays were due to price negotiations with the government. "I could have the contract immediately if I would reduce the prices, he said.

"We are fighting hard to get this contract, and we are fighting hard, not only to get the contract, but to make it also profitable."

RBC Capital analysts called the lowered mid-term targets a "widely expected reset" with "a general dose of conservatism."

"[The] emphasis on digitalisation offerings and the broadness of the portfolio should also help address sentiment around Rheinmetall being an "old-tech" name in a "new-tech" paradigm," the analysts added.

Some investors are now questioning what role legacy defense contractors like Rheinmetall, BAE Systems, and Leonardo will play in an evolving military context, increasingly focused on technology, autonomous systems, and drones.

Papperger pushed back on the idea that drones will render traditional military equipment obsolete, arguing that militaries must still replace their aging fleets and maintain the ability to transport troops on the battlefield.

"Don't believe what a lot of people [are] telling you in the narrative 'we only need drones.' Forget about that," Papperger said. "The market potential is still there."

Rheinmetall on Friday also warned that Europe faces mounting security threats beyond conventional warfare, pointing to an increase in hybrid attacks ranging from industrial sabotage and cyber espionage to airspace disruption and satellite jamming.

#2783. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2782. Lakers blow 20-point lead in NBA Cup loss to Spurs in biggest test of season

SAN ANTONIO — JJ Redick, and the rest of the Lakers, acknowledged the four-game road trip would be a "massive challenge" because of different tests each team would present.

The biggest test of the trip, and the season so far, came Friday night at Frost Bank Center in the form of Spurs, the reigning Western Conference champions.

And for the first 19 minutes, the Lakers looked like a team ready to ace the test. But the problems they had to answer evolved, and they didn't find enough solutions, leading to the Lakers blowing a 20-point lead and suffering a 99-96 loss to the Spurs to close out NBA Cup group play.

Victor Wembanyama, who scored 25 points and grabbed 16 rebounds, celebrates during the Lakers' 99-96 NBA Cup loss to the Spurs on Nov. 27, 2026, in San Antonio. AP

The Lakers fell to 15-4 with the defeat, with the Spurs improving to 16-4 in a battle between the top two teams in the Western Conference.

And though neither Luka Dončić nor Victor Wembanyama had an efficient offensive performance, the Spurs star big man got the bragging rights, at least for one night, in the battle between two of the top contenders for MVP.

Dončić had 31 points, eight assists and six rebounds, but made just 1 of his 13 3-point attempts (14 for 31 from the field) on a night the Lakers struggled with their outside shooting — especially in the second half.

The Lakers shot 28.6% on 3s (6 of 21) in the final two quarters, with the returning Austin Reaves being the only Laker who found a consistent rhythm from beyond the arc.

Reaves (21 points, eight rebounds on 8-of-15 shooting) made all five of his 3s in the second half, including two late in the fourth to keep the game close after struggling with his ball security (seven of Lakers' 20 turnovers).

Wembanyama led the Spurs with 25 points (10-of-25 shooting) and 16 rebounds, while Stephon Castle had team-highs of 26 points and nine assists.

Luka Dončić, who scored 31 points, dribbles up court during the Lakers' 99-96 road loss to the Spurs. NBAE via Getty Images

The Lakers dominated the Spurs early, with nearly flawless execution of their defensive game plan to play off San Antonio's struggling 3-pointer shooters and shrink the floor against Wembanyama.

They held the Spurs to 38.9% shooting in the opening quarter, including 0 for 6 on 3s after the Spurs missed their final six shots (four 3s) of the first.

The Lakers' defensive intensity carried over into the second, with their stout halfcourt defense helping them take a 45-25 lead midway through the quarter before the Spurs cut their deficit to 54-38 going into halftime.

But the script flipped going into the third for the Lakers, who experienced their own offensive struggles. The Lakers scored 14 points on 6-for-24 shooting (2 for 13 on 3s) in the third, with the Spurs tying the game at 68 apiece when Keldon Johnson made a corner 3 with two seconds left in the third.

The Spurs led by as many as eight points in the fourth, but Reaves and Dončić made big shots late to keep the game close before the Spurs closed them out.

#2780. Stock up, stock down: Bears shock Lions on Thanksgiving

The Chicago Bears have nine wins and have clinched consecutive winning seasons for the first time since 2005 and 2006.

It was the first time this season the Bears had to dip into their 2025 bag for one of their late-game heroic comebacks.

No complaints, of course; getting the W was the most important thing and there are no style points in the NFL.

Since most of this season has been winning from taking an early lead, watching the Bears come from behind felt oddly nostalgic.

I was relieved when the Bears won, so I wouldn't be in an awful mood for the rest of the holiday.

Dillon Thieneman, S – I've already committed the correct spelling of Thieneman to memory, which is a good sign. He was second on the team in tackles. His interception came at the exact right time when the team needed it. He now has three interceptions this season, which leads the Bears.

T.J. Edwards, LB – Anytime you think Edwards' best is behind him, he comes up clutch. He's made some fantastic plays in key moments this year. And perhaps none were bigger than Thursday's tackles of Sam LaPorta on third down and then coming right back and tackling Jahmyr Gibbs. Gibbs has been perhaps the toughest player to tackle this season. Plus, he tipped the pass that Thieneman ended up intercepting.

Rome Odunze, WR – Odunze always seems to come up clutch for the Bears and his QB. His 41-yard touchdown saved the game. Then his spectacular toe-tap catch in the corner of the endzone won the game. The game-winner also came on a fantastic route. Rome has hit another level over the last month and he and Burden are going to be an even bigger part of the pass game without Cole Kmet and Colston Loveland.

Montez Sweat, DE – You can take your pick for the DL here, but I'm singling out Sweat. The Bears had zero sacks. Credit goes to the Lions, for sure. You could see the extra attention they gave to Jeffery Simmons. But Sweat was credited with just two tackles. It was a poor showing for the defensive line that's been greatly improved since Simmons' arrival.

Theo Benedet, LT – Theo has been mostly good this season, but he struggled against the Lions. A lot of the pressure Caleb had came from the left side or up the middle. We all know what Bradbury is, but Benedet takes the spot this week.

Malik Muhammad, CB – It wasn't all bad, but his run support had a couple of bad moments. And while the Bears' defense as a whole had its share of moments against Jahmyr Gibbs, one of the best in football, it was Muhammad who I saw missing opportunities the most against Detroit.

Who is on your up and down lists this week? Who do you think performed great and not-so-great against Detroit?

#2777. Previewing the New York Jets’ opposition: Miami Dolphins

The Miami Dolphins haven't lost to the Jets in Miami since 2014. That was the game when Geno Smith, in his first stint with the Jets, lit them up in a 37-24 win, posting a perfect quarterback rating. The Jets will be favored to end the drought on Sunday against the 1-9 Dolphins, even though Miami beat them 28-25 last month at MetLife Stadium.

Let's break down the Dolphins' roster going position by position:

A lot has changed since these teams met four weeks ago. The most significant change being that quarterback Malik Willis is now on injured reserve.

This means that the current starter is Kyle McCord, who until two weeks ago had never thrown an NFL pass.

McCord didn't disgrace himself in his first game, which was a close loss to the struggling Colts. He ended up with just over 200 yards and a rushing touchdown as he completed 17 of 32 passes.

He didn't fare so well against the Bills on Sunday, though, as he barely reached 100 passing yards, completing only 12 of 32 passes.

The backup to McCord is a familiar face – Brady Cook, who started over the last month of the season for the Jets as an undrafted rookie in 2025.

On the offensive line, the starting group has stayed healthy, with center Aaron Brewer and rookie left guard Kaydn Proctor missing one start each and the other three starters having played all year.

A change has been discussed, though, as right tackle Austin Jackson has given up a team-high five sacks and could be replaced there by Proctor at some point. This move would be made with an eye on the future, as Jackson is out of contract at the end of the year and the first round pick Proctor has had a good year inside, but did play tackle in college.

Left tackle Patrick Paul has had a decent year despite giving up a team-high seven penalties, but right guard Jonah Savaiinaea continues to be a weak link.

The backups include center Andrew Meyer and guard Jarrett Kingston, who have started one game each this year. Sixth round rookie DJ Campbell was recently released to the practice squad after the team claimed Christian Haynes from Seattle.

Chukwuebuka Godrick and Caedan Wallace round out this group.

After De'Von Achane was lost for the year, Ollie Gordon II has settled in as the number one back and it's not out of the question he could make a run at a thousand-yard season as he's already got over 600 rushing yards at 4.4 per carry and racked up over 100 in the last meeting against the Jets.

Backup Jaylen Wright has been staggeringly inefficient in reserve, as he has just 49 yards on 30 carries. The number three, rookie Kaytron Allen, has just two carries for minus-one yard, but did catch a 46-yard pass.

DJ Herman remains on the roster as a blocking back.

Miami's reloaded pass catching group got a boost in the last meeting between these teams as third round rookie Caleb Douglas returned from injury to catch two decisive touchdown passes.

The Dolphins' leading receiver is Malik Washington, but he has just eight catches for 101 yards over the past three games.

Tight end Greg Dulcich has been the other main contributor with 26 catches and his 86 yards on five catches against the Jets easily represents his best output of the season.

Miami will be hoping for more over the rest of the season from their other rookie pass catchers. Chris Bell, a third round pick, has good size at wide receiver but just 13 catches. Fifth rounder Kevin Coleman has just nine receptions, although two were for a touchdown. They also have three rookie tight ends backing up Dulcich – Will Kacmarek was selected in the third round and Seydou Traore in the fifth, while Justin Joly was picked up from Denver, who also drafted him in the fifth round.

Additional depth at wideout comes from Jalen Tolbert but Jared Wayne has been ruled out.

The biggest change on defense since these teams last met is that defensive lineman Zach Sieler, arguably their best remaining veteran player, is now on injured reserve. In addition, the other two starters – Zeek Biggers and Jordan Phillips – were also placed on injured reserve.

This leaves former first round pick Kenneth Grant, who himself had only recently returned from injury, as a starter alongside Keith Cooper Jr., who was poached from the Panthers' practice squad a couple of weeks ago.

Jordan Jefferson was recently signed from the practice squad and was the main backup last week.

Ex-Jet Eric Watts, who as we know can also play on the edge, was also signed to the main roster from the practice squad and was the fourth man in the interior rotation last week.

On the edge, Chop Robinson has recently picked things up after a slow start. He has five sacks to lead the team, including four in the past five games.

Josh Uche has three sacks as the other starter on the edge with Malik Herring as the main backup and undrafted rookie Rodney McGraw behind him. Robert Beal Jr. was recently released.

Second round pick Jacob Rodriguez has continued to impress at linebacker with 93 tackles and two interceptions, although last year's leading tackler, Jordyn Brooks, is right behind him with 92 tackles.

Willie Gay also plays a role in base packages, but Jackson Woodard is primarily just a special teamer.

Following Shaka Heyward's recent release, Miami has just four linebackers on the active roster as fourth round pick Trey Moore remains on injured reserve. However, veteran Ronnie Harrison was himself just activated from injured reserve and his game status for Sunday is questionable.

Miami has recently got some guys back in the secondary, with rookie safety Kyle Louis having returned from injured reserve and Darrell Baker Jr. having been activated from the PUP list.

Both are currently in rotational roles, though, as fifth round rookie Michael Taaffe and Dante Trader as still listed as the starting safeties with Jason Marshall and JuJu Brents outside and first round pick Chris Johnson in the slot.

On Sunday, Trader missed the game due to injury and Zayne Anderson started in his place. Trader should be available to play this weekend but Brents, who has a team-high seven penalties, has been ruled out.

Safety Julius Wood and ex-Jet Jaylin Simpson are the only other Dolphins defensive backs to see action this year, albeit only 30 snaps between them. Cornerback Marcellas Dial is also on the roster.

The Dolphins are currently on their third kicker as Daniel Carlson recently took over from Zane Gonzalez. Carlson is currently being elevated from the practice squad each week. He made three of three field goals in his Miami debut last week.

At punter, the situation is also clouded by the fact that Bradley Pinion isn't 100 percent. The Dolphins signed Jack Browning to the active roster as well as cover and he will punt this weekend as Pinion has been ruled out.

In the return game, Washington has been handling punts and Wright has been doing most of the kickoff returning. Coleman has also been involved.

Simpson is the primary gunner and has been productive in kick coverage, as have Louis and Woodard.
