#2775. Player Grades: Lakers vs. Spurs

There's a belief that games this early in the season, or at perhaps any point in the regular season, don't matter because all 82 games count the same in the win-loss record. And that wouldn't be incorrect.

A win over the Kings and a win over the Spurs hold the same value in the standings at the end of the season. But there are games that have a different feel and intensity to them. They matter more because you need to see how you stack up against the best of the best.

When the Lakers played those games last season, they were pretty routinely outclassed. While they may have been the No. 3 seed in the standings at the end of the season, there was a sizeable gap between them and the Spurs and Thunder.

Friday was a chance to see how much had changed after a busy offseason, and the answer was pretty conclusive. The Lakers are a legit contender.

On the road against a fully available Spurs team, the Lakers not only went toe-to-toe, but dominated portions of the game. Even in the second half, when things were slipping away, LA had big answers and kept making plays.

At the end of the day, they came up short, but it's hard not to come away from this game encouraged about the Lakers moving forward. After a slew of uninspiring performances against bad teams, seeing them land some shots against the cream of the crop in the West is a great sign.

So, let's dive into the loss. As always, grades are based on expectations for each player. A "B" grade represents the average performance for that player.

21 minutes, 2 rebounds, 2 assists, 4 steals, 1 foul, 1 block, 0-4 FG, 0-4 3PT, -1

The Lakers really got minimal production from their wings on Friday and it hurt. Thybulle was actively defensively and helped the Lakers make the run in the fourth quarter with a pair of timely steals.

33 minutes, 10 points, 3 rebounds, 3 fouls, 3-7 FG, 2-4 3PT, 2-3 FT, -13

LaRavia was easily the most effective of the wings, but it was a low bar to clear. He's a lot more valuable when he's able to rebound, but between Kessler grabbing eight offensive rebounds and Victor Wembanyama existing, there weren't many to be had.

34 minutes, 10 points, 13 rebounds, 4 assists, 1 steal, 1 block, 4 fouls, 2 turnovers, 3-5 FG, 4-4 FT, -7

All things considered, Kessler held his own, as best as one could, against Wemby defensively. There were more than a couple of moments where Wemby did some insane things that you just can't stop. The foul call late in the game was soft, to say the least, and maybe he could have grabbed a few more rebounds, but there's only so much you can do against an actual Monstar.

36 minutes, 21 points, 8 rebounds, 1 assist, 7 turnovers, 1 foul, 8-15 FG, 5-9 3PT, -5

Austin's game was the inverse of the team. He really struggled in the first half and then nearly saved the team with his shooting in the fourth quarter. He had 19 points on 7-11 shooting with all five of his threes in the second half.

It wasn't a shock that he was rusty. If anything, it was a surprise he shook it all off in one half.

38 minutes, 31 points, 6 rebounds, 8 assists, 3 steals, 3 fouls, 3 turnovers, 14-31 FG, 1-13 3PT, 2-3 FT, +1

Luka was fantastic in the first half and then drifted in the second half. He never got his 3-point shot going, which really hurt. But he also only shot five in the second half and made a concerted effort to get to the rim when it mattered.

The three free throws, though? No shot he wasn't fouled more than that.

25 minutes, 9 points, 1 assist, 1 steal, 1 block, 1 foul, 3-6 FG, 2-4 3PT, 1-2 FT, +0

Going with Grimes late was the right call. But he's paid a lot of money to step up in these games and he was too much of a passenger. This applies to a couple players, but Grimes has to be better on offense.

13 minutes, 3 points, 4 rebounds, 1 steal, 1 block, 1 turnover, 1-5 FG, 1-3 3PT, +4

The good: Mamu hit his first three in 16 days. Not a typo. He also had a couple of good defensive possessions.

The bad: That was his only made shot and he, again, did not do enough offensively for a player making a lot of money.

16 minutes, 2 rebounds, 3 assists, 2 turnovers, 0-3 FG, 0-2 3PT +5

A really rough night offensively, like so many other role players. He had a couple of corner threes that would have been big shots that did not fall.

14 minutes, 12 points, 4 rebounds, 1 block, 2 turnovers, 5-10 FG, 1-3 3PT, 1-2 FT, +2

This performance was really a best-case scenario from Sexton in a game against a contender. He came in, provided a boost and scored when one of Austin or Luka wasn't on the floor, but he didn't overstay his welcome.

This high-level game is the type of game where it's hard to play Vando with his limitations. It's also the type of game where it'd be great to have a true backup center.

There were a couple of fair complaints about Redick in this one. He might have been able to take another timeout in the third quarter when the Spurs were rolling. He also didn't challenge a couple of plays I felt he should have.

When Kessler was called for a bizarre foul on Austin's 3-pointer, it felt like an easy challenge. I also didn't think Kessler fouled Wemby, but that was because I'm not a fan of the rule and I do think the refs would have upheld it.

But you can't criticize him too much on the challenges because he saved it for a moment that gave the Lakers the ball and helped them nearly win the game.

It was also interesting that neither Cameron Carr or Adou Thiero got minutes. This was a high-level game, so I get it. But he shortened the rotation to basically just nine guys.

Friday's DNPs: Cameron Carr, Adou Thiero, Jaden Hardy, Carson Cooper, Kevon Looney

Friday's inactives: Arthur Kaluma, Chris Mañon, Bronny James

#2773. German software firm SAP is reportedly cutting at least 10,000 jobs in AI push

SAP is preparing a plan to eliminate at least 10,000 positions as the German firm reshapes its software business for the artificial intelligence era, Bloomberg reported on Thursday, citing people familiar with the matter.

"The business environment in which we operate is changing rapidly through AI, and we are continuously reviewing how our organization and workforce need to evolve to remain successful," SAP said in a statement to CNBC.

"As part of this ongoing transformation, roles and functions will naturally change over time. Beyond this, no decisions have been made regarding any further measures. We do not comment on speculation."

The company did not provide any further details on potential job cuts.

The report came as the company flagged a cybersecurity attack, but said it had found "no evidence of unauthorized access to customer business data."

"SAP has identified suspicious activity and promptly implemented the necessary measures to protect its customers and preserve the security of its services," a company spokesperson said.

"Our customers trust us to protect their data, and that remains our highest priority. In a highly dynamic threat environment, we will continue to strengthen our security to provide the secure and resilient environment our customers depend on."

Other software companies have also tied workforce reductions to AI initiatives, though for different reasons. WiseTech Global said in February it planned to cut about 2,000 jobs over two years as it integrated AI into its products and operations.

Atlassian said in March it would cut about 1,600 jobs partly to fund further AI investment, while Cloudflare said in May it would eliminate more than 1,100 roles as it moved toward an AI-first operating model.

In the U.S., employers cited AI in 121,401 job-cut announcements through October 2026, accounting for about 20% of the total, according to a Nov. 5 report from outplacement and executive coaching firm Challenger, Gray & Christmas.

SAP employs more than 110,000 people worldwide. Bloomberg reported that all departments had been asked to identify potential cuts, with product, engineering and sales in particular focus.

The company also underwent a reorganization in 2024, when it eliminated around 10,000 positions through a program that relied mainly on voluntary departures and retraining.

Its headcount later remained relatively flat as it hired for new roles, Bloomberg reported.

In 2023, SAP CEO Christian Klein told CNBC he did not necessarily expect a reduction in the workforce, saying he expected the company to grow and "accelerate" its total revenue.

The reported cuts come as Klein pushes SAP toward an AI-first strategy. This year, SAP completed four acquisitions — Reltio, Dremio, Prior Labs and TechWolf — and made strategic investments in AI-focused companies including Parloa, n8n and Conduct.

The acquisitions are intended to help SAP organize company data, develop AI models for business information and analyze workforce skills.

#2770. How to watch Commanders vs Cardinals: TV, radio, streaming, Week 12 injury report

The Washington Commanders hit the road this week after their big, primetime win over the Cincinnati Bengals. They're traveling out west to take on the hapless Arizona Cardinals, and flew out on Friday to prepare for Sunday afternoon's game.

The Commanders improved to 4-5-1 with their big, primetime win over the Cincinnati Bengals, and then moved into 2nd place in the NFC East after the Cowboys' hilarious Thanksgiving day loss to the Eagles. Washington is still two games behind the New York Giants, who pulled off their own upset over a strong AFC team when they took down the Jacksonville Jaguars in Week 11.

The Cardinals' season is already cooked at 3-8 and they're on their fourth starting QB, former Commanders journeyman Josh Johnson who is on his 14th NFL team. He started the final two games of the season for Washington last season, winning the finale against the Eagles backups. Arizona is also having issues on the defensive side of the ball, firing coordinator Nick Rallis, and replacing him with Teryl Austin.

The Commanders are 7-point road favorites, which is a massive line for a west coast game. The Cardinals are a team Washington should put away early, and keep their feet on the gas. Jayden Daniels should have a massive day in another return to Arizona. His first trip back to The Grand Canyon State as a rookie resulted in a 42-14 victory. Let's run it back!

#2769. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2767. Planes, trains and automobiles: The best ways to beat travel fatigue—so you can come home feeling rested and fulfilled

Five-hour flights. Eleven-hour road trips. Fifteen-hour train rides. All potentially exhausting options for the annual tradition of convening around the dinner table with loved ones on Thanksgiving.

An estimated 81.5 million Americans are projected to travel, or have already traveled, at least 50 miles from their homes between Nov. 24 and Nov. 30, according to the American Automobile Association. And their mode of transportation is shifting: Fewer people are flying or driving this year compared to 2025, and more are traveling by buses, trains and cruises, AAA data shows.

High gas prices and soaring plane ticket costs may factor into the shift. As of Wednesday evening, fuel prices are roughly 17% higher than they were on the same date in 2025, according to AAA data. Meanwhile, "months of elevated jet fuel costs due to the conflict in Iran have contributed to Thanksgiving and Christmas airfares reaching 10-year highs," according to fare-tracking platform Hopper.

The alternate modes of transit may open travelers up to new experiences — and new forms of travel fatigue to keep you from returning home feeling rested and fulfilled. You're probably already familiar with the exhaustion you might get from a day of plane travel, for example. In airplanes, "there can be lower humidity, maybe lower oxygen levels at cabin pressure. Sometimes people report feeling more dehydrated or drained. You're adjusting to that change in altitude," says Alaina Tiani, a Cleveland Clinic behavioral sleep medicine psychologist.

But even waiting in places like train stations or cruise ports can feel draining due to sheer sensory overload, Tiani says. And then, depending on your mode of travel, you may still need to deal with cramped seating and limited ability to get up and walk around or drink some water and eat a snack.

Once you arrive at your destination, managing responsibilities like remembering where you put your hotel key and deciding what to eat and how to structure your day can also take a mental toll, says travel writer Shannon O'Donnell, who's written about managing travel fatigue. "All of that actually requires a lot more thinking and planning for your brain," she says, adding: "People just don't understand how those add up and why a relaxing vacation suddenly has them feeling wrecked."

The consequences can sneak up on you, O'Donnell notes. Travel fatigue can "present as maybe a lack of interest in something that you might usually find fascinating," she says. Instead of looking out the window on a plane ride or playing car games with your kids on a road trip, you might disengage and appear apathetic to the people around you.

You might stand a better chance of a fulfilling, restful holiday if you prepare for your travel fatigue. Here, Tiani and O'Donnell share their advice for how to do it:

#2763. What is ‘texture-maxxing’? The food trend putting crunch on the menu

Texture-maxxing is the latest food trend taking social media by storm as consumers, driven by aesthetics and taste, look for a sensory experience.

British supermarket Waitrose's latest food and drink report highlighted "texture-maxxing" as one of the trends of the year. It describes the phenomenon as a multi-sensory indulgence that is "fuelled by texture-maxxing crunch and intense flavour hits," pushing shoppers to fill their trolleys with crunchy peanut butter, dumplings, and trifles.

"This year, the sound of food went mainstream," Waitrose's Senior Development Chef Will Torrent said in the Wednesday report. "Driven by social media's obsession with audible, multi-sensory cooking, we're creating high-contrast meals that combine shatter-crisp crunch with pillowy chew."

"The emphasis in social media content has flipped from narrative voiceovers to ASMR-inspired audio, where the evocative sounds of food are just as important as the visuals," he added.

Food trends on social media have ranged from the obvious to the absurd. Fibermaxxing and proteinmaxxing have taken off in the past few years. Meanwhile, food and beverages like Dubai chocolate, protein coffee, and ube were some of the big social media hits.

Nestlé's USA 2026 trends found that 75% of Gen Z and 80% of millennials sought out texture in snack cravings with the hashtag "crunchtok," amassing 1.5 billion views.

Waitrose noted that shoppers looking to maximize on texture bought its viral No.1 Alphonso Mango Dessert, which involves "cracking through the crisp shell" to access a "smooth mango mousse and passion fruit curd on a crunchy white chocolate base."

Waitrose's TikTok and Instagram videos of eating the dessert were watched by more than two million people, with a whopping 6,173 hours and 36 minutes of play time on TikTok. Its videos featuring crunchy toast also went viral.

The U.K. grocer's sales of jarred chili crunches, which include crispy rayu oils such as itsu Crispy Chilli Oil, saw a 13-fold jump. Meanwhile, crunchy peanut butter outsold smooth.

Dumplings also had a moment, with sales of chicken gyoza dumplings up 116% this year and searches on waitrose.com for gochujang and peanut butter udon noodles jumped 60%.

"We've seen an explosion in demand as shoppers treat dumplings not just as a quick weeknight bite, but as a total sensory experience," Waitrose's Executive Chef Stephen Parkins-Knight said.

"Driven by the rise of texture-maxxing, people want that ultimate contrast: delicate skins, juicy fillings and crispy skirts, topped with very chilli crunch. With innovation ranging from fusion fillings to rich dipping broths, dumplings have taken a delicious centre stage in 2026," he added.

Texture-maxxing also extended to desserts, as shoppers bought twice as many chocolate trifles compared to 2025, and sales of strawberry trifles increased 160%.

Tiramisu was also popular, with searches for waitrose.com's tiramisu recipe page increasing 155% year-on-year.

#2759. Steelers WR DK Metcalf reportedly fined $26,867 for incidents that led to Week 11 ejection vs. Eagles

Pittsburgh Steelers wide receiver DK Metcalf was reportedly fined twice by the NFL for a pair of incidents during last week's game against the Philadelphia Eagles that led to his ejection.

According to The Ringer's Tom Pelissero, Metcalf was fined a total of $26,867 for grabbing Cooper DeJean's facemask ($11,941) and then attempting to poke the Eagles cornerback in the eye ($14,926).

Metcalf will reportedly not face further discipline and will not appeal the fines.

In the third quarter of Pittsburgh's 19-16 loss, Metcalf was ejected after picking up his second personal foul of the game.

According to Steelers coach Mike McCarthy, a referee told him that Metcalf "poked a defender in the eye." The wide receiver denied doing that while meeting with reporters on Monday.

"I didn't try to poke him in the eye," Metcalf said. "I was just telling him to stop pushing me. That's all I said."

DeJean said after the game that Metcalf "just missed" poking him in the eye.

"Almost, almost," DeJean said. "He just missed. … We were just competing. Things like that happen in football.

"It wasn't anything crazy. Nobody was really saying anything, two guys were going at it. He was trying to block me, I was trying to get him off me. Things like that. Obviously it carried over to whatever that was at the end."

Alleged eye pokes are nothing new for Metcalf. He was fined $12,172 after Week 8 last season for sticking a finger inside the facemask of Green Bay Packers linebacker Quay Walker.

Metcalf recorded only one reception for 2 yards during that loss to the Eagles. He bounced back on Friday during a 27-5 win over the Denver Broncos, catching three balls for 36 yards and a touchdown.

Houston is looking for its second two-win streak of the season and need to capitalize on a wounded bird to kickstart their campaign for the playoffs. #2756. James Small Tells NASCAR Hopefuls to Keep Working at Home—His Crew Has No Kids

James Small has a message for anyone who wants to work in NASCAR: the job does not end when the clock says it should.

Small, who leads the No. 19 team at Joe Gibbs Racing, described the grind of a Cup job on a recent Dinner With Racers episode, as transcribed by EssentiallySports on Nov. 27.

"We're working and racing here. You come in when you want to come, and you come in when, you know, it's done, or you feel it's done. And if it's not done and you're not getting the work done, then you're screwing up, and you've got to keep working at home," Small said.

The Cup schedule runs from February to November with 36 points races and two exhibition events. Crews travel almost every weekend, and the work at the shop continues during the week.

Small also described the kind of thinking he wants on his team.

"Even if you don't know everything about NASCAR, knowing that you have a very systematic way of thinking. You know, you think about the problem. How are you going to solve it? What are the things that affect it? What are the outcomes I'm expecting?

"And how do I, if that's not what happens, how do I then go and think about it or reanalyze and work it out and on again? I feel like that style of thinking is what is successful because it allows you to have a better understanding of when things work and when they don't," Small said.

He also explained how he screens people when he hires.

"I've had to hire people, and there's certain people you can have a conversation with about racing and just general philosophy and their thought process. And for me, I can very quickly figure out, talking about them just randomly, on how they would go about certain things. And if they say the wrong thing there, that's a red flag. I'm very lucky the people on my team right now don't have kids," Small said.

Small knows the grind firsthand. The Melbourne native worked as a race engineer in Australian motorsports in the V8 Supercars championship before moving to the United States.

He was an engineer at Richard Childress Racing from 2014 to 2016, then went to Furniture Row Racing. There he was lead engineer on Erik Jones' No. 77 in 2017 and on Martin Truex Jr.'s No. 78 in 2018.

JGR made him Truex's crew chief in 2020, and the pair worked together through 2024. Small now works with Chase Briscoe, and the pair reached the Championship 4 in their first season together in 2025.

Small also said on the podcast that he thinks Cole Pearn, his predecessor on Truex's pit box, left the sport at the perfect time. Pearn stepped away after 2019 to spend more time with his wife and children in Canada.

#2755. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

The comments came after U.S. envoy Jared Kushner told a podcast Wednesday that Israel "needs to get out of the constant wartime mindset."

Late Thursday, President Donald Trump said on PBS that the U.S. had "victory at hand" in the Iran conflict and reiterated that victory would come "whenever I want it to be, to be honest."

Speaking during a Thanksgiving call with U.S. military personnel, Trump said Washington was still trying to give Iran "every chance to make a deal and save a lot of lives."

But he added that he was not sure whether Tehran would agree.

"Some people question why. Are they crazy? Maybe they are," Trump said.

#2753. Jaguars vs. Titans: Staff roundtable and game picks for Week 12

Welcome to Big Cat Country's staff roundtable!

This week, we're previewing the Jacksonville Jaguars' matchup against the Tennessee Titans in Week 12.

Question 1: What's your top question about the Jaguars this week?

Travis Holmes: Following Sunday's performance from the offensive line, the offensive struggles up front are becoming somewhat concerning. An inability to slow the opposing pass rush or establish the run has been a primary common denominator in Jacksonville's losses to Denver and New York, as well as its near-loss to Philadelphia. The formula for defeat seems simple: keep Jacksonville's offense in low-scoring games by dominating the offensive line and give yourselves a chance at an upset. What's particularly concerning heading into this week is that Jacksonville currently has six offensive linemen dealing with injuries. Left tackle Walker Little and center Robert Hainsey both exited Sunday's game and were limited in Wednesday's practice. Jonah Monheim and Cole Van Lanen were also listed on the injury report, while Anton Harrison remains on season-ending IR. Patrick Mekari, whose 21-day window was opened on Wednesday, was also limited. He could still be a week or more away from returning. With so many moving pieces up front, the health and consistency of this unit could become a major factor on Sunday.

Gus Logue: Can Trevor Lawrence bounce back after his worst performance of the season? Liam Coen said on Friday that he's looking for his quarterback to "get back to the basics, the details of our reads, your feet, rhythm, timing, throwing to the open guy, and just going through your progression … trust the guys to make plays, trust the protection and scheme." The Jaguars should win this matchup because they have better players and better coaching, but in the grand scheme of things, it'd be great to see Lawrence rediscover his mojo.

Henry Zimmer: My big question is with the offensive line. Injuries are mounting up at both tackle positions and that clearly caused issues on Sunday. If the team cannot protect Trevor Lawrence, we all see what can happen. This unit needs to figure it out if the Jags are to have a shot at winning in the playoffs.

Question 2: What's the key to a Jaguars victory this week?

Travis: The key for Jacksonville against Tennessee is getting off to a fast start on both sides of the ball. While the objective was similar in the Jaguars' Week 10 matchup against the Titans, and the final score reflected a 17-point blowout victory, the game was much closer than the scoreboard suggested late in the game. At 8:21 remaining in the fourth quarter, Jacksonville held just a seven-point lead, leaving the offense in a position where it still needed to close the game out due to defensive struggles. Given the Jacksonville defense's recent history of struggling to stop opponents late in games, the Jaguars would be wise to avoid allowing this matchup to become another close affair in the fourth. A strong start, coupled with consistent execution throughout the game, could go a long way toward putting Tennessee away early.

Gus: Shutting down Tennessee's running game. Derrick Henry may not be there anymore, but the Titans did rush for 105 yards on 17 total attempts (including QB runs) two weeks ago in Nashville. Jordan Davis and Jessie Bates III will provide reinforcements for the Jaguars' defense, which should allow Anthony Campanile to stop the run from light boxes the way he did when DaVon Hamilton was healthy. This will be a good test for Davis especially.

Henry: The key to winning is as simple as it normally is when playing far inferior teams: just play normal football and stick to the basics. Tennessee is clearly better than its record suggests but that doesn't mean that they should be in this football game. Attack Cam Ward, run the ball, and get out with a much-needed AFC South victory.

Question 3: Who will be an X-factor for the Jaguars this week?

Travis: My biggest question heading into this matchup revolves around the ground game, but not necessarily involving the running backs. This week's X-factor is Tennessee Titans quarterback Cam Ward, and the use of his legs. The second-year quarterback rushed for 39 yards in the previous meeting, and Jacksonville is coming off a game in which Giants quarterback JJ McCarthy similarly rushed for 33 yards, consistently extending plays and moving the chains with his legs. If Jacksonville can correct this recent deficiency and do a better job rushing with lane integrity, it could significantly limit Tennessee's ability to sustain drives and keep its offense behind the sticks. That adjustment could go a long way toward helping the Jaguars leave EverBank Stadium with their eighth win on Sunday.

Gus: I gotta go with Josh Cameron. The rookie receiver had his best performance of the season in Nashville (3 catches, 67 yards, 1 TD). Now that Parker Washington is week-to-week with a shoulder injury, Cameron is in line for the biggest workload of his young career. Expect him to be utilized out of the slot and in 3-TE personnel packages. I think we could be in for a very productive game for Cameron on Sunday.

Henry: Going back to my big question, I think the X-Factor this week is either Wyatt Milum or Cole Van Lanen. Which one of these guys shows up and plays well if Walker Little cannot go? In many respects, the season hangs on their shoulders if they are the two last lines of defense at the tackle position.

What are your Week 12 predictions, Jaguars fans? Let us know in the comments!

#2752. Under-14s beat Santos in first leg of Paulista final, in Cotia

On Saturday (28), the Under-14s came from behind to beat Santos 2-1 at CFA Laudo Natel in Cotia, São Paulo, in the first leg of the Campeonato Paulista final. Piva and Thieghi scored the goals.

São Paulo's best chance of the first period came in the tenth minute, when Navarrete knocked the ball past his marker, ran around him to collect it and shot over the bar. In the 20th minute, Santos opened the scoring.

In the very first minute, Matheus had a good opportunity to equalize. A through ball put him one-on-one with the goalkeeper, who managed to save with his foot.

In the sixth minute, Moisés crossed into the box and Piva headed toward goal, but the opposing goalkeeper made the save and cleared the danger.

In stoppage time, the goalkeeper palmed the ball away and it fell to Piva, who tried his luck from outside the box. His shot took a deflection and ended up in the back of the net, leveling the score.

In the fifth minute, Ryan Marcelo controlled the ball, beat his marker and passed to Piva, whose shot was blocked by the defense. From the resulting corner, Isaac sent the ball into the box and Thieghi rose highest to head home and put São Paulo ahead.

The return leg will take place next Sunday (6) at 9 a.m. at Vila Belmiro in Santos, São Paulo, with live coverage on the Paulistão and Ulisses TV YouTube channels.

#2751. Utah Jazz vs Denver Nuggets: Player Grades

Home or away, starters or bench, injured Nurkic or healthy Nurkic – the Jazz simply cannot win close games. The game was knotted with around 3 minutes to go, and there was not a neuron in my mind that had any confidence that this game was going to end in any other way that Utah turnovers and Jamal Murray jump shots. Part of the issue is the inability the team has to coordinate when individual players plan on playing well; a good Ace Bailey night seemingly is mutually exclusive with a good night from the bench mob, and don't even think about asking Keyonte George and Darryn Peterson to both knock down their shots during the same game. The Nuggets were able to take advantage because of their talent advantage; Jamal Murray and Nikola Jokic are good enough basketball players that their bad nights are few-and-far-between enough for games to be won in crunch-time. Which Jazzmen signed up for quality-basketball-player duty tonight? The short answer is "not enough", but the longer answer will be given below.

Note: These grades are more-or-less relative to the player. An A grade for Darryn Peterson is different than an A grade for Tony Bradley.

What an uninspiring performance. Ace, praised for his toolsy-ness, is so prone to give absolutely nothing when his shot isn't falling, and his shot is rarely falling. His defense cannot be classified as that of a lockdown or that of an elite event creator, so instead you get a player who doesn't consistently make life harder for anyone on the opposing team. This would be more acceptable if Ace had any practical skills as a creator or shot-maker that one could rely on from a game to game basis. Instead, he is getting closer and closer to be being treated as a legitimate non-shooter, and I can't remember the last time he took a player off the dribble and created any sort of advantage. Ace is not yet good enough at the little things to be an actualized version of the do-everything super role player that he is projected as, and this means that he is not yet good enough to count on to lead to wins.

Loving what I've seen from Filipowski recently. It sounds overly simple, but being big and strong is still a valuable trait in basketball, and not just at the center position – being able to feasibly play a man as large as Kyle Filipowski at power forward is a blessing. The stats weren't awesome today, but he just made things happen; the ball swung quicker, defensive positions ended faster, everything ran smoother. I was particularly impressed with his hands – there were multiple plays where Filipowski maintained possession through traffic or pulled down a contested rebound where I thought that "there is no one else on the Jazz team that can do this at power forward". It'd be awesome if Kyle hit shots, but the healthy version of this player is a winner.

Not sure what to do with Keyonte's game today. Dynamic as a jump shooter, and that's valuable! But that dynamicity hid a pretty flawed process, especially evident at the end of the game. When the going gets tough, George simply isn't a strong enough decision maker to fuel effective late-game offenses as the point guard. The body language at the end of the games reflects this deeper, clutch time issue – Keyonte struggles to take a figurative punch from the other team without reeling over and effectively admitting defeat. The shots went in tonight, which sets a high floor on his grade, but they won't always go in; when they don't the version of Keyonte that we got today will struggle to be a part of winning teams.

Not much to say about Green. He didn't make any meaningful impact on Murray's offensive process (and I don't really care what he does to any of the other Nugget's players; who cares if Cameron Johnson struggles to "get his" off of the dribble?), and he didn't make shots. I appreciate the energy that he brings, but that energy isn't too valuable by itself. Rough night.

Maybe it's problematic that the Jazz have so many simple players. There isn't a lot of potential for explosive performances or super cohesive offenses when a multitude of Utah's players (Green, Hayes, Okogie) play games that can be explained in a sentence – each have a role that's important, but a team can only hold so many simplistic players before the team becomes simplistic. Hayes did well today, but in exactly the ways you'd expect – he rolled to the rim, finished, and tried his best on defense. That's good! but it isn't too straining to the defense, or difficult to scheme against. You know what Hayes does, and the Nuggets do too. Maybe I'm reaching for ways to explain this teams misfortunes, but simplicity does seem to me to be part of it.

I've remembered recently why I enjoyed the Svi experience so much last season – players with size who can hit shots off movement and try on defense are valuable. Today, he did just that; he provided offensive jolts when the Jazz needed it, and was difficult to be picked on defensively. An efficient Svi is a desirable Svi, so as much as I appreciate what he's providing to the team at the moment, I wouldn't be surprised if a contender comes a-calling as we get closer to December 15th.

It took no time for Nurkic to be the exact same player that he was last season. He immediately made some high-leverage passes; his 11 assists are counted the same as any other player's 11 assists, but these felt particularly valuable because of how advantageous of situations he placed his teammates in – it was open threes and dunks for those cutting around Nurkic tonight, and you could tell they appreciated it. However, some of the negatives of 2025-26 Nurk reared their ugly head as well, most pressingly how rarely and poorly his feet move. That wasn't too troublesome all the time against the Nuggets, because his mass seemingly threw Jokic off of his game, but when he was moved onto the perimeter or had to move in transition, the surprisingly effective early-season Jazz defense suffered. It's good to have Nurkic back, nevertheless, but it'll be even better once the injured JJJ returns and conceals some of his more eye-opening flaws.

Another simple player, so another simple write-up. His grade is slightly higher than Green because he missed less, but he played a very similar game. His defense wasn't really felt and he didn't have the opportunity to take or make many jump shots. On games like this, there isn't much Okogie can do to provide positive value.

Wow, what beautiful synergy Darryn Peterson already has with Jusuf Nurkic, made only more beautiful because of the sheer predictability of it all. This was clearly how Darryn was meant to be employed, and it's lovely to finally see it be actualized. I am not yet worried about Darryn's jump shooting, but I can't pretend that he isn't currently hurting the team with his missed shots. However, what really dropped his grade tonight were some of the defensive lapses. I worry that, with the introduction of the plodding Nurkic into the starting lineup, the defensive missteps from all three of the youngsters will be so much obvious – any missed backdoor cut will lead to a dunk because Nurkic isn't as quick as Hayes when they recover to the rim. There were a couple plays like that from Peterson tonight, and I hope he spends a bit more of his energy cleaning up these rough patches defensively now that the starters have become a bit more offensively slanted.

I just can't quit Brice. When the bench minutes work, it's because Brice worked. Today, the Jazz won the Sensabaugh minutes. Surely, this is partially because he happened to not play too often against Murray and Jokic, but the tape also reflected how positive a player can be when they combine a scoring mentality with general playmaking competency. Say what you will about Brice, but you cannot call him one-note; he can stroke it from downtown, he's got the footwork to make it work from anywhere in the midrange, and (most nights) he has no aversion to a solid connective pass. The Jazz simply do not have many players who can do what Brice can do, so when Brice is having a good night, he provides such a unique element to the offense. I believe Brice can be the engine of a good bench offense, and he showed all the tools that have constructed that belief tonight.

Who stood out for you? Which grades would you change? Sound off below!

#2749. It’s not just you: Durable goods are getting less reliable, from sputtering cars to glitching smart TVs

"They just don't make things like they used to." The phrase, formerly exclusive to grandparents, is now used by consumers of all ages.

Across the United States, durable goods like cars, appliances and electronics are becoming less reliable. In the automobile industry, owners of 3-year-old models reported an average of 204 problems per 100 vehicles as of Feb. 12, the highest level since 2022, according to a report from consumer intelligence company J.D. Power. Reliability is a growing concern for dishwashers and front-load washers too, respectively experiencing an average of 92 and 91 problems per 100 units as of Oct. 29, according to a similar J.D. Power report.

Consumers have noticed: Shoppers dealing with high inflation rates and added tariffs on imported goods are unsatisfied with all kinds of products, from cars and tech devices to furniture and kitchen gear. Just over three-quarters of Americans say they believe companies often sell lower-quality products and services at higher prices, according to an Axios and Harris Poll survey published in May 2025.

To be sure, reliability and durability aren't the only ways to define a product's quality. Aesthetics and performance matter to consumers, too. Your television today may not last as long as the one you grew up with, but it's probably larger and thinner with better picture definition — and it likely costs much less, when adjusted for inflation, says Justin Sirotin, an industrial design professor at the Rhode Island School of Design. Indeed, the average price of a television was roughly $622 in 1996 adjusted for inflation, whereas a 55-inch LCD model now costs $232 on average.

But because they're penny-pinched, consumers are becoming more focused on finding high-value products — ones that perform consistently and reliably — than on price, according to a 2025 Deloitte report.

Unfortunately for shoppers, some businesses are also economically squeezed, and may manufacture products with cheaper, less durable parts to keep their profits growing, Sirotin says. Plus, we've all gotten used to sleek products with impressive features at affordable prices, and when businesses have to deliver on those aspects to grow demand, longevity can drop down the list of priorities. Manufacturers may prioritize quality on the external parts of a product — like the picture quality on a television — and get their cost savings with cheaper internal components that you won't initially notice, says Keyana Sapp, an investigative journalist and creator of the Worse on Purpose newsletter.

In some cases, a product's durability may even be part of a "planned obsolescence," wherein businesses intentionally give goods a certain lifespan so customers need to buy replacements within a predictable timeframe. Planned obsolescence isn't necessarily rampant, and it's hard to prove that any individual company purposefully designs products to fail, Sapp says.

But historically speaking, it's not entirely a conspiracy theory. In 1924, major light bulb manufacturers formed an international coalition and agreed to lower and standardize the lifespan of incandescent light bulbs to 1,000 hours. The cartel broke up during World War II.

You can, of course, try extending the longevity of products you've already bought by paying to repair them, rather than getting a brand-new replacement. But as devices like vacuum cleaners and headphones evolve technologically, they require increasingly specialized tools and labor, says Shahram Mokhtari, lead teardown technician at online repair community and e-commerce site iFixit. Specialized screws, glued components and limited access to repair information can make some products difficult or impossible to fix, the Federal Trade Commission reported in May 2021.

Europe has right-to-repair regulations that require manufacturers of certain products to provide access to replacement parts, tools and information. Only nine U.S. states have similar legislation for consumer electronics.

If you want to know how long a product might last before you buy it, look up its reviews or do some research to understand if it's complicated to fix, Mokhtari recommends. Or, if you have the budget, you can prioritize higher-priced products that might bring both initial quality and long-term longevity. This may require intentional long-term planning for some consumers — even as online shopping has made impulse buying easier, Sirotin notes.

"It takes a lot of intentionality by the consumer to say, 'OK, well, is this chair really a chair that I want to have for 25 years? Or is this the chair I'm going to buy and sit in for six months, and then I'm just going to get another chair?'" says Sirotin.

#2745. 'The Traitors' ended with a $246,300 prize—here's who won and what they told us about spending it

When Shane Beatty, a contestant on NBC's "The Traitors: New Blood," deceived his fellow finalists, he walked away with the entire $246,300 prize. But the emotional toll of winning caught him by surprise.

"The second I had to reveal that I was a Traitor, everything hit me at once," Beatty, a 32-year-old ironworker from Staten Island, New York, tells CNBC Make It. "I had built genuine relationships with these people, and knowing that some of them were blindsided by my decision was difficult."

Now that he's won, Beatty has a plan for his money, starting with setting aside a sizable share for Uncle Sam. As for the rest, he's more interested in financial security than the trappings of newfound wealth.

After winning, Beatty doesn't expect his day-to-day life to change much.

"I'm still going to get up, go to work, and continue doing what I've been doing as a Local 580 ironworker," he says. "I love my career, and winning this money doesn't change that."

Beatty plans to pay for a wedding he and his fiancée previously struggled to afford, help his mother pay down debt and clear his own debts before saving the rest.

"What it does give me is an opportunity to take some financial pressure off, be a little more comfortable and put myself in a better position for the future," he says.

But before spending his winnings, Beatty is accounting for what could be his single biggest expense — taxes.

"First thing I'm gonna do with the money is put at least $100,000 in a separate bank account just so I can have it ready for Uncle Sam," he told Entertainment Weekly last week.

Setting aside $100,000 is a reasonable move given Beatty's potential tax liability, according to tax experts.

"Taxes are often an afterthought when winning big on a gameshow," says Riley Adams, a certified public accountant and founder of Sequoia Ridge CPAs. "So, it's definitely smart on his part to get ahead with his tax planning."

Game show winnings are generally taxed as ordinary income, meaning the prize money is added to a winner's other taxable income for the year. That can push a winner into higher tax brackets.

In addition to federal income taxes, Beatty, who lives in New Jersey, will have to account for state income taxes.

While Beatty did not disclose his income to CNBC Make It, ironworkers in the New York City metropolitan area typically earn around $100,000 a year, according to 2025 Bureau of Labor Statistics data.

Assuming Beatty lives in New Jersey, earns $100,000 from his job in New York in 2026, receives his winnings this year, files as a single taxpayer with no dependents and takes the standard deduction, here's how much of his prize money he could keep, according to Adams' calculations:

Under this scenario, Beatty's winnings would push him from the 22% federal income tax bracket into the 35% bracket. Factoring in state income taxes, an estimated 35% of his prize would go to taxes, according to Adams' calculations. Setting aside $100,000 would give Beatty a comfortable cushion under this scenario. His actual tax bill, however, will depend on his income, deductions and other financial circumstances.

Jim Guarino, a certified public accountant and managing director at Baker Newman Noyes in Woburn, Massachusetts, arrived at a similar estimate.

"Folks tend to get excited by the 'gross amount' of their total winnings," but Beatty is "extremely tax-aware" in recognizing how much less he'll actually get to keep after taxes, he says.

For Beatty, the money won't radically change his life, but it will give him greater financial security.

"Having a savings cushion and knowing I don't have to worry about living paycheck to paycheck or falling behind on bills means so much to me," he says. "I want to be responsible with this opportunity and make sure it gives me greater financial peace of mind."

#2742. The Tech Download: Will Jensen Huang’s return be the spark the shaky AI market needs?

The recent Nvidia saga has had more twists and turns than an Agatha Christie novel.

It all started with Nvidia co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang's admission that he had been unfaithful to his wife. Days later, amid an independent board investigation and broader scrutiny over national-security concerns, he resigned. Less than two weeks later, Huang was back at the helm.

During all of that, Nvidia stock lost over $1 trillion and then regained just under $1 trillion.

The leadership drama has played out at a time when the artificial intelligence market is looking shakier than it has in some time.

Neoclouds have seen stocks plummet in recent months as investors question the sustainability of the AI boom. Chip stocks like Arm and AMD have both fallen in the past month.

Huang's surprise return saw Nvidia stock surge 31%, with other AI stocks also gaining. But the sheen was off by Tuesday's close, with Nvidia and a host of chip stocks falling.

The sharp reversal raises questions for investors: did Huang's return restore confidence in Nvidia, or did it spark a short-lived relief rally as the market faces broader questions about returns on AI investment?

Judging by the first few days of his return, it's a hard one to call.

Volatility in the tech sector looked set to continue on Friday, as tech stocks rebounded again.

"Wall Street pared its Monday advance as sobriety returned to the market and a focus returned to fundamentals," wrote Kyle Rodda, analyst at Capital.com, in a note at market close on Tuesday.

"While Jensen Huang's reinstatement as Nvidia CEO is indubitably a boon for the markets and the overall AI trade, it does not assuage every risk and concern about an industry confronting a potential peak in its investment boom and moderating earnings growth."

The early week rally was "largely driven" by Huang's return, said Violeta Todorova, senior research analyst at Leverage Shares.

"Fresh tariff threats from the Trump administration have added to market uncertainty," she added. "The short-term rally has reversed course, and Nvidia's share price is likely to trade sideways between $125 and $170 in the near term."

Nvidia stock closed at $149.30 on Wednesday, giving it a $3.6 trillion market cap — a figure around $2 trillion lighter than it was at its peak in October.

"We expect a selective recovery across the AI ecosystem, and NVDA to trade sideways and stay volatile below its October peak at least until the Dec 1–3 Congressional appearances," Egor Tolmachev, senior analyst at Freedom Capital Markets, said in a note on Wednesday.

Other key AI stocks have also been volatile. Anthropic's first week as a public company has been mixed. AMD has fallen 25% in the past five days, and memory chip maker Micron has dropped 18%. Both spiked on Monday before paring gains.

The Huang evangelists might've been hoping for a speedier return to AI boom times.

But while the Nvidia CEO's return has removed one source of uncertainty around the chip giant, the volatility among AI stocks suggests investors are still trading on questions much bigger than who occupies the chief executive's office.

Tech German software major SAP is reportedly weighing cutting at least 10,000 jobs in AI push.

U.S. adds Megaspeed to trade blacklist over export control concerns amid Nvidia controversy.

Robots could be doing household chores in just 1-2 years, a Chinese EV firm says.

SpaceX, Musk pledge to donate a share each to active Trump Accounts.

Jensen Huang tells staff that Nvidia doesn't need a succession plan, according to people inside the room.

Jensen Huang is back as the CEO of Nvidia, and is now tasked with selling the company's AI vision while also defending himself in Washington, my colleague Kif Leswing wrote on Tuesday.

#2741. Survey results: Fans confident Seahawks will best 49ers

The Seattle Seahawks can move themselves into the NFC playoff picture by beating their divisional rival, the San Francisco 49ers, on Sunday. If you ask a Seahawks fan, they'll probably say that Seattle will win that game, at least according to our Seahawks Reacts survey results.

When asked if the Seahawks would beat the 49ers in the pivotal matchup, 50% of fans said the 6-4 team would upset the current No. 1 seed in the NFC. It should be noted that this survey was taken before the 49ers ruled out starting quarterback Brock Purdy, so in all likelihood this percentage of fans has only grown since Friday afternoon's announcement.

Seahawks fans' confidence in an upset isn't entirely unfounded, as Seattle has fared much better against the Bay Area team since they hired Mike Macdonald, especially in Santa Clara. Macdonald's defense has proved that it can stop a Kyle Shanahan-led offense before, including in Week 18 of the 2025 season, where the Seahawks held the 49ers to just 3 points.

The Seahawks have won their last three games at Levi's Stadium, including Super Bowl 60, giving them some confidence heading on the road. Seattle is also coming off a bye week, while San Francisco is coming back from a trip to Mexico City against a physical Minnesota Vikings team, which might contribute to the confidence of fans.

Notably, Seahawks fans aren't entirely behind their team, with 36% predicting a 49ers' victory and 17% projecting it to be by two scores. The 49ers haven't beaten the Seahawks by two scores since Macdonald's first season as head coach on Oct. 10, 2024, when they won 36-24 at Lumen Field.

One thing is clear from the survey results, though: fans expect this game to be close and once again decided by one score as their first meeting was.

In addition to the 49ers question, fans were once again polled on whether they thought the franchise was moving in the right direction.

This result is a solid mark for a 6-4 team on the playoff bubble heading into December, but is also a puzzling one with context. Last week, 74% of Seahawks fans were confident the team was trending positively, and the team didn't play a game on Sunday, meaning something over the bye week has fans a little more shook than they were a week ago.

Perhaps that has something to do with the 49ers' thrashing of the Vikings on Sunday night, which positioned them as midseason Super Bowl favorites. It could also be the continued emergence of the NFC North, which has had a hold of three of the seven playoff spots for most of the year.

With the Seahawks sitting idle, maybe the realization that the defending champs will need a near-perfect second half to make the playoffs became a little more real.

I'd lean toward the last of the three reasons, but there's certainly plenty of theories to concoct as to why confidence has dropped in the organization without the team playing a game. Either way, Sunday's game will surely change that number in a massive way as the Seahawks take their playoff hopes to Santa Clara to face Mac Jones' 49ers.

Let us know what you think of the results and drop your theories of why fans don't trust the organization as much after the bye week in the comments below!

#2740. Prediction markets outlook for stocks in 2026 dims amid AI sell-off

As stocks continue their tech-driven sell-off, prediction markets traders are seeing a bleaker outlook for the remainder of the year.

On platform Kalshi, speculators place just 6% odds that the S&P 500 will cross 8,000 in 2026. On Oct. 13, those odds were as high as 72%.

Contracts ask traders if the S&P 500 will go above various levels in 2026, and they're resolved using data from S&P Dow Jones Indices.

On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite turned negative for the year as investors continued to dump tech stocks, primarily those with exposure to the artificial intelligence build-out. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and the S&P 500 fell into correction territory, too.

The S&P 500 has slashed its year-to-date gain to just over 2%.

The Stock Trader's Almanac in a Thursday note broadly agreed that the hopes for a December rally are fading, with the caveat that a "Santa rally" could still appear at the end of the year.

"The market is under pressure and more downside can't be ruled out," the note said.

The Stock Trader's Almanac sees 2026 following the year-end pattern of 2018, when the Federal Reserve was in a tightening cycle in a midterm year. The Fed is widely expected to hike rates at its December meeting.

In that scenario, the Stock Trader's Almanac thinks more churn is to come. "The low is more likely in mid- to late December than this week."

#2739. Spain’s election could pull Madrid closer to Trump’s Washington

Spanish voters head to the polls on Sunday for a snap election that could bring down socialist Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez, who has repeatedly clashed with U.S. President Donald Trump.

It could also put the far right in government for the first time in the country's modern democratic era.

The center-right Popular Party is the frontrunner, and it could win a majority with far-right Vox, according to El País's final polling averages. A government led by the PP could shift Spain's approach to Washington, Beijing and NATO.

It's the first test of whether Europe's mainstream conservatives are willing to bring the far right into government, ahead of elections in France and Poland next year.

Despite Spain's economy growing 2.7% year over year in the third quarter, more than twice the euro zone's 1.3%, the campaign has been dominated by housing and cost-of-living crises.

Inflation soared to 4.9% in the year to October, above a euro zone average of 4%. November's flash estimate published Friday showed Spanish inflation cooling to 4.1%, still well above the 2.3% reading at the start of the year. In an October YouGov survey, 53% of respondents named housing one of the country's two most important issues.

Home purchase prices nearly doubled in the decade to the second quarter of 2026, according to Eurostat. In 2025, house prices rose nearly three times as fast as household incomes, the European Commission said.

Nationwide protests began after an 87-year-old woman died two weeks after being evicted from her Madrid apartment in September.

Sánchez called the snap election after lawmakers rejected two government decrees designed to broaden housing protections, including limiting rent increases and blocking certain evictions and automatic lease renewals. Congress later ratified a revised version of one decree, limiting rent increases and evictions in some circumstances.

Sánchez has made a name for himself internationally after clashing with Trump over NATO spending, the war in Gaza and Spain's refusal to let U.S. forces use its bases for operations against Iran. Trump even threatened to cut off "all trade" with Spain during the dispute over the bases.

At a Nov. 21 rally in Tenerife, Sánchez asked: "What good has President Trump brought to the United States and, by extension, to the world?"

He has also told voters that the PP and Vox would roll back government cost-of-living assistance. Days before the election, Sánchez's government approved 1.21 billion euros ($1.33 billion) to extend public transport discounts through 2027 and approved a general pay increase of up to 4.5% for 2027.

But in a final pre-election survey for El Español on Nov. 23, 66% of respondents gave Sánchez a failing grade and 29.5% a passing one. His government has faced scrutiny over its handling of the Ceuta migrant crisis, two deadly train collisions this year and corruption cases involving people close to the prime minister.

PP leader Alberto Núñez Feijóo, the current favorite to replace Sánchez, has made alleged government corruption and inflation central to his campaign. At a Nov. 22 rally in Murcia, he said the government wanted voters to forget "that inflation is eating into your pay check, that you can't afford a home, that you're paying more than ever for everything."

The PP led the final El País polling average, published Nov. 24, with 31.4%, followed by Sánchez's Socialists at 29.1% and Vox at 17.9%. Several seat projections put the PP and Vox above the 176 seats needed for a majority in the 350-seat lower house. If Vox joins a government, it would be the first far-right party to do so since Spain returned to democracy after Francisco Franco, the dictator who ruled for 40 years, died in 1975.

The PP wants to repeal the 2023 housing law and scrap rent caps in designated high-pressure areas. It proposes speeding up construction permits and lowering taxes on first homes for buyers under 40. Vox also wants to repeal the housing law and would prioritize Spanish citizens for subsidized housing and public services.

Sánchez's Socialists want to extend renter protections and make at least a quarter of new homes public or permanently price-restricted. It would use proceeds from a bank levy to support housing, which it says will raise a further $1.5 billion. The PP has pledged to scrap this.

The PP also favors easing climate rules, extending the life of nuclear plants and replacing a decree that sets tight new renewable-power requirements on data centers with large new grid connections.

A PP-Vox coalition would have its tensions, with immigration likely to be a major pressure point as Vox has an increasingly hard line on deportation and the militarization of some borders. The parties clashed in regional governments over the distribution of unaccompanied migrant children, leading Vox to quit several coalitions in 2024.

Immigration has since gained more prominence amid the government's handling of reports that more than 70,000 people crossed from Morocco into the Spanish enclave of Ceuta on July 30 and 31.

"Immigration has played a significant role in supporting Spain's recent growth, a strong role for Vox could complicate the government's ability to sustain that," said Jess Middleton, senior Europe analyst at risk intelligence company Verisk Maplecroft.

The parties also differ on defense commitments: both support more investment, but neither has committed to NATO's target of spending 5% of GDP on defense. The PP pledges closer NATO cooperation, while Vox puts greater emphasis on border security.

The PP favors closer integration with the European Union, while Vox wants more independence from it. The success or failure of a PP-Vox partnership will also have wider lessons for Europe "where long-standing firewalls against far-right parties are coming under strain," added Middleton, pointing to the rise of Germany's AfD and the prospect of Marine Le Pen potentially winning next year's election in France.

A shift to the right could improve relations between the U.S. and Spain. Sánchez used his tenure to strengthen ties with Beijing, the country's largest goods trading partner outside the EU.

The U.S. remained Spain's largest source of foreign direct investment. Flows more than doubled to 11.6 billion euros in the 12 months through June 2026, from 5.1 billion euros in the previous 12 months, according to Spain's Economy Ministry.

Carla Hobbs, head of the European Council on Foreign Relations' Madrid office, expects a PP-led government to seek closer ties with Washington and bring its China policy more in line with the EU's. Vox could complicate that. The first test for Washington would be whether a new government changes Spain's position on defense spending or the use of its bases.

#2737. NBA Cup bracket watch: Who's in, who's out after a decisive Friday slate

The 2026 NBA Cup knockout round bracket is set after an action-packed final day of pool play on Friday, Nov. 27. The final eight teams left standing are the Philadelphia 76ers, Boston Celtics, Memphis Grizzlies, Atlanta Hawks, Dallas Mavericks, Detroit Pistons, San Antonio Spurs and Portland Trail Blazers.

That means a new champion will be crowned for the fourth consecutive year since the tournament's inception in 2023 as the New York Knicks managed to win just one game this time around in East Group B.

The Spurs were the final team to clinch their spot on Friday, closing out the group stage with a thrilling 20-point comeback over the Los Angeles Lakers led by Stephon Castle (26 points, 9 assists, 4 rebounds) and Victor Wembanyama (25 points, 16 rebounds, 4 steals, 2 blocks). They'll be the wild card in the West after the Portland Trail Blazers won Group C outright with their 116-94 win over the Golden State Warriors. Both teams finished 3-1, but the Blazers get the tiebreaker due to their head-to-head win over San Antonio.

The Grizzlies secured their spot with a 114-109 win over the Minnesota Timberwolves, eliminating the Anthony Edwards-less Wolves in the process and advancing into the knockout stage for the first time in franchise history.

Beyond the quest for bragging rights, there are substantial financial incentives at stake in the NBA Cup tournament. Players advancing to the eight-team knockout round earn approximately $50,000 each, with bonus amounts increasing at each subsequent stage. Members of the winning team can expect a payout exceeding $500,000 per player, while the runners-up will receive over $200,000 apiece.

Here's an updated look at the bracket and schedule ahead of the NBA Cup quarterfinals:

The Philadelphia 76ers, Boston Celtics, Memphis Grizzlies, Atlanta Hawks, Dallas Mavericks, Detroit Pistons, San Antonio Spurs and Portland Trail Blazers have advanced.

Below are the list of teams that have been eliminated:

Portland Trail Blazers (3-1) (+34) CLINCHED

Golden State Warriors (2-2) (-24) ELIMINATED

Los Angeles Lakers (2-2) (-12) ELIMINATED

Cleveland Cavaliers (2-2) (+2) ELIMINATED
