#2736. 2027 NFL Draft prospects for the Giants in Rivalry Weekend – Saturday

Good morning New York Giants fans! Happy Saturday and welcome back to Rivalry Week 2026!

We already talked about some of the best players from Friday's slate of games, so now we're on to Saturday. We have some of the biggest games and the best rivalries in sports today. And one of the best parts of the "Transfer Portal Era" is that we're getting more "good against good" matchups than ever before.

As always, we're going to use college football and the NFL Draft as a lens through which to look at the Giants. So we'll be sticking to prospects that fill the Giants' needs as they're currently perceived.

We don't talk about 'Cuse all that often, but Peal is an intriguing player. He was a four-star recruit who spent the first two years of his career at Georgia before transferring to Syracuse. Peal has NFL traits at 6-foot-1, 200 pounds with good speed and play strength. He has solid fluidity, as well as good instincts in zone coverage and run defense. Peal has the traits to be a good press-man corner as well, but he hasn't played up to his potential yet. He could be a great developmental prospect for the Giants if he slips to them on Day 3.

The Giants should be watching the entire Irish secondary. They (probably) won't have a chance at CB Leonard Moore, and their secondary is in a great place right now. However, it's a unit with high attrition and subpackages require a lot of DBs.

That said, I want to take a look at a player who could be matched against Peal.

Faison has exploded onto the national scene as a senior and has 56 receptions for 724 yards and 7 touchdowns. He doesn't have great size at 5-foot-11, 185 pounds, with great speed, quickness, and ball skills. Faison primarily plays out wide but could play inside as well. He tracks the ball extremely well, has the ups to high-point the ball, and also has upside as a punt returner.

We've talked about both of the Miami defensive tackles before, and they're both worth watching this game. Moten Sr. is slightly smaller (listed at 6-foot-3, 300 pounds), however he plays with consistent pad level and power as well as the quickness to win as a pass rusher. He can play on the nose despite his weight, as well as play at a 3 or 4i technique. Moten will likely land as a Day 2 prospect, though he could be a fringe first rounder for the right team after a strong draft process.

Jones originally enrolled at Georgia in 2024, but didn't appear all that often before transferring to BC for the 2026 season. Jones has eligibility remaining, so it might behoove him to return to school and continue to develop. As things stand now, he will likely find his way onto the field as a special teams player while he continues to develop as a linebacker. Teams will likely view the 6-foot-2, 240-pound Jones as more of a WILL with pass rush upside than as a full-time edge defender.

The Giants definitely need a more explosive element out of their backfield, and Brown could certainly supply that. He's a small back at 5-foot-9, 190 pounds, but boasts elite quickness, agility, and explosiveness. Brown has the ability to cut on a dime, stick his foot in the ground, and be at top speed almost instantly. He also has great vision to identify holes as well as anticipate defenders. He's averaged 7.5 yards per carry over his three years at Louisville, including an incredible 8.8 yards per carry last year.

Another running back to watch in this game, Baxter is much more of power back than Brown at 6-foot-1, 230 pounds. He started at Texas before transferring to Kentucky after injuries derailed the start of his college career. Baxter has the potential to be an every-down back, with untapped upside as a pass catcher as well as his ability as a rusher. He might not have blazing speed, but he does hit top speed quickly, which can make him tough to bring down thanks to his size and contact balance.

Baxter's size and versatility could make him appealing to a Giants team looking for a "bell cow" back.

Washington is the other corner at Clemson across from Ashton Hampton, who could well be a Top 10 pick. Washington is an interesting player with rare speed and ball skills with 5 interceptions and 7 passes defensed this year. He started his career at Penn State before transferring to Clemson after James Franklin was fired. Like most former Penn State players, Washington could still use development in the finer points of playing his position, but he has rare ball skills and a team will want to find ways to get him on the field.

Stewart's draft stock has been an absolute rollercoaster this year. He started out as a Top 5 player, before his draft stock began to drop due to questions about his lack of production. It then fell off a cliff when he was suspended by both South Carolina and the NCAA for his actions after completely losing his temper. Stewart's draft stock could be incredibly volatile, so he could go in the Top 10, or he could fall to the bottom of the first round.

After returning, Stewart has played much more under his control and has played some of the best football of his career. He's long, fluid, fast, and explosive, with the ability to simply take over games when he's on his game. The Giants' incredible depth at edge defender has been one of the real strengths of their team this year, and they could look to reinforce it if Kayvon Thibodeaux leaves via free agency.

Hall is going to be an intriguing prospect in a deep defensive tackle class. He's a big, powerful nose tackle could appeal to the Giants at 6-foot-4, 320 pounds and has significant upside as a run defender who could also contribute to the pass rush. The intrigue comes from the fact that despite being a senior who has been playing real snaps since his freshman year, he only played 14 total games in 2024 and 2025 due to injury. He dealt with stress fractures in 2024 and a knee injury in 2025, but is back to full strength in 2026 and has emerged as a stalwart in the middle of a great Georgia defense this year.

Haynes is a well-travelled senior who started his career at Alabama in 2023, then transferred to Michigan for the 2025 season, and is now at Georgia Tech. He has solid size at 5-foot-10, 210 pounds and has been a productive runner at each of his stops. He has a career 6.1 yards per carry average, averaging 5.9 yards per carry at Alabama, 7.1 YPC at Michigan, and 5.4 YPC at Georgia Tech. It's notable that he's produced at three different schools, each with different offensive lines, blocking schemes, and offensive schemes. Haynes is a fluid runner who has the feet to pick his way through the trash and is very difficult for defenses to bring down cleanly. He's averaging 4.2 yards after contact this year after averaging 3.5 yards after contact last year.

We highlighted Hernandez here last week, and we're going to do so again this week. Wake Forest has stormed onto the national scene and Hernandez is one of the primary focuses of their offense. He has only average size at 6-foot, 190 pounds, but he has been consistently productive for the Demon Deacons and is currently 6th in the country in receiving yardage. Every year there's a with unassuming traits who has the potential to take the NFL by storm if he gets the right opportunity. This year that might be Hernandez.

Tight end isn't a dire need for the Giants, but considering how often they use multiple TE sets, they could look to to another late round prospect to add competition. Hasley has solid size at 6-foot-5, 250 pounds, and is a capable receiver as well as blocker. He's a high-effort player who's a reliable safety blanket for his quarterback as well as a solid blocking tight end. Hasley is a former linebacker and has only been playing tight end full time since 2023, so he still has plenty of upside with development.

I've already talked about Bruins' running back Wayne Knight and believe that he's a player everyone should be watching, so I want to take this opportunity to shine a light on the UCLA defense.

UCLA comes into this game as one of the biggest surprises in college football, boasting a 10-1 record despite losing their starting QB (Nico Iamaleava) in his fifth start. Their defense has been a big reason for their success, and their ability to take the ball away in particular. Safety Scooter Jackson leads the Bruins' defense — and the Big10 — with 5 interceptions as well as a forced fumble, and has also returned one of those picks for a touchdown. He's also 6th in the Big10 with 9 passes defensed on the season as well. Jevon Holland is one of the best safeties in the NFL this year, and they've gotten very good play from Ar'Darius Washington and Jason Pinnock as well. That said, the Giants could be interested in having more young talent in the pipeline to keep the level of play high.

USC left tackle Elijah Paige and QB Jaden Maiava will get most of the attention in this game. However, the Giants are pretty well set at quarterback, and it's probably too early to draft a developmental tackle highly with both Andrew Thomas and Jermaine Eluemunor in place for the foreseeable future.

So we're turning to the defensive side of the ball and Jide Abasiri. We have, for the most part, looked at defensive linemen with nose tackle upside for the Giants. However, it's certainly possible that they're set there for a good long while with both Bobby Jamison-Travis and C.J. Okoye in the fold. Abasiri profiles as much more of a defensive end or under tackle who can play the 5-technique, 4i, or 3-technique depending on the down, distance, and package called. He's a powerful player at 6-foot-5, 280 pounds with the room on his frame to add weight if necessary. Abasiri is a better run defender than pass rusher right now, and could be a player in the mold of Chauncey Golston.

There are a lot of different ways we could go with this BYU squad, but LJ Martin makes sense in the context of how focused the Giants have been on the running back position since free agency.

Martin has been limited by ankle injuries this year, but is a force when he's healthy. He's a physical downhill runner at 6-foot-2 and 225, and is a very difficult player to bring down. He has very good vision and patience behind the line of scrimmage, as well as excellent contact balance and surprising agility for such a big player. Martin is averaging 4.5 yards per carry after contact and 6.6 yards per carry overall. He's also a useful player in the passing game, with great upside as a pass protector while also being an underrated receiver out of the backfield. Martin lacks home run speed, but everything else about him says "every-down Bellcow".

We've mentioned Tengesdahl before, and it bears repeating. The Cincinnati offensive line is probably the most interesting unit on their team from a Giants' perspective, and the left guard spot is the most in need of an upgrade for the Giants.

The contract extension signed by Jon Runyan Jr. this year could make it difficult for the Giants to move on from him, but it's still something they could consider after the season. Tengesdahl is a squat, powerful guard at 6-foot-3, 325 pounds with obvious power in his upper and lower body. He has the potential to be a mauler in the run game as well as a reliable pass protector against most defensive tackles. The Giants should probably consider leaving Sisi Mauigoa at right guard to fully develop and finding a right tackle of the future later on, so pairing Mauigoa with a player like Tengesdahl could give them a very formidable interior.

Sherrill is probably the Commodores' best prospect this year, and the one most scouts will be watching this year. He's a compact, twitchy receiver at 5-foot-11, 200 pounds, and is a threat at every level of the defense. He has the agility to uncover quickly for short passes as well as the ball skills to produce on intermediate and deep routes. He will likely excel in offenses that put receivers in position to create yards after the catch, and could be a real weapon in a "Shanahan-McVay" scheme. With that in mind, it's a question as to whether a team like the Giants are the best fit. However, he's also improved every year in college and can probably be a productive player for any offense.

I've been wanting to talk about Carter for a while, and this is a great opportunity. The Giants' linebacking corps is the deepest and most talented its been in decades, but they should still consider Carter if he's available. Not only is he a great player, he is a (by all accounts) a great human. Carter's girlfriend, an ICU nurse, was diagnosed with cancer early this year and he has publicly supported her through her fight. He now makes a donation to cancer research for every play he makes, from $427 for a tackle and $500 for a sack, to $1,000 for a turnover and $5,000 for a defensive touchdown — So far he's donated over $30,000.

The $427 amount is a reference to the two games he was suspended at the start of the season by the NCAA for accepting a $427 flight from an agent last year when he briefly declared for the draft before returning to school (and paying the agent back).

On the field, Carter is a menace. He's a bit undersized at 6-foot-1, 235 pounds, but he has a sky-high football IQ, a wide-open motor, and the athleticism to cover a lot of ground at the second level. He's been highly productive this year (as you could guess from his donation total), notching 76 total tackles, 11.0 tackles for a loss, 5.0 sacks, 1 forced fumble, 1 pass defensed, and 3 fumble recoveries. His draft stock will be interesting to follow over the winter and spring, but his character and ability on the field could make him very attractive to the Giants wherever he lands on their board.

It's honestly difficult to highlight a single Alabama player, and rather than try to separate a single defender from a thoroughly talented defense, I'm going to the offensive highlight.

Ryan Coleman-Williams exploded onto the national scene as a true freshman, taking the country by storm. He was unable to maintain the torrid pace of his freshman campaign and took a step back as a sophomore, struggling mightily with consistency. He's rediscovered his consistency this year, and with it his confidence, and has returned to his 2024 form.

Coleman-Williams has a similar build and skillset as Malik Nabers at 6-foot, and roughly 185 pounds (though he could certainly come in heavier at the Combine), with simply electric quickness and speed. He has the type of twitch to allow him to separate at will on underneath routes or pick up chunk yardage on run-after-catch opportunities, and the speed to blow the top off a defense on vertical routes. He's a surprisingly physical and competitive receiver, with unexpected ability as a blocker in the run game or on screen passes.

Concerns about Coleman-Williams' consistency and ball skills could drop him in an absurdly deep receiver draft, but that could also make him an absolute steal for a team that believes in him and is able to develop him.

I was tempted to go with iDL Cody Sigler here, and he's definitely worth keeping an eye on as a later-round defensive tackle prospect. That said, Alabama boasts one of the best defenses — and defensive secondaries in particular — in college football. That makes this an excellent opportunity for WR Keshaun Singleton to boost his draft stock.

Singleton is similar to Malachi Fields as a big-bodied outside receiver at 6-foot 220 pounds, with good suddenness out of his breaks for a big receiver and strong hands at the catch point. He also doesn't have great deep speed, but is able to create separation with his route running. Singleton has a career-high 58 catches after transferring from South Florida to Auburn this year, and has currently tied his career-high 8 touchdowns. He could be a name to watch on the third day of the draft.

The Giants like Bryan Hudson at center, but they could also decide to find competition for the spot or an outright upgrade. If so, Iapani Laloulu (nicknamed "Pancho") is a player to keep an eye on. He's played both left and right guard as well as center for the Ducks, but he's excelled at center since moving there last year. He has great natural leverage and a powerful frame at 6-foot-2, 325 pounds, and at least as much play strength as his frame would indicate. While many centers tend to be lighter and use positioning and technique to beat bigger nose tackles, Laloulu meets them on their own turf and plays with a mauling mentality. He's capable of distorting the line of scrimmage and is very difficult to uproot and move off the ball when he's playing with good leverage.

Greg Roman wants to be able to run downhill and between the tackles, and that could make Laloulu very attractive to the Giants.

Washington is another school that's a bit frustrating from a scouting perspective. They have intriguing players, but few of them are draft eligible this year, and their most intriguing player — Demond Williams Jr. — is a quarterback.

McLaughlin, however, is both a senior and an interesting safety prospect. He has great size for the position at 6-foot-2, 205 pounds and currently leads the Huskies in tackles (79), tackles for a loss (9.5), sacks (3.0), forced fumbles (1), passes defensed (8), and interceptions (3). He's a physical downhill player (as you can tell from his tackle production) as well as natural ballhawk who has produced against the pass in each of the last two years and also as a pick-six in both 2025 and 2026. Considering how well McClaughlin adapted when he transferred to Washington from FCS level Northern Arizona, it's likely that he'll either see his draft stock rise considerably over the coming months or that he'll far out-play his draft slot.

#2735. EU leaders fail to break $1.7 trillion budget deadlock as political pressure mounts

European leaders failed to break a deadlock over the EU's next long-term budget on Thursday, leaving negotiators under pressure to meet their year-end target before a busy 2027 election cycle adds further complications.

National leaders are divided over the European Union's next long-term budget, which sets spending priorities and limits from 2028 to 2034. It requires unanimous approval from all 27 member states to pass, as well as a majority vote in favor from members of the European Parliament.

Representatives from EU member states met on Wednesday and Thursday to discuss the latest budget proposal from European Council President António Costa, which amounts to a spending plan worth 1.58 trillion euros ($1.73 trillion). It marks a downward revision of around 10% from the original proposals for the bloc's long-term budget — known as the Multiannual Financial Framework, or MFF.

The EU's budget negotiations come against a backdrop of surging government borrowing costs, spiraling inflation and sluggish growth across much of the continent, as well as concerns about rising budget deficits in various EU countries.

Failure to meet the year-end deadline for the budget to be agreed risks deepening the political deadlock at the center of the ongoing talks.

At a summit in Brussels Thursday, budget talks went on for 12 hours, according to a BBC report — with some participants said to have walked out of the meeting as conflicts emerged around spending priorities and aid for Ukraine.

A spokesperson for the European Commission was not available to comment on Thursday's talks.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz told reporters on Thursday evening that the talks had brought "new movement" towards reducing the overall size of the budget, adding that leaders would reconvene in mid-December to resume talks.

But he also said: "The European Union comprises of 27 member states, not 23 or 24."

Merz, alongside the leaders of Denmark, the Netherlands, Austria, Finland and Sweden, said in September that the original 2-trillion-euro proposal would need to be reduced by "several hundred billion euros."

"We must make choices," they said in a letter addressed to Costa and Irish Taoiseach Micheál Martin, pointing out that their six countries finance almost 40% of all contributions to the budget.

They called for funding to be focused on defense spending, competitiveness and innovation and "the fight against irregular migration."

Costa labeled the revised budget plans "realistic" when he presented them last week, noting that despite the 10.2% reduction in spending the proposals still represented a 120% jump in competitiveness funding and a 232% upswing for defense compared to the current budget.

"We didn't reach an agreement," he said in a statement after Thursday's meeting. "Regarding the global volume of the budget, some member states asked for further reductions, most opposed further cuts. And some even proposed to reverse some cuts."

Some European parliamentarians also appeared unconvinced by the new proposals.

"Parliament is consistent and proposes more investments, [the] Council is inconsistent and proposes cuts," Romanian MEP Siegfried Mureşan said on Wednesday.

"When the Council finally reaches a position, that's when negotiations will start, not when they end. As prime ministers cannot dictate to national parliaments, prime ministers also cannot dictate to the European Parliament to adopt a budget that is too small, and which does not equip Europe for the future."

Elections are set to go ahead in key EU economies including Spain, Poland and Italy over the next 12 months, while France's spring presidential election is at the forefront of regional leaders' minds.

Current front-runner for the French presidency, far-right candidate Marine Le Pen, has previously called for the country to leave the EU and to cut its contributions to the bloc's budget.

Incumbent French President Emmanuel Macron stressed on Thursday that he had limited time left to have his say on EU spending.

"After 31 December, I will no longer be in a position, politically or democratically, to reopen discussions on the budget and bring them to a conclusion, as we will be ​entering a period far too close to the presidential election in France," he told reporters after the talks, according to French publication France 24.

France, the EU's second-biggest economy after Germany, is being tasked with drastically cutting spending to bring its 5.1% national deficit closer to an EU guideline of 3%, while reeling from months of mass protests, riots and strikes over education funding and a cost-of-living crisis.

Other tech-related stocks, including Micron, Qualcomm, Lumentum and Tesla all gained over 5% on Friday as the sector rebounded from a heavy sell-off earlier this week. SanDisk was Friday's biggest mover as investors rotated back into some AI trade names, surging 15.96%.

In a separate post on X, Musk said SpaceX's contribution would be transferred next month, while his personal donation would follow when his lockup period expires in June next year.

Nearly 70 million Trump Accounts had been created as of October, according to the White House.

"Not just SpaceX, but all sorts of different players in this market. Because at the end of the day, we want everyone to compete and succeed. I think we're going to see a lot of players entering this space soon," Carr said.

#2731. Cleveland Browns: Remembering their two Sunday night wins

The Cleveland Browns make their return to Sunday Night Football this week as they host the Las Vegas Raiders.

It has been five years since the NFL deemed the Browns worthy of a Sunday night game, with the last appearance resulting in a 16-10 loss to the Baltimore Ravens in Week 12 of the 2021 season in a game where the Browns intercepted Baltimore quarterback Lamar Jackson four times.

The last time it was a home game? That was in 2019, when the Browns lost 20-13 to the Los Angeles Rams, as Cleveland's late comeback fell short after the Browns were unable to convert a first-and-goal with less than a minute remaining.

This will be just the 14th time the Browns will be playing on a Sunday night in the regular season, with the first game coming in 1984 when they faced the Denver Broncos in a special edition of Monday Night Football.

Cleveland is just 2-11 in their previous appearances, so let's take a look back at those two victories.

Cleveland's maiden win on a Sunday night was also the last time they won a regular-season game in Pittsburgh.

Quarterback Tim Couch threw touchdown passes to André Davis and Kevin Johnson to give the Browns a 16-3 lead early in the second quarter. The Steelers pulled within six points after a one-yard touchdown run by Jerome Bettis, but Couch smartly moved the Browns down the field and scored on a nine-yard touchdown run to send the Browns into halftime with a 23-10 lead.

Cleveland's defense essentially shut down any hopes that Pittsburgh had for a comeback when cornerback Daylon McCutcheon picked off quarterback Tommy Maddox and returned it 75 yards for a touchdown just five minutes into the second half.

Couch finished the game by completing 20-of-25 passes for 208 yards and two touchdowns in what turned out to be the last highlight of his career.

Cleveland moved to 10-4 on the season after taking down the Giants in a game flexed into the Sunday night time slot.

Quarterback Baker Mayfield led the Browns on a pair of 95-yard touchdown drives as he finished 27-of-32 for 297 yards and touchdown passes to Jarvis Landry and Austin Hooper. Running back Nick Chubb capped off the scoring for Cleveland with a one-yard touchdown run in the fourth quarter.

Cleveland's defense limited quarterback Colt McCoy, starting in place of the injured Daniel Jones, to just 221 passing yards, and the Giants finished with just 288 yards of total offense.

The win moved Cleveland closer to their first playoff berth since 2002 and was their first season with double-digit wins since 2007.

Sunday nights have not been kind to the Browns over the years, but this week's game against the Raiders presents a huge opportunity for Cleveland. Win, and the Browns not only stay firmly in the playoff race in the AFC, but also set up a showdown next week with the Cincinnati Bengals for first place in the AFC North.

The Browns have been waiting five years to be back on Sunday night. Time to show the nation what they have been missing.

#2730. Chinese chip stocks notch biggest ever one-day gains as Beijing signals broader market support

Chinese stocks rebounded sharply on Friday after a rare intraday state media readout cheered investors as top policymakers pledged to expand the role of state-backed funds in supporting the market.

The STAR index surged 14%, led by Chinese chipmakers such as CXMT, Cambricon and SMIC. The CSI Semiconductor index of 40 chip stocks climbed by 17.6% in its biggest single-day gain on record, surpassing the prior record of nearly 17% in late 2024, according to Wind Information.

The gains were widespread across mainland and Hong Kong stock indexes. The Hang Seng index rose 2%, while the CSI 300 index climbed 4%, its biggest jump since October 2024, according to Wind data.

The rally comes as governments pour support into domestic chip industries while tightening controls on sensitive technology in the global race for artificial intelligence.

The U.S. unveiled a new rule last week to further restrict Chinese companies’ access to advanced AI chips, including through overseas data centers, adding pressure on Beijing to accelerate its push for semiconductor self-sufficiency.

Friday's rally comes after the CSI 300 on Thursday sank to its lowest level since the escalation of the U.S.-China tariff war in the spring of 2025, according to Wind data.

In recent months, investors have weighed the commercial feasibility of Chinese artificial intelligence developers and tighter U.S. restrictions on advanced chips.

Chinese Premier Li Qiang oversaw a meeting Friday morning of the State Council, the country's top governing body, which saw officials pledge to strengthen the role of "National Team" — referring to state-backed funds — to stabilize markets and ensure ample liquidity, according to a readout released by state media Xinhua News.

The explicit reference to the "national team" is a first for a State Council meeting, said Bob Chen, a Shanghai-based venture capitalist and economist.

Authorities also stressed that "capital markets are crucial to the broader economy," according to the readout, a rare comment from a government that has not always looked favorably on the financial sector.

"Stock market performance is now prioritized at the highest level for the Chinese government," which is boosting investor confidence, Chen said.

"That's very strong language from the top officials," said Zong Ke, a portfolio manager at a Shanghai-based quantitative fund.

By Ke's calculations, stock trading volumes surged rapidly after the readout was released around 1:00 p.m. local time (12:00 a.m. ET). Full-day turnover across the Shanghai, Shenzhen and Beijing exchanges was 52% higher than the previous session.

Policymakers also vowed to strengthen the role of "stabilization funds" like Central Huijin Investment, a state-owned investment firm that holds equity stakes on behalf of the State Council.

That message "effectively institutionalized Central Huijin's role as a state stabilization fund," said Hong Hao, managing partner of Lotus Asset Management. "And it's significantly positive news for the market."

Hao expects this market rally to prove more durable than the "fleeting bounce" triggered by Beijing's stimulus talks in the past, which wore off in days. "This time it's for real."

The readout of the Cabinet's meeting, typically released well after trading hours, was published around noon local time during trading hours, an unusual timing that's viewed by markets as a bullish signal.

The State Council also pledged better policies to steer medium- or long-term funds, such as insurance and social security funds, into equities, and encouraged listed companies to pay more dividends and step up share buybacks, while vowing a crackdown on financial fraud to protect investors.

The national team will accelerate asset purchases, Ke said, adding that he expects more supportive policies to follow, such as further loosening the institutional constraints that have limited long-term funds from investing in stocks.

Beijing's reliance on intervention to offset stock volatility has, however, raised questions about the market's ability to sustain longer-term gains.

But the market needs this kind of support right now, said Ke, as the near-term aim is to ease liquidity pressure and restore confidence so the market doesn't fall much further.

Chinese AI-related stocks also carry less downside risk than their U.S. counterparts, as the companies have spent less on AI so far and their shares have already been through a sharp correction, Ke said.

That, together with the pledged state backing, has given investors more confidence that the market may be nearing its bottom, Ke added.

#2729. Spurs use strong second half to rally for win over Lakers

The San Antonio Spurs got through a Thanksgiving hangover in the first half to rally from a 20-point deficit and defeat the Los Angeles Lakers 99-96 at the Frost Bank Center on Friday night. With the win, the Spurs advanced in the NBA Cup, clinching the wildcard spot to face the Dallas Mavericks in the knockout round.

San Antonio played like they ate too much Thanksgiving turkey as the Lakers forced two consecutive turnovers to begin the game. The Spurs turned the ball over six times in the opening 12 minutes and were 0-for-9 to begin the game, not exactly a winning formula to win a basketball game against any team.

The Spurs' perimeter defense was nonexistent in the first half as several Lakers players were left open for three as Los Angeles built a 34-17 lead in the second quarter. San Antonio did not hit a three-pointer until 2:42 remaining in the second quarter and were just 2-of-12 from beyond the arc at the break.

San Antonio turned things around in the second half, chipping away at the Los Angeles lead. The Spurs opened the third quarter on a 12-7 spurt and outscored the Lakers 30-14 in the period behind a better offensive output and strong defense from Carter Bryant on Luka Doncic and Austin Reaves.

"That young man dominated the game for a portion of the game, and hope he feels that," Spurs coach Mitch Johnson said of Bryant's performance. "I hope he understands how different he can make us, and he jumps off the page or the screen if you're watching the game with his defensive capacity and capabilities.. he's a very talented young man, so he was a big part of when we flipped the game."

Keldon Johnson nailed his only three of the game in the closing moments of the third quarter to tie the game at 68 going into the final 12 minutes. In the fourth quarter, Victor Wembanyama and Stephon Castle locked in defensively to make life difficult for Doncic, who scored 31 points on 31 shot attempts.

Offensively, Castle took advantage of being left open and made the Lakers pay, drilling three of four triples en route to a team-leading 26 points.

"We put the work in, and you see the results come," Castle said of his performance after the game. "It's definitely a great feeling. I appreciate my teammates and my coaches keep trusting me with that. I will keep staying aggressive with it."

Props to the Frost Bank Center crowd for being loud. When the Spurs made their comeback in the second half, it felt like the Western Conference Finals in there. It played a big part in the energy for the team for sure.

Dylan Harper returned after a 10 day absence from a hip injury and he looked like he didn't miss a beat. He was San Antonio's best player in the first half and would finish with 14 points off the bench in 24 minutes.

Wemby would shake off a poor first half to score 25 points on 10-of-25 shooting and pulled down 16 rebounds. While Doncic had more points, Wembanayama got his team the win and first place in the Western Conference standings as of now.

Wembanyama mentioned postgame that the Spurs did not come out "meaner" to open the game like they did in the second half. This has me curious how they will come out on Sunday afternoon, where they have historically struggled in afternoon games.

The Spurs are now 16-4 on the season and will return to the court on Sunday afternoon when they face the Boston Celtics at 3 p.m. to close out a brief two-game homestand.

#2728. Budget airline Norse Atlantic files for bankruptcy in Europe after failing to secure funding

Budget carrier Norse Atlantic filed for bankruptcy Friday, saying it failed to secure funding after soaring fuel costs strained the company's operations throughout the year.

Its parent company, Norse Atlantic ASA, concluded that the Norwegian carrier no longer has a basis to continue operations.

"Despite extensive efforts, the Company has so far not been able to secure sufficient funding to meet its short-term liquidity needs," the firm said in a statement Friday.

It comes days after Norse said it's facing a "critical financial situation" and was considering its options from a strategic takeover to ACMI contracts, which refer to short-term capacity leasing. The company's shares closed 85% lower on Wednesday.

The board has now filed a bankruptcy petition with the Agder District Court, where a trustee will be appointed to manage the process further.

"The Board of Norse Atlantic Airways AS has made the difficult decision to file for bankruptcy despite extensive efforts from the Company and the Group to find a solution," Norse Atlantic CEO Eivind Roald said.

"We are deeply sorry for the impact this has on our passengers, employees and partners. The Board, management and advisers of the listed entity Norse Atlantic ASA and the UK subsidiary are working to find a solution for the remaining part of the business to continue operations," Roald added.

The company has canceled all flights, and ticket sales have been discontinued.

Norse was founded in March 2021 and offered cheap flights between Europe, North America, Africa and Asia.

The Iran war began on Feb. 28 and caused jet fuel prices to skyrocket, forcing airlines around the world to grapple with higher costs. Prices are now up nearly 72% to $155 per barrel year-on-year as of the week ending Nov. 20, according to the International Air Transport Association's Jet Fuel Price Monitor.

Norse Atlantic's Roald told CNBC in May that he wasn't "overly concerned" about access to fuel but flagged that the company had canceled over 700 flights, which impacted more than 240,000 passengers.

Ryanair's CEO Michael O'Leary warned in April that if jet fuel prices continued to stay elevated throughout the year, then other European airlines were likely to face "financial difficulties" during the winter season.

In fact, several European airlines have stopped operating this winter, including Slovakia's AirExplore, Spain's AlbaStar, Ireland's Fly4 and Spain's Plus Ultra, which has canceled all flights but hasn't permanently closed.

#2727. Cowboys vs. Eagles: The good, the bad, and ugly from Week 12

To say the Dallas Cowboys completely embarrassed themselves with the 39-38 loss to the Philadelphia Eagles on Thanksgiving is an understatement. This was a game they were in complete control of until they had a meltdown in the fourth quarter allowing the Eagles to come back and win with a last-second field goal.

Hopefully the Cowboys embarrassing loss didn't leave a sour taste in your mouth ruining your Thanksgiving meal because this Week 12 loss has to be one of the biggest letdowns many of us have witnessed from this organization. There was a lot of good for Dallas in this matchup, but sadly all of the bad and ugly made it all but meaningless.

Week in and week out CeeDee Lamb and Quinnen Williams are continually proving why they are considered among the best that their positions. Both are playing at a consistent All-Pro level this season and are quite possibly having the best year of their careers despite playing on a bad team. 134 receiving yards on seven catches has No. 88 at 1,242 yards on the season, putting him well on pace to shatter his season best in 2023 (1,749). And, with another QB sack Sunday, No. 92 nine so far this year means he could break his 12-sack season in 2022.

Entering the fourth quarter Sunday afternoon the Cowboys had a comfortable lead leading the Eagles 31-17. For most teams around the league that usually equates to victory, but sadly not for the Cowboys. Dallas was able to put seven more points on the board, but allowed the Eagles to score 22, including a last-second field goal that secured the 39-38 victory. We can point the finger at players and coaches alike that led to this meltdown, but we can probably be all agree the majority of the blame lands at Brian Schottenheimer's feet.

The Cowboys defense was able to sack Joe Milton five times Sunday afternoon, but he ended up getting the better of them in the end, picking apart the secondary and making them look absolutely ugly. The Cowboys castoff, and the Eagles third QB of the year, ended up throwing for 331 yards and four touchdowns in this Week 12 matchup. He's the latest backup QB to shred Dallas' secondary this season and may or may not be the last. Regardless of what his future may be in the league, he completely embarrassed his former teammates on Thanksgiving.

#2725. The Nasdaq is in freefall. Here's one stock to look at

I asked Claude after Friday's close what I should do about the tanking stock market. Its advice was surprisingly useful: avoid panic selling and look for opportunities. A 40 VIX, which it touched Friday, might provide those opportunities. Specifically, a higher VIX (higher options premiums) signals fear, but it also creates an opportunity to sell elevated premium in some premium stocks, like Amazon.

Amazon's cloud business is growing at its fastest pace in nearly five years. AWS revenue rose 39.3% YoY in the third quarter, and management reported a $578 billion backlog. Trailing 12-month operating cash flow rose 29% to $168.3 billion. However, infrastructure investment accelerated, resulting in more than $42 billion in negative cash flow. The competing forces of accelerating growth and the cost of supplying it could keep the stock in its range over the next four weeks. Its shares are only off 14% from its recent highs, comparatively better than the Nasdaq Composite's near 19% decline.

AWS demand supports the lower end of the range. The backlog gives Amazon a basis for adding capacity even as investors scrutinize the cash cost. Cash spending could temper an upside rerating.

The next leg higher may require evidence that new capacity can earn attractive returns at this scale, and that evidence may be in short supply over the next couple of months.

Sell the Dec. 18, 2026 AMZN 235/285 strangle for an indicative $5.90 credit per share.

Max reward — $590 per strangle if AMZN finishes between $235 and $285 at expiration, before fees.

Breakevens at expiration — $229.10 and $290.90.

Max risk — Unlimited on the uncovered call, but one should think of the trade as risking getting short at a net price above $290, a nearly 13% premium to today's closing price. The downside loss would reach $22,910 per strangle if AMZN fell to zero. The credit is roughly 2.3% of a $260 reference share price over four weeks, or nearly 30% on a simple annualized basis. That comparison uses the share price as its denominator. Actual return on capital depends on margin and the cost to close or settle the trade.

Buying in bear markets, as the Nasdaq just touched, is never easy. But those opportunities don't present themselves too often. What are you going to do with it?

#2723. Utah Jazz vs Denver Nuggets: Recap and final score

With the return of Jusuf Nurkic, the Utah Jazz got a taste of what the rest of their season will look like, but it was sadly not enough to get a win. Utah lost to the Denver Nuggets 120-107.

It wasn't an unwinnable game, though, which has been the biggest frustration this entire season. Yes, the Utah Jazz are running some very young lineups, but they have been in a lot of these games, and tonight was another example of them not being able to close it out.

It's the usual list of reasons. The first being turnovers. Utah had 18 in this one compared to 9 for the Nuggets. And these aren't your run-of-the-mill half-court offense turnovers, the kind you expect game to game. These are the pick-6 type turnovers, the ones that lead to immediate points in transition for the other team. Keyonte George had a fantastic scoring night: he shot 9/14 from the field, 5/9 from three, and scored 25 points. He deserves full credit for being a big part of Utah being in this game with that efficient scoring. But what could have gotten the Jazz over the hump was if he hadn't turned the ball over 6 times. And Keyonte George isn't the only one turning the ball over, but this is a good example of why Utah is right there. They have the talent and show that they can absolutely win more games, they just shoot themselves in the foot and it's what is causing a lot of these losses.

The good news? These are fixable things. Can you get the incredible scoring from George minus the turnovers? If you can, he becomes an all-star player and Utah will start rising in the standings.

The other reason Utah lost this game? More questionable substitutions. With two minutes left, Will Hardy had Kyle Filipowski and Svi Mykhailiuk in the game over Ace Bailey and Josh Green. It's clear that was done to help with the offense, but it didn't work. We've had multiple games like this over the season where bench players have been given the green light to end games, but it hasn't worked. You also have to ask yourself: what is someone like Kyle Filipowski doing that earns him minutes? Ace Bailey missed a corner three that would have been huge, and it led to him getting subbed out at the end of this one. But if you rewatch this game and watch Kyle Filipowski near the basket, he's almost nonexistent as a deterrent to the other team scoring. Yes, it would be nice if Ace Bailey were a more consistent three-point shooter, but what exactly has Kyle Filipowski done to earn that trust from Will Hardy? It's a little baffling.

Jusuf Nurkic looked great on offense as a passer and screener. Keyonte George, Darryn Peterson, and Ace Bailey all got better looks and easier buckets, whether from getting cuts to the basket or brick-wall screens opening up lanes and shooting pockets. Clearly, Nurkic was on some sort of minute restriction. But when Nurkic starts playing closer to 30 minutes per game, we should see this Jazz offense reach the level we all expected.

All in all, it's a frustrating loss because it was a winnable game. But with the return of Jusuf Nurkic, this Jazz team looks better and should have a better shot at beating the Suns on Sunday.

#2722. Liesman: Why Greenspan could bet on productivity — and Warsh needs to hike

In a discussion on CNBC's Halftime Report Wednesday, the question came up: Why can't Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh hold the line on interest rates the way his icon Alan Greenspan did at the beginning of the dot-com era?

Greenspan famously leaned against inflation hawks on his committee in 1995 and 1996, holding the line on interest rates amid strong growth, because he believed in the productivity promise of the internet revolution.

It would be nice if Warsh could follow the same playbook amid the artificial intelligence build-out and keep rates steady until the productivity arrives. Unfortunately, a quick look at the numbers shows just how different the situation is for Warsh when it comes to inflation, the magnitude of the capital-investment boom and the monetary and fiscal stimulus boosting the economy.

Bottom line: the Fed looks like it has to hike — and maybe substantially — to be anywhere close to the position where the Fed was in Greenspan's time at the dawn of a major technological revolution.

It may be that the massive capex spending on AI will mean more productivity and less inflation down the road. But the front-end build-out is proving to be more inflationary, and more challenging, to a Fed already dealing with high inflation. In many ways, that's the opposite of what Greenspan had to handle.

#2721. Top candidates for UTSA's next coach as Roadrunners search for deep Texas ties

As UTSA searches for a new head coach in the wake of Jeff Traylor's departure, the Roadrunners' top priority will be finding a candidate with deep Texas connections, a program source said.

A mix of sitting head coaches, former head coaches and current coordinators are all expected to be considered, with an emphasis on finding a leader who can recruit the state. The ideal candidate will understand UTSA's philosophy, the culture of San Antonio and the qualities that have worked for the Roadrunners in the past.

UTSA's next hire will be just the fourth coach in the history of the young program. Though Traylor and Baylor had not finalized a contract as of Saturday, a source said an agreement appears imminent.

With that in mind, a look at five names to watch in UTSA's search for a replacement:

LUBBOCK, TEXAS - NOVEMBER 07: Offensive Coordinator Mack Leftwich of the Texas Tech Red Raiders celebrates during the second half of the game against the West Virginia Mountaineers at Galaxy Stadium on November 07, 2026 in Lubbock, Texas. (Photo by John E. Moore III/Getty Images) (John E. Moore III/Getty Images)

A 32-year-old Denton native, Leftwich is viewed as one of the brightest young offensive minds in the sport. He also boasts familiarity with the area, orchestrating high-flying attacks as the offensive coordinator at Incarnate Word in 2022 and with Texas State in 2023 and 2024 before making the jump to Texas Tech last year.

He's spent five years of his career in San Antonio, working as an assistant at UIW from 2018 through 2021 before earning the promotion to coordinator.

Leftwich would become the youngest head coach in the FBS, but he's already been the target of speculation in at least one search, telling the media in Lubbock last week that he would not be pursuing the vacancy at his alma mater, UTEP.

"Being a head coach is something that at some point in my career I would like to do. That's a goal of mine and an aspiration I have," Leftwich told the media in Lubbock. "Right now, that opportunity just didn't seem like the right move for me, as much love as I have for my alma mater."

Texas Tech remains in the thick of the race for a College Football Playoff berth, checking in at No. 11 in the latest rankings with a spot in the Big 12 championship game already secured.

Whether a UTSA offer would be strong enough to lure Leftwich into the next phase of his career remains to be seen.

HOUSTON, TEXAS - AUGUST 28: Head coach Colby Carthel of the Stephen F. Austin Lumberjacks looks on during the fourth quarter against the Houston Cougars at TDECU Stadium on August 28, 2025 in Houston, Texas. (Photo by Maria Lysaker/Getty Images) (Maria Lysaker/Getty Images)

The year before Traylor was hired at UTSA, he interviewed for the open position at his alma mater Stephen F. Austin, only to see the program instead hire one of his longtime friends in Colby Carthel.

A 50-year-old Texas native, Carthel has spent his entire career in the state, leading the Lumberjacks for the past eight seasons. Stephen F. Austin has put together its best season under Carthel's watch this year, surging to an 11-1 record and the No. 5 seed in the FCS playoffs while claiming a second consecutive Southland Conference championship.

LOUISVILLE, KY - OCTOBER 09: Florida State Seminoles Head Coach Mike Norvell during a college football game between the Florida State Seminoles and Louisville Cardinals on October 9, 2026 at L&N Federal Credit Union Stadium in Louisville, KY. (Photo by James Black/Icon Sportswire via Getty Images) (Icon Sportswire/Icon Sportswire via Getty Images)

Perhaps the most headline-grabbing hire in play for the Roadrunners, Norvell could view UTSA as a chance to begin rebuilding his profile just weeks after being fired from Florida State.

Norvell finished his seven-year tenure at Florida State with a 41-39 record, most notably leading the program to a 13-0 start in 2023 before being snubbed from selection to a four-team College Football Playoff because of an injury to starting quarterback Jordan Travis.

The Seminoles' program never rebounded from the heartbreak, but Norvell has proven he can thrive at the Group of Six level, going 38-15 across four seasons at Memphis from 2016 through 2019.

Norvell is under no financial pressure to embrace the Group of Six grind - Florida State owes him a nearly $50 million buyout - but the 45-year-old Irving native could embrace the chance to start restoring his reputation with his first Texas-based job.

DENTON, TX - AUGUST 30: Lamar Cardinals head coach Pete Rossomando walks along the sidelines during the game between the North Texas Mean Green and the Lamar Cardinals on August 30, 2025 at DATCU Stadium in Denton, Texas. (Photo by Matthew Pearce/Icon Sportswire via Getty Images) (Icon Sportswire/Icon Sportswire via Getty Images)

Another FCS standout, Rossomando has led an improvement in each of his four years at Lamar, culminating with a 10-2 record this season and a No. 4 seed in the FCS playoffs. The Cardinals' offense is led by junior quarterback Aiden McCown, the younger brother of UTSA quarterback Owen McCown.

A 54-year-old native of Staten Island, New York, Rossomando is light on Texas roots, as the stop at Lamar marks his only experience in the state. His first head coaching experience was at the Division II level with the University of New Haven in New Haven, Conn. - UTSA president Taylor Eighmy's hometown - from 2009 through 2013. He also coached at the FCS level with Central Connecticut from 2014 through 2018.

BATON ROUGE, LOUISIANA - SEPTEMBER 06: Head coach Sonny Cumbie of the Louisiana Tech Bulldogs (R) speaks to Eli Finley #84 of the Louisiana Tech Bulldogs prior to the game against the LSU Tigers at Tiger Stadium on September 06, 2025 in Baton Rouge, Louisiana. (Photo by Tyler Kaufman/Getty Images) (Tyler Kaufman/Getty Images)

A 45-year-old Abilene native who spent a combined 13 years in assistant roles at Texas Tech and TCU, Cumbie has built Louisiana Tech into a top Group of Six contender through his five years at the helm.

The Bulldogs enter the weekend 9-2 and 7-0 in the Sun Belt, earning votes in both major top 25 polls and fighting for College Football Playoff consideration.

Though moving from one Group of Six program to another could be considered a lateral switch, the American offers a higher profile than the Sun Belt. Louisiana Tech also announced in February that Cumbie's restructured annual salary was $760,000 for this year, coming in at less than a third of Traylor's $2.7 million salary in 2026.

Still, Cumbie's interest in the UTSA opening is not guaranteed, as he may opt to wait out a Power Four opportunity.

#2720. Falcons with much to prove during the final stretch of 2026

Only seven games remain in the regular season, and we still have more questions than answers about this roster and coaching staff. These are the Falcons that I'll be keeping a close eye on.

If Kendal Daniels and Harold Perkins Jr. get to have their training wheels taken off, why doesn't Terrell? The Falcons made Avieon their first pick of the Cunningham-Ryan era, but he's become an afterthought and is losing out during the weekly roster shuffles when he should always be in the deck.

It's not like the corner hasn't performed when given the opportunity; he's been as steady as one could ask of a rookie. Jeff Ulbrich needs to override the special teams-driven decisions and give his rookie the developmental snaps he needs.

One of the most polarizing Falcons in franchise history continues to divide the fan base. You may not be a fan, but you won't convince me that the player leading the team in receiving touchdowns is useless. Pitts has been a legitimate weapon in the red zone, something fans begged to see for years, but his usage outside of that area has once again called his role on this team into question. Rees doesn't allow the tight end to stretch his legs until the game is out of hand.

Pitts is eating a heavy diet of screens and short routes, and that doesn't play into his skill set. If the Falcons can't get the tight end involved more over these final seven games, then a potential trade should be explored this offseason.

Jessie Bates is no longer here, which means Watts has the keys to the backend, but the safety has been in a sophomore slump. He's still shown flashes of upper-tier safety play, but not enough. Watts' open-field tackling has been far too inconsistent, and he's been at the scene of one too many coverage busts. With Bates gone, Watts can play like more of a traditional FS while Hellams slides in as a more traditional SS.

This should help Watts and simplify his responsibilities; he struggled in the box and is better further from the line of scrimmage. The team will need to invest in another safety this offseason, but if Watts finishes the season strong, the team won't have to spend major capital on his next running mate.

Fans can't seem to decide who they dislike more. Penix purists will tell you the OC isn't giving the quarterback enough opportunities to succeed. At the same time, the other side of the narrative is adamant that the QB is leaving his play caller hanging out to dry with his inaccuracy. Both sides make valid points, but when push comes to shove, one of these two will be dropped if things don't improve.

Rees has to improve his game flow and stay committed to the run, even if it's an illusion. Take the 49ers game, for example: the Falcons didn't dominate on the ground, but they ran Bijan 20 times and threw the ball out of multiple TE personnel groupings at a higher rate than in any other game. The threat of the formation and consistent run plays helped relieve pressure on Penix and the offense, and Rees needs to do more of that. He also needs to adjust his 3rd-down screen rate and force the QB to either make a play or fail trying. He won't get better on those downs tossing it to poorly blocked screens.

Penix has to hold up his end of the deal and keep the offense on track. His misses have created more timelines than a Marvel movie. Rees has schemed up opportunities for the quarterback, but he isn't cashing in on them, and it's exasperating the offense's lack of flow. When Penix is off schedule, everyone is, and his confidence is in shambles right now.

He has to be better at managing pressure; when accounting for throwaways, he's still off target more than anyone, and that's a critique fans are tired of hearing. He isn't going to be a volume shooter in this offense, and that means he needs to be efficient from start to finish. He'll never be under a bigger microscope than he will in these last 7 games.

Probably not good that his suspension ended 2 weeks ago and hardly anyone has noticed. The troubled defender got a slap on the wrist for his offseason antics, and now he needs to prove to the team he can be a professional and grow as a person. However, a mysterious back injury seems to be holding him back, and that's a problem for a player who needs to keep developing. 10.5 sacks is nothing to stick your nose up at, but half of those were cleanup sacks, and Pearce was the worst run-defending edge on the team.

Many of us remember that magical Vic Beasley season and know how numbers can be deceiving. Pearce can't live off his rookie season forever, but if this back keeps limiting him to 15 snaps a game, the team might shut him down early.

Some players listed aren't as worried about their futures as others, but that doesn't make their development any less important over this final stretch. Atlanta needs to get the best look at each because all of them can influence how Atlanta attacks the offseason. Who will you be watching the most?

#2718. Africa’s richest man is taking his mega-refinery public in a blockbuster IPO

One of the world's largest oil refineries is set to become Africa's biggest public firm in a highly anticipated offering that the continent's richest man has marketed as an "IPO for the people."

Dangote Petroleum Refinery and Petrochemicals FZE, founded by Nigerian billionaire Aliko Dangote, is expected to begin trading in Lagos on Dec. 1, according to multiple media reports, following regulatory approval. A Dangote Refinery spokesperson did not respond to a CNBC request for comment.

Reuters' calculations put Dangote Refinery's IPO-implied valuation at about $47 billion, based on the offer price and the company's registered share count. That would make it the biggest stock market listing Africa has ever seen.

The Lagos-based refinery is regarded as a crown jewel of Nigeria's energy industry given the facility's role in transforming the oil-rich country's fuel market since it started operating in 2024.

"With a minimum subscription of just 10 shares, the offer is designed to give ordinary Nigerians and Africans an opportunity to own a stake in the refinery," Aliko Dangote said at the IPO launch in early September.

"The IPO is positioned not simply as a capital-raising exercise, but as an opportunity for workers, entrepreneurs and everyday Nigerians to participate in the refinery's future growth," he added.

Built for around $20 billion, the Dangote Refinery is now one of the largest in the world, with a crude oil distillation capacity of between 650,000 to 700,000 barrels per day.

It has emerged as a key provider of jet fuel in recent months, with buyers pivoting to West Africa amid Middle Eastern supply disruptions due to the Iran war.

"In theory, it's a win-win situation," Cyril Widdershoven, a maritime, energy and geopolitical analyst at Blue Water Strategy, told CNBC by phone.

Dangote Refinery has bolstered its reputation as a key Atlantic Basin facility amid soaring global demand for refined petroleum products, Widdershoven said, and the company intends to double its refining capacity to 1.4 million barrels per day by 2029, which could further strengthen its competitiveness in global markets.

However, despite the refinery's importance to Nigeria's economy, questions remain over its investment case, Widdershoven said, citing construction costs, commissioning delays, and the possibility that new capacity comes online with weaker margins.

"Right now it's like Anthropic or Nvidia, you are now buying the hype," Widdershoven said.

The Dangote Refinery has had "an extremely strong year," Mick Strautmann, market analyst at Vortexa, told CNBC by phone.

Between January and October, Vortexa data showed Dangote's seaborne refined product loadings stood at around 400,000 barrels per day, which is roughly 50% more than the same period last year.

More recently, however, the facility has seen refined-product loading jump to a record of 575,000 barrels per day through the first three weeks of November, surpassing an October record of 550,000 barrels per day, Strautmann said.

"It's been able to ramp up quite spectacularly and provide a lot of these products to the market in an extremely high price environment," Strautmann added.

"So, it's been one of the few places that's adding a lot of fuel supply to the Atlantic Basin, and at the same time, you know, to make West Africa less reliant on imports from other regions, particularly Europe for its gasoline supply," he said.

David Omojomolo, an emerging market economist focused on Africa at Capital Economics, said the upcoming Dangote Refinery IPO has captured global attention because the facility is seen as a rare example of an industrial policy success story on the African continent.

"It's been a good news story, and it's been an optimistic one as well," Omojomolo told CNBC.

"I don't want to be a hostage to fortune and say this time is different … but it does feel like a buzz and it's a question as to does it force other, say, billionaire industrialists to put their money where their mouth is in terms of helping the region's industrialisation prospects," he added.

#2717. Lakers are ‘real’ deal — even Victor Wembanyama thinks so

Like the rest of us, Victor Wembanyama was skeptical of the Lakers.

The darlings of the NBA entered Friday's game against the Spurs with the top record in the NBA at 15-3. But they hadn't played the league's most elite teams. They hadn't really proven themselves.

So, Friday was a temperature check. It was a harsh dressing room mirror that would expose their flaws in front of a national television audience.

"I was surprised of the quality of the game that they offered and he offered, collectively," Wembanyama acknowledged. "I mean, I know what to expect of Luka. As a team I wasn't sure what they were built of, at least from the start of the season, but it's real. It's for real, they're good."

The Lakers lost, 99-96. But the final score was far less important than what transpired.

The Lakers led by as much as 20 points. They bottled up Wembanyama in the first half, holding him to just eight points on nine shots and five rebounds. Over that period, Luka Dončić had as many rebounds as Wembanyama (five) while scoring more than twice as many points (19).

The Spurs reached the Finals last year. Wembanyama is unequivocally considered the next face of the league once the 41-year-old LeBron James retires. This was about as big of a test as the Lakers could receive.

And at the beginning of the game, they were jaw-droppingly good.

The Lakers were outscored in the third quarter, 30-14. Over that period, they were outshot from the field, 48.1% to 25%, and from beyond the 3-point line, 37.5% to 15.4%. They unraveled, culminating in Keldon Johnson making a 3-pointer with 1.8 seconds left to knot the score at 68-68 heading into the fourth quarter.

Victor Wembanyama (left) and Luka Dončić look on during the Lakers' 99-96 NBA Cup loss to the Spurs on Nov. 27, 2026, in San Antonio. NBAE via Getty Images

Wembanyama and Dončić, who are in a two-horse race for the MVP Award, knew the stakes.

Everyone on the court was acutely aware of the chatter this game would generate. They knew this contest meant so much more than another regular-season game in November.

Ultimately, the Spurs clawed a few inches ahead as the final buzzer sounded.

After Wembanyama's sluggish start, he finished with 25 points, 16 rebounds, four steals and two blocked shots. But he had as many field-goal attempts (25) as points.

Dončić had a game-high 31 points, six rebounds, eight assists and three steals. But he went 1-for-13 from beyond the arc.

Luka Dončić looks to make a move on De'Aaron Fox during the Lakers' NBA Cup road loss to the Spurs. AP Photo/Darren Abate

Both of them shined. Both of them struggled. The Spurs likely prevailed because they've experienced moments like this before.

They were three wins away from a championship last year. If they hadn't blown double-digit leads in all five of their games against the Knicks in the Finals, they would've been the reigning champions.

If anything, it was incredibly impressive that the Lakers did what they did Friday. They have nine new players. They weren't supposed to be this good.

And on Friday, they were ready for this test.

They came out of the gate with the requisite intensity. They slammed the accelerator. From top to bottom, nearly everyone was starring in their roles. Heck, Dončić even looked good when he took on the Herculean task of guarding Wembanyama in moments.

But the Lakers ran out of gas. They sputtered. They couldn't sustain that for 48 minutes.

Overall, the Lakers should be proud of themselves. This proved they can compete with the best teams in the league. They are contenders.

Victor Wembanyama looks on during the Spurs' home win over the Lakers. NBAE via Getty Images

Said Lakers coach JJ Redick: "I think the spirit of our group was awesome."

Added Dončić: "I think, you know, we could obviously win this game. I don't think we played great and we were there. One-possession game, away game against San Antonio, which is a great team."

If that was a preview of the Western Conference Finals, can it be May yet?

If that was what happens when Wembanyama and Dončić go head to head, can we figure out a way to add a one-on-one showcase to All-Star weekend?

This was fun. This was theater. This was thrilling.

The Lakers have officially arrived on the scene.

They lost but they won the respect of the NBA.

#2716. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2715. He fought the Education Department — and won back his student loan forgiveness credit. What other borrowers can learn

Earlier this year, Nicholas Yund was months away from having his federal student loans forgiven. Then, in early August, the University of Minnesota employee logged into his account and saw his forgiveness date pushed back by nearly two years.

"I could barely breathe," said Yund, 45. "That's when I went online to various Facebook and Reddit groups and saw it was happening to thousands of other people."

Yund's qualifying-payment count fell from 116 to 93, leaving him 27 payments short of the 120 required for Public Service Loan Forgiveness, a federal program created in 2007 that cancels the remaining student loan debt of borrowers working for a government or nonprofit employer.

The reduced payment counts for Yund and other PSLF borrowers result from what the U.S. Department of Education has said is an effort to correct payment-count errors made during the Biden administration.

"These errors resulted in inaccurate payment counts for some borrowers," a department spokesperson wrote to CNBC in an email. "The Department remains committed to ensuring that every qualifying payment is properly credited to a borrower's account."

The sudden reductions in payment counts have left borrowers scrambling to determine which months were removed and whether their current timelines are correct.

The American Federation of Teachers recently sued the Trump administration over its management of the PSLF program, claiming that it is unlawfully blocking tens of thousands of borrowers from student loan forgiveness. Filed Oct. 30, the suit is still pending.

For borrowers nearing the finish line, losing qualifying months can mean staying in repayment — and in qualifying public service work — for years longer than expected. It can also delay other financial goals.

Yund said a longer wait for student loan forgiveness would have kept him from putting more money toward retirement savings and enjoying more disposable income. His current student loan balance is around $20,000.

But in the end, he found a way to get his qualifying PSLF payments restored. Here's how.

When Yund saw his PSLF count drop, he called his loan servicer, Missouri Higher Education Loan Authority, or MOHELA, and asked what had happened. The answer offered little reassurance.

"The agent said they had received lots of calls regarding that issue," Yund said. He said he was told there had been a technical problem but got no explanation of what it meant for his account or when it might be fixed.

A MOHELA spokesperson referred CNBC's questions to the Education Department, which did not respond to questions about Yund's case.

Yund began looking for answers elsewhere.

That month, he opened cases with the Minnesota Attorney General's office and Democratic Sen. Amy Klobuchar's office. He also had something that could help make his case: a complete record of his payments.

"I kept bank statement records of every payment made as well as downloaded all of my payment history from my student loan servicers," Yund said.

Brian Evans, press secretary at the Minnesota Attorney General's office, said it referred Yund's complaint to the state's Student Loan Advocate, who, he said, "is in a better position to help resolve his complaint."

Klobuchar's office told him it had opened a congressional inquiry with the Education Department on his behalf, according to communications between the senator's staff and Yund reviewed by CNBC.

"Senator Klobuchar's office was very helpful and timely, reaching out to me directly by phone with additional questions and concerns," Yund said.

Those communications show the Education Department responded to the senator's office, saying that its review found that some of Yund's payments did not qualify under standard PSLF.

But the department said that he could receive credit for that period through Temporary Expanded Public Service Loan Forgiveness, or TEPSLF. That program can count payments made under certain repayment plans that are not usually eligible for standard PSLF, provided the borrower meets additional requirements, such as working in a qualifying public service position.

On Sept. 6, Yund logged into his account again. Most of his payments had been restored, but he was still two payments short of his earlier count.

"I was incredibly frustrated with the lack of oversight and errors demonstrated by the Department of Education," Yund said.

But he also saw a reason to keep pushing: "This was an opportunity to advocate for myself and, by extension, draw attention to an important problem we are facing."

His advice to other student loan borrowers? "Be persistent to the point of annoying," he said. "Call your Senators and House Representatives to open cases on your behalf. Do not give up hope."

Yund is once again nearing debt forgiveness. He had expected it in November; now he anticipates it in January or February.

He already knows how he'll celebrate when he's student loan-free: dinner at Meritage, a popular French brasserie in St. Paul. He knows what he'll order, too. He's had plenty of time to think about it: the crispy half chicken.
