#2714. Behind Enemy Lines: Seahawks vs. 49ers Week 12 preview

The Seattle Seahawks season is approaching a critical point. A win against the San Francisco 49ers would keep the Seahawks alive in the NFC West race, but a loss would make the division title an extremely difficult proposition and leave the Wild Card as their primary route to the postseason.

Even that path is becoming increasingly complicated. With the NFC North teams putting together strong seasons, Seattle could need 12 wins to have a realistic shot at a Wild Card spot. And the schedule offers little room for recovery, with two games against the Los Angeles Rams, along with the New York Giants, Dallas Cowboys, Philadelphia Eagles and Carolina Panthers still ahead.

There are no Raiders or Cardinals-type games left on Seattle's schedule. The Rams remain a difficult division opponent, the Giants have shown they can be disruptive defensively, Dallas has an explosive offense despite its defensive struggles, Philadelphia has a talented defense and surprised everyone with the victory on Thanksgiving, and Carolina is having a competitive season with Bryce Young while fighting for the NFC South. Seattle already lost to Washington with its backup quarterback, another reminder that there are no guarantees left on this schedule.

Seattle cannot afford to spend time looking at the standings or calculating playoff scenarios. The Seahawks need to win this game first. Then they can worry about the next one.

With Brock Purdy ruled out, Mac Jones will take over at quarterback for San Francisco. There is some reason for optimism when looking at Mike Macdonald's history against backup quarterbacks. In 2024, Seattle went 2-0 against teams starting Skylar Thompson and Jimmy Garoppolo, while in 2025 the Seahawks also defeated Jacoby Brissett, who had taken over for an injured Kyler Murray, and Max Brosmer. However, that history is not a guarantee of success. Seattle already lost to Washington with Marcus Mariota starting in place of Jayden Daniels this season, showing that facing a backup doesn't automatically make the matchup easier.

Jones is also far from an inexperienced backup being thrown into an unfamiliar situation. He went 5-3 as a starter for San Francisco in 2025, throwing for 2,151 yards, 13 touchdowns and six interceptions. He has already demonstrated that he can operate Shanahan's offense and keep the 49ers competitive without Purdy. The drop in individual talent is real, but Seattle shouldn't expect the entire offensive operation to suffer because of it.

Perhaps the bigger question is San Francisco's offensive line, particularly at right tackle. Colton McKivitz suffered a season-ending ankle injury against Minnesota and was replaced by Vederian Lowe, who will now take over as the starter. Lowe was originally brought in as a swing tackle, and Seattle should look to test him with its pass rush, especially if it can create favorable one-on-one situations.

Still, Shanahan's offensive structure should remain largely unchanged. His system is built around creating favorable matchups, using pre-snap motion and giving quarterbacks defined reads. Expect San Francisco to lean heavily on short passes and quick throws, allowing Jones to get the ball out before Seattle's pass rush has time to reach him. That approach can limit the impact of the quarterback's individual limitations while keeping the offense on schedule.

Seattle will need to be disciplined. Attacking Lowe is important, but the defense also has to be ready for an offense designed to neutralize pressure with quick decisions. Purdy's absence is certainly significant, but between Jones' previous success as a 49ers starter and Shanahan's ability to create favorable situations, the Seahawks have plenty of reasons to take this matchup seriously.

Seattle was competitive in its previous matchup with San Francisco despite committing multiple turnovers, including two interceptions from Sam Darnold. The Seahawks were still in position to potentially force overtime before the controversial ending, but the mistakes made their comeback much more difficult.

Darnold has thrown nine interceptions in seven games, averaging 1.29 interceptions per game. He is tied for sixth in total interceptions, while his interceptions-per-game rate is the highest among quarterbacks who have played at least seven games. Daniel Jones leads the league in total interceptions with 13, but Darnold's rate is even more concerning.

The numbers matter even more when looking at Darnold's rankings across the league. His interception total places him among the league leaders, and his interceptions-per-game rate is even more concerning when accounting for the games he has missed.

Seattle doesn't need Darnold to win the game on his own. It needs him to protect the football, avoid unnecessary risks and keep the offense from giving San Francisco short fields. Against the 49ers, turnovers could be the difference between a competitive game and a difficult afternoon.

Seattle needs to run the football to take pressure off Darnold, but San Francisco presents a difficult matchup up front.

The interior of the 49ers' defensive line has been effective against the run, making it difficult to consistently gain yards between the tackles. Trying to attack the edges isn't necessarily an easy alternative, either, with Fred Warner and Dre Greenlaw capable of pursuing the ball and closing space quickly.

Seattle has been more effective running between the tackles, including with Zach Charbonnet, so abandoning the middle of the field would be counterproductive. But the Seahawks will need to be smart about how they attack, mixing their concepts and finding ways to create favorable blocking situations instead of repeatedly running into San Francisco's strength.

The ground game is important for more than just gaining yards. If Seattle can establish it, the offense can control the pace, keep the defense honest and reduce the number of obvious passing situations Darnold has to face.

San Francisco's pass rush hasn't quite lived up to its reputation in terms of sack production. The 49ers have recorded 17 sacks, placing them near the bottom of the league, but that doesn't mean Seattle can afford to take the matchup lightly.

Osa Odighizuwa has been an important addition to the defensive front, providing another disruptive presence on the interior. And Nick Bosa is still Nick Bosa. If he hasn't been producing sacks at the level expected, that only makes him more dangerous heading into a matchup against Darnold.

However, the previous matchup should be a warning for Seattle. The 49ers recorded five sacks against the Seahawks, including four in the first half, and Darnold was brought down five times. Odighizuwa was among those who got to the quarterback, while San Francisco's defensive front consistently disrupted Seattle's protection.

Seattle needs to protect the interior, avoid obvious passing situations and prevent Bosa and Odighizuwa from taking over the game. The season sack numbers may not be impressive, but the 49ers have already shown what they can do against this offensive line.

Mike Evans dominated Josh Jobe in their previous matchup, winning in just about every way imaginable. He used his strength and physicality, beat him with speed, won through technique, created separation inside and outside, and made plays downfield.

Evans finished with seven catches for 113 yards and a touchdown, including a 28-yard reception that highlighted just how difficult the matchup was for Jobe.

Mike Macdonald has explained the trade-offs involved in providing additional help to Evans, as doubling him can create problems elsewhere in the defense. But Seattle cannot simply repeat the same approach and expect a different result.

The problem is even bigger now with Keenan Allen also in the picture. Allen is another experienced receiver capable of finding openings and exploiting coverage mistakes, and he has already shown a good connection with Brock Purdy despite having limited time with the team.

Seattle needs to find a way to make Evans' life more difficult without leaving Allen free to take advantage of the attention elsewhere. Jobe cannot be repeatedly left on an island against Evans and expected to win those matchups.

Christian McCaffrey is a major part of the problem, but the bigger question for Seattle is how aggressively it wants to defend the run without compromising the rest of the defense.

Against the Raiders, Seattle was extremely aggressive in trying to stop the ground game, even against an offense that had struggled to run the ball. That approach worked, but San Francisco presents an entirely different challenge. The 49ers have a legitimate running threat in McCaffrey, who can hurt defenses both between the tackles and as a receiver out of the backfield.

McCaffrey doesn't need much space to turn a routine play into an explosive gain. If Seattle's linebackers become too aggressive trying to stop the run, San Francisco can take advantage of the space behind them, particularly in the intermediate areas of the field.

This is where Mike Macdonald may need to find a balance. His defense has been excellent, and there is no reason to abandon its core principles. But the Bears and Commanders games showed that Seattle can struggle when an opponent finds ways to exploit its defensive approach. Macdonald may need to be more willing to adjust his aggressiveness and coverage responsibilities depending on the opponent.

With Mac Jones starting, Seattle might be tempted to commit more resources to stopping McCaffrey and forcing the backup quarterback to win through the air. That could be a mistake. Shanahan's system is particularly effective at creating favorable passing situations, and Jones doesn't need to play like Purdy to take advantage of openings created by the offense.

Containing McCaffrey while protecting the middle of the field will be one of Seattle's biggest challenges. The Seahawks cannot allow their commitment to stopping the run to become an opportunity for Jones to attack the spaces behind the linebackers.

This is a game that perhaps would have been better positioned for Seattle later in the season, similar to last year's matchup against the Rams, when the Seahawks had an opportunity to make a statement at a critical point in the playoff race. Instead, Seattle is facing San Francisco now, with very little room left for mistakes.

Purdy's absence certainly improves Seattle's chances, but it doesn't change the fact that the 49ers are a well-coached team with an offense capable of making life difficult for opposing defenses. Mac Jones doesn't have the same individual talent as Purdy, but Shanahan's system can still make the offense work efficiently under Jones.

#2713. New car prices are at a record high. Here's where to look for deals

Consumers who are in the market for a new car may find the latest pricing data somewhat sobering.

The average transaction price for a new vehicle reached an all-time high of $51,109 in October, up 2.1% from a year earlier, according to a report from Kelley Blue Book, an auto valuation company. The amount excludes incentives, taxes and fees.

But for consumers who can't spend that much — or don't want to — the good news is that plenty of buyers are shelling out much less for a vehicle.

"The average is telling us as much about product mix as it is about affordability," Erin Keating, executive analyst at Cox Automotive, which owns Kelley Blue Book, said in the report.

Of the five highest-volume car types sold in October, three — compact and subcompact SUVs, and compact cars — sold at prices far below the overall average transaction price, she said. For example, the average amount paid for a compact car in October was $28,030.

Additionally, sales of vehicles priced from about $28,000 to $38,000 climbed 9.6% from a year earlier.

"Consumers are still buying plenty of vehicles well below the headline average transaction price, even as strong demand for full-size pickups and other larger, well-equipped vehicles keeps pushing the average to new highs," Keating said.

The record price comes amid a 34% jump in the average cost of a new vehicle since October 2019, when it was $38,259, according to Kelley Blue Book data.

While pandemic-related supply shortages helped drive up prices earlier this decade, consumers' appetite for larger, more expensive vehicles like SUVs and pickup trucks has also pushed the average higher. Full-size pickups, for instance, sold for an average of $67,912 in October, according to Kelley Blue Book.

Yet buyers who would struggle to afford a $50,000-plus new car can still spend less and find deals in the current market.

For starters, automakers have been slower than usual to roll out 2027 models, at least in part because 2026 cars continue to sell well at current prices, said Ivan Drury, director of insights at Edmunds, an automotive research company. In October, 76% of new-vehicle sales were for current-year models, compared with 59% a year earlier.

This slowdown in the annual model-year transition means more 2027 cars will hit dealer lots soon, which suggests dealers and automakers are motivated to clear 2026 inventory, Drury said.

But waiting until the last week of the year to buy — thinking dealerships are desperate to make the sale — won't necessarily get you a better deal, he said.

"You're not going to get massive savings by waiting," Drury said. "Everybody's busiest time of the year is between Christmas and New Year's, but it's not like the best deals are then — that's a perception thing."

Some manufacturers are offering incentives on certain 2026 models, which can reduce the cost of a new car by thousands of dollars.

For example, the 2026 Nissan Pathfinder, a midsize SUV with a starting price of $41,565 — which includes destination charges — comes with a rebate of $5,000 for qualified buyers through Nov. 30, according to Cars.com, an online automotive marketplace.

Just be sure to check your local dealerships to confirm participation for any manufacturer's incentives, experts say.

Look for cars that have been sitting on dealer lots for an extended time, Drury said. In September, new cars took 58 days to sell, on average, according to Edmunds.

Generally, dealerships consider cars unsold for 60 to 90 days to be aging inventory, which may motivate them to offer a better deal, according to Autotrader.com, an online auto marketplace owned by Cox Automotive.

"You'll find the vehicles that dealers definitely want to get rid of the most," Drury said. Looking for older inventory will be "your quickest way to find a dealership that's thinking, 'Yes, I too want to get rid of this. Help me help you,'" he said.

Listings on some car-shopping websites include how long a car has been on a dealership's lot. You can also ask the dealership how long a particular car has been in its inventory.

Leasing may present an opportunity to get into a car with lower monthly payments than financing the same vehicle, depending on the terms of the lease.

However, fewer attractive leasing deals are available, a trend that started during the pandemic, Drury said. Plus, leases, which are typically for three years, usually come with mileage limits and charges for excessive wear and tear.

Nevertheless, leasing may make financial sense for a consumer who "gets that two- or three-year itch for a new car," Drury said.

This is because after the first two or three years of making payments on a car loan — a time period when the steepest depreciation generally occurs — the car's value may be less than what is still owed on the loan, depending on the specific terms. This is known as being upside down or underwater, or having negative equity. In that case, the amount must either be paid off or added to the buyer's new loan.

"We see so many cars traded in that are 3 years old, and the customer is upside down by $7,000, $8,000 or $10,000 and they roll that into another loan," Drury said.

You also may be able to find a good deal on a used car that's not too old.

The average transaction price for a 3-year-old vehicle was $32,039 in the third quarter, down slightly from $32,461 in the second quarter but 3.1% higher than $31,067 a year earlier, according to Edmunds. It's also higher than the average $27,133 list price for used cars of any age in October, according to Cox.

However, these 3-year-old cars are selling for far less than they did when they were new — $16,562 below the original sticker price of $48,601, on average. That marks the largest dollar gap Edmunds has on record, surpassing the previous high of $16,252 in the last quarter of 2019.

Some 3-year-old models tend to take longer to sell than others, which may give buyers some negotiating power, according to Edmunds. For example, a 3-year-old Jeep Cherokee, with an average transaction price of $23,138 — compared with its original price of $42,263 — takes an average of 64 days to sell, according to Edmunds.

Additionally, a good credit score can help keep the cost of financing a car purchase down. The higher your score, the lower the interest rate you can get, generally speaking.

And, finally, if you're trading in a car, be sure to shop it around, Drury said.

"Get multiple offers. They can vary by thousands of dollars," Drury said. "Have those offers in hand when you're at the dealership that has the next car you want to buy."

#2712. Needing Neemias Queta

Establishing a personal best should be the cherry on top of a win. Unfortunately, that just wasn't the case for Neemias Queta on Friday night.

With a chance to win the East's Group C in the Play-In Tournament, Queta grabbed a career-high 22 rebounds in his 13th straight start this season against the Hawks. His six off the offensive glass contributed to Boston outscoring Atlanta 18-6 in second chance points and his three stocks help hold the Hawks to just 87 points. However, in a one-point loss, Neemias Queta (and his teammates) were a -2 and that's all that'll matter at the end of the day.

When the season started, it was Mitchell Robinson (3 years, $47 million) who got the initial call at center. It made sense that the Celtics' big free agent signing would get that nod. But Queta signed a similarly sized extension as well (4 years, $56 million) and despite Robinson's positive impact with the opening five with a +19.5 net rating, it's been the 27-year-old entering his third year in Boston that has been the stabilizing force at what head coach Joe Mazzulla has called the most important position.

"The most efficient shots are at the rim, so on offense or defense, they gotta go through you," Queta said at shootaround. "So being able to get as many easy looks at the rim offensively, whether it's through screening, whether it's me getting the dump down, whether it's me being an offensive rebounding guy and make those shots. Defensively, deterring people out there impacts a lot of the game."

Robinson has been out of the lineup since the start of the road trip in Denver as he recovers from COVID. In his absence, Queta has continued his strong play despite the losses. To date, he's second to Paul George in plus-minus at +160 for the year and that success has earned him more playing time. His 33 minutes against the Hawks was the most he's played all year, but that's also come with more scrutiny.

"I've got a little added responsibility, added trust," Queta said. "Obviously, as the years go by, I think I'm entering my prime and I'm more confident and more comfortable out there."

Unfortunately, the Hawks disruptive defense forced Queta to another career-high: seven turnovers.

"Whether it's them speeding me up, whether it's me not reading the game, not being patient, not sure," Queta said. "I can't recall the last time I had this many turnovers in a game. I'm not worried, but it's definitely something to be concerned about and fixed."

On Sunday, Queta will face his biggest test yet: Victor Wembanyama. With the Celtics losing five of their last six games, a win in San Antonio could be the switch flip the team is looking for. For the always improving Queta, it's just another game in his development success story.

"Having last year's ceiling being this year's floor, I think I'm in a good spot. I'm just trying to build off that."

#2711. The doctor, the patient and the smartwatch: What physicians think of the third voice at your appointment

A health-tracking device quite literally saved the life of one of Dr. Linda E. Tepper's patients, she says.

After a heavy snowstorm, the 89-year-old man, who has metastatic prostate cancer, slipped and fell while clearing his backyard with a snow blower, says Tepper, a New York City-based internist at NYU Langone Health. He fractured three bones in his face, had a subdural hematoma and was unconscious in the snow.

"His Apple Watch detected it and called the police, the ambulance and his daughter," Tepper says, adding: "He would have died out there."

Wearable health trackers — devices that can measure health metrics ranging from heart rate to sleep and menstrual cycles — are a big business these days. Whoop and Oura, which both sell health wearables, have both announced valuations of more than $10 billion since October 2025. Apple, Google, Samsung and Garmin each sell their own fitness-tracking products, as part of an estimated $71 billion market in 2025.

And big business often draws skepticism from some consumers, particularly when health is involved. These devices can cost hundreds of dollars. Some require additional monthly subscriptions to analyze all the data they collect. Their accuracy has been questioned in various class-action lawsuits over the past 10-plus years: For example, Oura was sued in August for allegedly inaccurate sleep-stage tracking, a claim the company disputes.

So as more devices appear on Americans' wrists and biceps, you might wonder: What do actual doctors think of them? In some cases, doctors say wearable health trackers like Oura rings, Whoop bands and Fitbits can help them do their jobs more efficiently. In other cases, inaccurate data points can make patients unnecessarily anxious and present challenges for medical professionals.

Either way, doctors should get used to interpreting the data they provide, especially as they get "cheaper, more readily accessible, more readily available," says Dr. Neha Vyas, a family physician at Cleveland Clinic.

Vyas likes when patients ask her health questions prompted by the data from their wearables, she says. "They actually start a conversation," she says. "If we take the information they have and put it in their clinical picture, I can oftentimes guide them."

The tech can be beneficial for patients with heart issues, or who want to make sustainable lifestyle changes that could be better supported by using a step tracker or calorie intake tracker, says Vyas. "I only have one data point when I'm in the office. But if I can see what's been going on with someone, whether it's their heart rate, their steps [or] their calorie intake, I'm able to really get a lot of information," she says. "We can really make a profound difference in people's lives for the better."

Tepper says wearables like Apple Watches are particularly helpful for people with a high risk of falling and injuring themselves. Some of them can even be worn in the shower, she notes. "I think they're remarkable for that," she says.

On other occasions, devices worn by Tepper's patients have detected atrial fibrillation, an irregular heartbeat that can lead to blood clots, she says: "It was real, and we were able to treat them." Just about anyone can develop atrial fibrillation, according to the American Heart Association, though the risk increases with age and conditions like underlying heart disease or high blood pressure.

Overall, Vyas and Tepper agree: Wearable health-tracking devices can be helpful and, in some cases, even life-saving.

Health-tracking devices can produce a lot of data. Too much, sometimes, says Vyas: "How do we take all this data and actually make it actionable?"

Some data points that concern patients don't require action, but that doesn't always allay the patients' worries, Tepper says. She highlights sleep tracking as a common example: Some of her patients started taking sleeping pills unnecessarily because they were nervous about their sleep scores, she says. Some sleeping pills can create dependencies and introduce or exacerbate sleep disorders, including some that are potentially life-threatening, according to the Cleveland Clinic.

"I don't want everybody on a sleeping pill," says Tepper.

Sometimes, Vyas hears from patients at odd hours of the night because they're anxious about sleep alerts from their fitness trackers, she says. The devices can "create that cycle of health anxiety where they monitor, they get anxious, they need repeated reassurance, and then they keep monitoring just because it's something that's so readily available to them now," she says. "I do have to remember to change the conversation from, 'Hey, what does that data point tell us?' to 'How are you feeling? What are you feeling right now?'"

If you receive an alert or review some data from your health tech that you find alarming, your first step should be to gauge how your body feels, regardless of what the device told you, Vyas recommends. Then, if you're still worried, present your concern to your doctor before acting on it yourself, she suggests. Always consult with your medical professional before making any major changes that can affect your health.

"Just as with other forms of technology, it's not going away," Vyas says. Doctors aren't going away either, she notes.

#2709. Saudi Arabia shifts Vision 2030 spending amid economic and security pressure

Saudi Arabia's economic diversification strategy has moved from "spending and delivery at any cost" to "spending and delivery at the right cost," Economy Minister Faisal Alibrahim told CNBC on Friday.

It comes after the Kingdom scaled back or cut several flagship projects as it endures a sharp economic contraction.

Alibrahim also said Saudi Arabia would "stop at nothing from realizing long-term sustainable stability in the region" amid ongoing Houthi strikes on civilian and energy infrastructure that have killed and injured dozens and repeatedly rocked oil markets.

"The phase we're in today is one where every riyal is deployed with greater precision... it was clear that while we want to diversify or restructure our economy to depend less on government spend, there was a lot of catch-up that needed to be done, and for that, you saw government spend leading the way," Alibrahim told CNBC's Dan Murphy.

"Right now, that is shifting from spending and delivery at any cost to spending and delivering at the right cost. We're moving towards a policy-first approach to many of our policy areas and... objectives. We're also creating a clearer business environment, one that aligns the regulatory environment in the Kingdom with international laws."

That includes the recent announcement of a 15% minimum effective tax rate on multinational profits, which Alibrahim said would "create a clearer environment for the private sector to operate," as the government looks to attract more external investment.

Saudi Arabia has been on a mammoth capital spending spree since it launched its Vision 2030 plan to reduce its dependence on oil revenue in 2016, deploying the resources of its sovereign wealth fund — the Public Investment Fund, or PIF.

But more recently, it has reassessed the cost and commercial prospects of initiatives such as the new economic region NEOM and the futuristic "The Line" city, withdrawn funding from the LIV Golf tournament and halted construction on a planned giant skyscraper in Riyadh. In January, it said it would postpone hosting the 2029 Asian Winter Games, which had been scheduled for Trojena, a planned part of NEOM.

Saudi Arabia is meanwhile grappling with the destabilizing effects of ongoing strikes by Iran-backed Houthi militants in Yemen, which have hit targets including the PIF tower, Riyadh Airport and the country's largest oil refinery. The danger forced the Kingdom to call off its big international investor conference, dubbed "Davos in the Desert," which was due to be attended by some of the biggest figures in global finance in November.

"The human cost of crisis has been real, and so has been the economic cost," Alibrahim told CNBC.

Alibrahim said infrastructure investments had allowed the Kingdom's energy sector to restore production to near pre-crisis levels. It has meanwhile moved its import and export hub from the east to the west of the country via land-based transport due to the airport strikes, which has restored cargo levels.

He added that the attacks highlight the importance of diversification to the Saudi economy.

Real GDP contracted by 3.8% in the third quarter, according to government figures, with oil activities down 20.4% year-on-year partially offset by a 1.9% rise in non-oil activities. Net foreign direct investment inflows dropped 19.5% year-on-year in the second quarter.

The Saudi Ministry of Finance expects an overall contraction of around 2.1% this year and a rebound of 10.9% next year.

Advisory group Oxford Economics forecasts a more negative outlook. It sees the Saudi economy shrinking 3.4% this year and by 4% in 2027, amid escalating attacks on energy infrastructure even as a new minimum wage and continued Vision 2030 spending are expected to support incomes and domestic demand.

Akanksha Samdani, lead economist at Oxford Economics, said Vision 2030 was entering a new phase focused on returns.

"The government is managing fiscal pressures and shifting budgetary spending towards essential services and strategic priorities, while PIF is becoming more selective in its capital allocation and seeking greater private-sector participation," Samdani said, adding that some giga projects are being scaled back or delayed, while investment continues in sectors such as manufacturing, logistics, tourism, mining and technology.

"Our forecasts already assume that some of the Vision 2030 targets will be achieved later than originally planned. But the overarching goal of economic diversification remains unchanged," Samdani told CNBC by email.

Neil Quilliam, managing director of consultancy Azure Strategy, said investors are assessing whether Houthi attacks demonstrate a persistent vulnerability that could increase the cost and complexity of delivering Vision 2030.

"They have repeatedly demonstrated an ability to target important economic infrastructure and expose weaknesses in Saudi air defences," he told CNBC, with the attacks challenging the "assumption that Saudi Arabia has insulated itself from regional instability."

The economic impact of the strikes will be less about physical damage and more about whether they affect confidence in Saudi Arabia as a destination for tourism, entertainment and sports, a key part of Vision 2030 and a source of foreign capital and expatriate talent, he added.

#2708. Highlights: Stephon Castle leads Spurs in second-half comeback win over the Lakers

Down 54-38 at halftime, the San Antonio Spurs looked outmatched and outclassed by the Los Angeles Lakers. Just 24 minutes later, the team completed its most impressive comeback win. The Spurs outscored the Lakers 61 to 42 in the second half to win 99-96.

The Area 51 duo of Stephon Castle and Victor Wembanyama led the way. Castle led the Spurs in scoring with 26 points and 9 assists while shooting an efficient 10-of-15.

Castle was red hot from three, shooting 4-for-7 from deep. When the Lakers sagged off him, he hit pull-up threes. This shot came when the Spurs were just starting their second-half comeback. Steals were a major part of the run. San Antonio had 11 total steals and forced 17 Lakers turnovers. Castle does a good job here of jumping the passing lane and hitting the open shot.

Castle also made some strong plays in transition. This one will get more attention for Luke Doncic's near steal. Julian Champagnie should get a lot of credit for threading the needle on this touchdown pass.

In the first half, the Spurs showed what would make them so successful in the second – turning defense into offense. Castle recognizes the steal here immediately, sprints out, and gets a high-flying dunk on the other end.

Castle did a decent job of initiating offense, tallying 9 assists. He also had 6 of the Spurs' 19 turnovers. On this play, he showed the great lob chemistry he has with Wembanyama. Even through some contact, Wembanyama was able to get to a perfectly placed pass for an alley-oop layup.

Wembanyama didn't have nearly as many open runways to the rim for dunks as he has in previous games. Los Angeles did a great job of tagging him and bumping him off his spot as he tried to roll to the basket. Still, Wembanyama was able to put up 25 points, 16 rebounds, 4 steals, and 2 blocks in the win.

Wembanyama really had to fight on the interior against the tough Lakers center, Walker Kessler. Wembanyama ultimately won the matchup, thanks in large part to his activity on the offensive glass. Wemby had 7 offensive boards, including this one in which he grabbed his own missed shot and slammed it home.

Near the end of the game, San Antonio started isolation Wembanyama in the high post, letting him go one-on-one with Kessler. The Spurs got great results out of the actions – whether it be a Wembanyama jumper, a pass to an open guard, or a drive straight to the rim for a reverse dunk.

This play is a great example of how to take advantage of Wembanyama's gravity. As he brings it up the floor, all three Lakers defenders at the top of the key are watching him. Devin Vassell sees this and streaks to the basket for the lob finish.

The win wouldn't have been possible without Wembanyama's defensive effort. He forced the Lakers to take tough shots on the perimeter and in the mid-range. This block on Collin Sexton was helped by some tough defense from De'Aaron Fox, who funneled the ball-handler right to Wembanyama.

Fox came through for the Spurs when they needed him in the second half. He had 12 points, including some clutch free throws at the end of the game. He has been at his best when he is getting into the paint and finishing over defenders, or finding the big men for dump-offs.

San Antonio got a bump from the return of Dylan Harper, who had 14 points in 24 minutes. Having Harper come off the bench gave the Spurs a jolt in their second unit and helped them play more balanced lineups throughout the game. It doesn't look like Harper has lost a step since his groin injury.

San Antonio's bench played a pivotal role in the win. Keldon Johnson hit this game-tying three-pointer, his only basket of the game. Carter Bryant played excellent defense on Doncic and knocked down an awesome pull-up three-pointer in the midst of the Spurs' run.

With the win, the Spurs are moving on to the Knockout Rounds of the NBA Cup as the Western Conference Wild Card team. They will face off with the Dallas Mavericks on December 4th.

#2707. Trump demands probe into 'record setting error' after third-quarter GDP data gets massive revision higher

President Donald Trump on Thursday falsely deemed the latest gross domestic product revision a mistake, claiming that it should be "immediately investigated."

Data released Wednesday from the Commerce Department revealed that the U.S. economy exhibited much stronger growth for the third quarter than initially estimated, showing that real GDP grew at a 4.3% annualized rate. That was revised upward from the department's advance estimate of 3.4%.

That revision was a "RECORD SETTING ERROR," Trump said in a Truth Social post, suggesting that the initial release could have influenced the outcome of the 2026 U.S. midterm elections, where Democrats flipped both chambers of Congress and multiple governorships.

"I would like to wish a Happy Thanksgiving to even those who issued fake Economic Numbers, just prior to the Election, and corrected them yesterday, three weeks late, showing that the GDP was actually 4.3%, versus 3.4%, A RECORD SETTING ERROR, and one so grave that it should be immediately investigated," Trump wrote.

However, the difference between the advance estimate and the figure in the second release is far from being an all-time record. It was the largest revision since the 1.2 percentage point revision seen in the second quarter of 2020.

On an absolute basis, the latest revision was tied for 16th among all revisions from the advance estimate to the second release since 1990. It also tied four other quarters with a similar 0.9 percentage point difference — and was approximately double the average revision of 0.43 percentage points for all 147 quarters.

The upward revision shows Trump's policies are "delivering results" that defy expectations, White House spokesman Matthew Middleton told CNBC.

Revisions to U.S. economic numbers are not out of the ordinary. Initial reports count on partial survey data and early estimates. That data is later updated with more survey responses — which sometimes leads to changes from the initial reports.

GDP data is revised repeatedly over a five-year period. By the time the final revision is done, the average absolute error from the second release to the final is 1.14 percentage points.

The advance estimate for Q3 U.S. GDP, which came just days before Election Day in early November, was still better than the figure economists had expected at the time. It also showed a significant step-up in growth from the second quarter.

Because that was a strong showing for the economy, some on Wall Street consider this week's revision to be a confirmation of the economy's recent growth trend.

Jim Baird of Plante Moran Financial Advisors said immediately following the release that it "reinforces an increasingly familiar tension" that the economy is "performing quite well, but its resilience also gives the Fed an even stronger case for further rate hikes."

"The economy isn't merely hanging in there. It continues to demonstrate considerable resilience, with consumers spending, businesses investing and few signs that restrictive monetary policy is creating significant cracks in the broader expansion," the firm's chief investment officer said.

Traders are currently pricing in a nearly 100% chance of a hike at the Federal Open Market Committee's December meeting, according to the CME FedWatch tool. They're also anticipating an additional hike in January.

— CNBC's Jeff Cox and Fred Imbert contributed reporting.

#2706. The S&P 500 is in a correction. Bear markets and recessions don't always follow

The S&P 500 fell into a correction on Nov. 20 for the first time since April 2025 as stocks pulled back sharply from October's record highs.

The market correction, defined as a 10% decline from a recent high, came as investor concern mounted over a potential slowdown in AI spending, mega-cap tech valuations and the path of Federal Reserve interest rate policy. The S&P 500 fell 3.7% on Nov. 20 alone, closing at 6,910.39, its seventh consecutive daily decline. That left the index 11.6% below the Oct. 6 closing high of 7,818.93.

A correction can deepen into a bear market, defined as a drop of 20% or more from the most recent top. But history shows that even a bear market, let alone a correction, doesn't automatically mean the economy is destined for a recession.

In a CNBC analysis, eight of the 13 S&P 500 bear markets overlapped with recessions, as defined by the National Bureau of Economic Research, and five did not. The NBER says a recession is "a significant decline in economic activity that is spread across the economy and lasts more than a few months."

The 1973-74 oil shock, the dot-com bust in 2000, the 2007-09 financial crisis and the 2020 pandemic downturn all overlapped with recessions. But some major bear markets also occurred without one, including in 1987 and 2022.

Deutsche Bank macro strategist Henry Allen said that every S&P 500 bear market since the 1962 "Kennedy Slide" has coincided with at least one of three factors: a recession, worsening economic data raising recession fears, or a cycle of Federal Reserve rate hikes. None of those are present today, at least so far.

"Much as last week's selloff was severe, it's worth remembering that it has several differences with many of the other big risk-off moves in recent times," the London-based strategist wrote on Tuesday.

Allen said there hasn't been any sudden reassessment of the growth outlook recently, which happened during the Covid-19 shock, when the pandemic pushed the global economy into recession and the S&P 500 plunged 33.9% in just 33 days. Nor is the current situation similar to the 2025 "Liberation Day" tariff period either, which also prompted a reassessment of the growth outlook.

Economic numbers aren't deteriorating either, with the November U.S. flash composite Purchasing Managers' Index, or PMI, at 54.6, above 50 and indicating that business activity continued to expand. Another factor that often coincides with a recession, Allen said, was a big hawkish shift from the central banks, seen in the 1966 and 2022 bear markets.

"Today however, the equity declines have not coincided with a hawkish repricing," despite widespread expectations that the central bank is set to raise rates again at its final meeting of the year, on Dec. 9, Allen said.

Today, markets are pricing fewer Fed rate hikes through September 2027 than they were on Oct. 6, when the S&P 500 set its record, Allen said. "If anything, pricing has shifted slightly dovishly with the equity declines."

Over more than six decades since 1962, bear markets have always coincided with at least one of those three circumstances, according to Allen.

"So it would be unusual to see one today without those" happening too, he said.

I think we can be better, just watch the film and figure it out."

The Celtics have been tuning the ball over a lot recently, too. Mazzulla took the blame for that after the game. "That's something for me to go back and watch and figure out how I can put our guys in better positions. (Forcing turnovers) is something Atlanta does very well, just our playcalling wasn't right. We didn't put them in position and spacing to make the right reads so that one is on me."

The Celtics had the most turnovers they've had in the Joe Mazzulla era with 26 in the loss.

"A little of it is a stylistic change to the way we are playing," Mazzulla said. "Just with some off ball and moving a lot more and a little bit of unpredictability with the way we are playing."

Neemias Queta had seven turnovers on Friday night, the most of his career. He was asked after the game on what caused him to have that many turnovers. "I'm not sure. Whether it was them speeding me up, whether it was me not reading the game, not being patient, I'm not sure. I can't recall the last time I had this many turnovers in a game. Not worried, but definitely something to be concerned about and fix."

The turnovers have just compounded Boston's cold shooting streak. They need guys like White, Pritchard, Tatum, Baylor Scheierman and Hugo Gonzalez to make their threes. They've made them in the first half, but in the second half, those shots have not gone in.

I don't think it is time to panic. The defense remains elite and shots should eventually start to fall again. However, that doesn't mean you can't be frustrated or even worried. The Celtics have lost 5 of their last 6 games and that is cause for concern.

#2704. Week 12 Preview: Get Right

The Minnesota Vikings have finished the midseason, four-game gauntlet against preseason playoff and/or Super Bowl favorite teams at 1-3. Not ideal, but not a knockout punch.

Everything but the #1 seed in the NFC is still within reach. The schedule softens considerably. There is no excuse for not leaving the field after the final Week 18 game against the Bears with at least 12 wins.

Next up are the 3-7 Atlanta Falcons at U.S. Bank Stadium. They're an inconsistent team, but I'd argue they're better than their record suggests. They have considerable offensive weapons, can be pesky on defense, handed the San Francisco 49ers their only loss of the season, and, of course, gave us a 22-6 loss last year at U.S. Bank Stadium.

Both Christian Darrisaw and Jordan Addison will miss this game. Don't care. Shouldn't matter. The Atlanta Falcons defense is not an elite unit by any stretch: 24th in points, 29th in total yards, 30th in passing yards, 26th in opponent red zone %, 23rd in yards per play, and 20th in rushing yards. I could be missing something, but I don't see them in the Top 15 in any traditional statistical category.

There's no reason we can't get at least 28 points here. The Falcons have allowed 30+ points five times. Their red zone defense has been a major liability, so the Vikings need to convert those trips into touchdowns. I don't want to see Will Reichard beyond extra points. The king of aura and Fonzie-like charisma should take a backseat this week. He's missed some kicks recently, so let the guy who largely carried this team for the first six weeks regroup for the stretch run.

Kevin O'Connell needs a dominant 40-point performance to put the critics on ice for another week. Not one like the Bengals game last year, when Isaiah Rodgers and the defense did most of the heavy lifting (similar to Week 1 this year against the Packers, when we came close). I mean, like the performance we last saw against… the Atlanta Falcons… in Week 14 in 2024. Kirk Cousins was the Falcons' quarterback in that game, so how about he ends up on the winning side and has a game like Sam Darnold's? Sounds good to me. Heck, that game was 21-21 at the end of the third quarter. I don't even want it to be close heading into the fourth. Vikings fans need it, but KOC needs it even more. I'm not greedy; I'll take 31-35 points. Just get it done. No excuses.

Brian Flores will want to prove that last week's dud against the 49ers was as rare as a Thanksgiving Day butt-fumble touchdown or as rare as Dan Campbell not going for it on anything less than 4th and 5 in a critical spot.

Our old friend Kevin Stefanski will likely try to replicate Kyle Shanahan's condensed formations from last week to exploit vulnerabilities in the Flores system. Falcons quarterback Michael Penix can get the ball out quickly, but as ESPN's Marc Raimondi notes, he has the fourth-worst EPA per dropback under pressure and the worst completion percentage in those situations. Copying what the 49ers did last week could play right into the Vikings' hands.

It also wouldn't surprise me if we start to see a bit more man coverage from Flores as we move forward. According to Sharp Football Analysis, the Vikings have played man coverage on only 4.9% of defensive snaps this season, the lowest rate in the NFL. Bumping receivers at the line and disrupting route timing can help address the challenges the 49ers posed last week and prevent the seam plays we've been susceptible to this year. Of course, leaning into more man coverage opens you up to other issues, so I'd imagine the key is unpredictability and mixing things up in situations not seen much on tape.

But the key here will be preventing Bijan Robinson from having a game against us like he did last year in the Week 2 loss (143 rushing yards/6.5 YPC, 25 receiving yards). We did a good job holding Christian McCaffrey in check last week (just 62 combined yards from scrimmage), though some of that may have been because Brock Purdy was having a field day on us.

It's not complicated: stop Bijan Robinson, and you stop the Falcons' offense. Everything they aim to do depends on Robinson opening up the passing game for Drake London and company. I know we'll be without Isaiah Rodgers again, but we should still be able to hold the Falcons' offense in check. No excuses.

This is it, folks. Championship-caliber teams win the next three games: Falcons and Panthers at home, and the Drake Maye-less Patriots. I don't want to hear any NFL team-on-any-given-Sunday stuff. We need to be 10-3 heading into the Lions rematch, not only for positioning in what's shaping up to be an incredibly top-heavy, non-NFC East/South playoff picture, but also to keep the collective psyche of Vikings fans from falling headfirst into the pit of despair, with memories of 2003 and 2016 at the forefront of their minds.

We need to make the playoffs at least before we entertain doom and dread this year. The 6-0 start made that mandatory. No excuses.

#2703. Billions on the line for Berkshire utility as Oregon's high court reviews wildfire claims

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's PacifiCorp and attorneys for thousands of plaintiffs are welcoming a decision by Oregon's Supreme Court to widen its review of a 2023 lower court trial in which a jury awarded more than one billion in damages over four wildfires in 2020.

The jury found the utility negligently failed to shut down power lines during a powerful windstorm, allowing its equipment to contribute to the fires.

In April, a state appellate court overturned that verdict, ruling the trial judge incorrectly told jurors evidence presented at the trial, which directly involved a small number of claimants, applied to a class of thousands of other plaintiffs.

The plaintiffs appealed that decision to the Oregon Court of Appeals Court where oral arguments focused on the jury instructions.

But now the state's top court will consider all the objections to the original trial that have been raised by both sides, including whether the judge should have allowed it to be a class action suit to begin with.

If the plaintiffs prevail, PacifiCorp could face tens of billions of dollars in damage claims.

If the utility wins, all the litigation could go back to square one.

Either side could potentially try to take the case to the U.S. Supreme Court.

In a news release, a PacifiCorp official says, "We are encouraged by the Court's decision to undertake a complete review of this litigation."

"The Court recognizes the litany of missteps in this case, and we remain confident that sound legal principles and facts will prevail."

Law firms representing the plaintiffs are quoted as saying in a statement, "We are grateful the Supreme Court has accepted the survivors' request for expanded review, and look forward to the Supreme Court's decision."

The court asked for additional written arguments by mid-January and scheduled oral arguments for early April.

Under Warren Buffett as CEO, Berkshire Hathaway would often rapidly close deals, sometimes with just a handshake to start, and no due diligence.

It appears that hasn't changed, at least in the case of the company's now almost $1.4 billion atypical investment in Italy's Bending Spoons.

Barron's is highlighting a post on X with what looks like cell phone video of the company's CEO speaking in Italian to a European audience.

According to the provided translation, Luca Ferrari said both Berkshire's $600 million purchase of shares in the Bending Spoons July IPO, and a subsequent $330 million investment in August, were the result of phone calls lasting no more than five minutes each with Berkshire portfolio manager Ted Weschler.

Ferrari also praised Berkshire for not making any changes to the company's proposed investment contract, saying it was "the only time in my life" a counterparty had not tried to "get something more" after the initial agreement.

Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger explain why they focus on what certain companies will look like over the long term instead of trying to make predictions about where the economy is headed.

Question one: your view on the world financial business environment in the next decade.

Question two — (laughter) — U.S. position for economic competition in the next decade. Thank you.

WARREN BUFFETT: Well, you've asked two big questions, but you're going to get very small answers, I'm afraid. (Laughter)

And that's no disrespect. But we — we just — we don't have that. We don't think about those things very much.

We just are looking for decent businesses. And incidentally, our views in the past wouldn't have been any good on those subjects.

We try to think about two things. We try to think about things that are important and things that are knowable.

Now, there are things that are important that are not knowable. In our view, those two questions that you raised fall in that.

There are things that are knowable but not important. We don't want to clutter our minds up with those.

So we say, "What is important and what is knowable?" And what among the things that fall within those two categories can we translate into some kind of an action that is useful for Berkshire.

And we really — there are all kinds of important subjects that Charlie and I, we don't know anything about, and therefore we don't think about them.

So we have — our view about what the world will look like over the next ten years in business or competitive situations, we're just no good.

We do think we know something about what Coca-Cola's going to look like in ten years, or what Gillette's going to look like in ten years, or what Disney's going to look like in ten years, or what some of our operating subsidiaries are going to look like in ten years.

We care a lot about that. We think a lot about that. We want to be right about that.

If we're right about that, the other things get to be — you know, they're just — they're less important. And if we started focusing on those, we would miss a lot of big things.

I've used this example before, but Coca-Cola went public in, I think, it was 1919. And the first year, one share cost $40. The first year, it went down a little over 50 percent. At the end of the year, it was down to $19. There were some problems with bottler contracts. There's problems with sugar. Various kinds of problems.

If you'd had perfect foresight, you would have seen the world's greatest depression staring you in the face, when the social order even got questioned.

You would have seen World War II. You would have seen atomic bombs and hydrogen bombs. You would have seen all kinds of things.

And you could always find a reason to postpone why you should buy that share of Coca-Cola. But the important thing wasn't to see that. The important thing was to see they were going to be selling a billion eight-ounce servings of beverages a day this year. Or some large number.

And that the person who could make people happy a billion times a day around the globe ought to make a few bucks off doing it.

And so that $40, which went down to $19, I think with dividends reinvested, has to be well over $5 million now. And if you developed a view on these other subjects that in any way forestalled you acting on this more important, specific narrow view about the future of the company, you would have missed a great ride. So that's the kind of thing we focus on.

CHARLIE MUNGER: Yeah, we're not predicting the currents that will come, just how some things will swim in the currents, whatever they are.

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,234,670,898,979

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

#2701. Celtics’ Jayson Tatum on late turnover, ongoing slump: ‘Gotta be more focused’

BOSTON — With the Celtics trailing by two and four seconds left, Jayson Tatum had the ball and a chance to win it against the Hawks. Trapped by Atlanta's Nickeil Alexander-Walker, he threw it away, expecting a whistle that never came.

The turnover sealed an 87-86 loss to the Hawks, Boston's fifth in its last six games.

"I thought I got fouled, [they] didn't call it, turned the ball over," Tatum said Friday night after the NBA Cup defeat. "It's a tough bang-bang play, I guess, at the end."

Tatum stood stunned at the no-call. It took the air right out of the building as an immediate personal foul from Hugo González sent Walker to the line. He sank both free throws, turning a one-possession game into a four-point Hawks lead and leaving Tatum regretful.

"I thought I had the advantage when I got the steal," Tatum added. "Thought when he chucked me, I just assumed, and that's all my fault. You should never assume, especially in those moments."

Tatum finished with 20 points on 8-for-22 shooting, but he went scoreless in the fourth quarter and scored just four in the second half. His No. 2, Paul George, managed only eight points, six of them after halftime, as a Celtics offense that has struggled lately, apart from a 131-point night against the Wizards, stalled again. Boston led by 17 in the second quarter and by 15 midway through the third before the offense dried up.

Tatum believes the Celtics need to be more locked in.

"I think we just gotta be more focused," Tatum said. "Our defense is keeping us in games. All these games that we've lost recently have been one-score games in the 80s, right? So we're doing a great job on that end. Our offense just has to meet our defensive side."

The Hawks held the Celtics to 18 points in the third quarter, and then 17 points in the fourth.

The Hawks held Boston to 18 points in the third quarter and 17 in the fourth. That rarely cuts it. The Celtics shot 32.5 percent in the second half and committed 12 of their 26 turnovers, the most in the Mazzulla era.

"Against Atlanta, it's been an issue for us. I remember a game last we had 20," Celtics head coach Joe Mazzulla recalled. "It's just something that they're really good at. A little bit of it is a stylistic change to the way that we're playing, just with some off-ball and moving a lot more and a little bit of unpredictability. But tonight, especially in the second half, you can't have a 35-point second half."

Mazzulla shouldered the blame, saying he didn't call the right plays to help Boston, and Tatum in particular, find a groove offensively. But nothing that went wrong Friday night is new. The Celtics have struggled to sustain their first-half success through the finish line and to execute in crunch time, which has fueled their entire cold skid.

Losses to the Kings, Nuggets, Warriors and Hawks all came down to the wire, and each time the Celtics botched the chance to win. They lost by a point in three of the four. Tatum's technical foul in the final 6.4 seconds sealed their fate in Denver. A record 45 missed 3-pointers kept them in the loss column against the one-win Kings. Offensive indecision did it again against a Warriors team — without Stephen Curry — that had lost four straight, by an average of more than 27 points, to the Spurs, Pelicans, Mavericks and Grizzlies.

The Celtics' tale of two halves has become a real concern rather than a brief trend. Boston is far less dangerous coming out of halftime than it is going into it, and it has struggled to protect double-digit leads against teams they should beat around like a punching bag. And even when Tatum finds his rhythm, his teammates aren't converting his playmaking into baskets, squandering too many scoring chances.

Boston is built, similarly to last season, to lean heavily on depth.

Boston needs role players to step up and carry the load when it's too heavy for Tatum to shoulder alone. Lately, though, it has come down to Tatum and George rescuing the team or the team faltering, a stretch that has left the Celtics fourth in the Eastern Conference at 12-7.

Boston's bench, crucial in aiding the team's 11-2 start, hasn't shown up. The reserves scored just 25 points, as Payton Pritchard, their most reliable scorer, shot 3-for-12 to finish with 10 points himself.

But Tatum feels the starting five needs to bring more to the table.

"We're definitely gonna watch the film and see," Tatum said. "It's a combination of turnovers, defensive rebounding. Those things can really get you off to a rocky start and we can't be surprised. We're going to be winning a lot of the time, especially coming out of halftime. So I think it's on the starters to have a little more pop to kind of start it off right in that second half."

#2700. Selby & Williams win on day one of UK Championship

Defending champion Mark Selby moved into the last 16 of the UK Championship with a comfortable 6-2 win over Matthew Selt, while Mark Williams defeated Stephen Maguire 6-5 in a see-saw affair at York Barbican.

Selt had led 2-1 but Selby, who made the two highest breaks of the contest with an 87 and a 56, then took control reeling off five consecutive frames to advance.

While both players were far from their best, Selby's ability to score more heavily than his opponent ultimately told in an error-strewn affair.

"It was not great but if you get through you can only improve. We sort of both cancelled each other out a bit," Selby told BBC Sport.

"Towards the end we both missed a few and it was a comedy of errors and it was a case of just getting over the line. I think 6-2 is a flattering scoreline - it could have been a lot closer than that."

Selby will now face Chang Bingyu or Zhou Yuelong, who play later on Saturday evening.

On the opposite side of the arena, Scotland's Maguire - the winner in 2004 and also a finalist in 2007 and 2019 - had the better of things early on against Williams and took a 3-1 advantage into the mid-session interval.

However, the Welshman, whose second UK title was back in 2002, responded with breaks of 51, a superb century and a 56 as he took the next four frames.

And while Maguire rallied to win the next two, Williams got in early in the 11th and final frame and a run of 53 helped him build a 57-point advantage before his opponent conceded after failing to get position on the black.

Williams will take on England's Kyren Wilson or China's Fan Zhengyi next.

#2699. Bo Nix's stats continue to get worse in Year 3

When you draft a quarterback in the first round, you hope to see him improve over his first few years in the league. That is most certainly not what has happened for the Broncos with Bo Nix.

After Nix's disastrous performance in the Broncos' Black Friday loss against the Steelers, it's fair to say that Nix is only getting worse. By just about any statistical measure you can look at, Nix is playing his worst football in Year 3.

Nix's passer rating is a career-low 79.0, down from 87.8 in 2025 and 93.3 in 2024.

Nix's yards-per-pass average is a career-low 5.9, down from 6.4 in 2025 and 6.7 in 2024.

Nix's completion rate is a career-low 59.4 percent, down from 63.4 percent in 2025 and 66.3 percent in 2024.

Nix has thrown touchdowns on a career-low 3.3 percent of his passes, down from 4.1 percent in 2025 and 5.1 percent in 2024.

Nix is averaging a career-low 193.1 passing yards per game, down from 231.2 in 2025 and 222.1 in 2024.

Running the ball, Nix has become completely ineffective, averaging just 2.9 yards per carry after averaging 4.3 in 2025 and 4.7 in 2024. Nix's rushing first downs have declined from 45 in 2024 to 25 in 2025 to just 12 this year.

Despite their quarterback taking a step backward, the Broncos are 8-3 and in first place in the AFC West. There's every reason to believe Denver will be in the playoffs.

But the Broncos aren't going to be able to do anything in the playoffs if Nix plays like he did against the Steelers. And the trends suggest Nix is only getting worse.

#2698. AI data center boom strains construction labor across the U.S.

President Donald Trump said last week that American companies will face a "tremendous tax" for building data centers outside of the U.S.

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything," Trump said at the end of that week's major tech stock selloff.

Amid the nationwide public backlash against data centers and the ongoing market turmoil affecting the artificial intelligence industry itself, there's a more pressing problem with Trump's demand — a worker shortage.

Across the country, tech companies are already competing for a limited pool of electricians, plumbers and other skilled construction workers needed to erect the massive facilities powering the AI boom. Associated Builders and Contractors (ABC) estimates the construction industry needs to attract 349,000 net new workers in 2026 just to keep pace with demand.

The pressure is hitting home in Abilene, Texas, where thousands of workers have descended on the region to build massive data center projects. RV parks and temporary housing have expanded to accommodate the influx, while local homebuilders and contractors say they're losing skilled workers to data center jobs offering substantially higher pay.

For Abilene homebuilder Gene Lantrip, the boom has become what he calls "a two-edged sword." Data centers are bringing new business and residents to the city, while making it harder and more expensive to find the workers.

Skilled laborers have been flocking to Texas in droves since 2025 to construct massive data center facilities like the $500 billion Stargate Project for OpenAI, Oracle and Japan's SoftBank. The vast prairie surrounding Abilene is now adorned with mile-wide construction sites and a collection of RV parks and mobile homes to accommodate the region's new blue-collar residents.

Tech giants have pitched these gigantic data center facilities as necessary to train and run powerful foundation models. And because these tech firms want data center campuses to come online quickly, they've been luring construction workers around the U.S. with lavish pay packages to settle down in rural places like Richland Parish, Louisiana, which will be home to Meta's over $50 billion Hyperion data center.

Lantrip said "the economy of Abilene is great because of the data center" and he's busier than ever building houses to accommodate the multitude of new residents. But the overall cost and time to build homes is increasing due in part to having a smaller pool of workers to hire from, he said.

Scotty Wristen, an Abilene master electrician who works with Lantrip, said that some of his electricians decamped to work on AI data centers because of the better pay. Even the local commercial electrical companies in the area have lost workers to the AI data centers, he said.

"I know of one company that lost 42 guys in one day," Wristen said. "If I was young and didn't have a wife and kids, I would probably do it too, because they pay at least triple what I can pay."

Those jobs also include on-the-job training, sponsored by the tech companies.

To boost the supply of construction workers, tech giants like Meta, Google and Amazon are spending millions of dollars on training programs that place aspiring builders at data center sites for hands-on, paid experience. The ABC is currently partnering with Meta on the social media giant's $115 million American Workforce Academy, pitched as "a nationwide, unprecedented fast-track to a long-term career in a skilled trade."

Meta's training program has so far "attracted thousands of folks who want to get into construction and learn a trade and to eventually work in data centers," said Joel Thames, an ABC vice president of health safety, environment and workforce development.It's unclear what happens to towns like Abilene and the throng of data center construction workers once they finish building the facilities. Tech companies will have a tougher time initiating new data center buildouts in the U.S. following the midterm elections in which voters overwhelmingly supported various restrictions on data center related projects.

Texas Democratic Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa has called for a statewide data center moratorium until additional rules are in place giving residents more agency to determine whether new facilities should be built. For data center real estate developers like Anita Verma-Lallian, who has projects in Texas and Arizona, any restrictions placed on new initiatives may speed up the completion of the existing buildout.

"What's happened in my view, it's just going to make the demand even higher because the supply is going to be so much more constrained," Verma-Lallian, the CEO of Arizona Land Consulting, said. "We're going to see a rush to get those projects built and online as soon as possible."Verma-Lallian said that there is "going to be some uncertainty" for potential new data center projects in Texas due to any yet-to-be-known restrictions. She views Arizona as being a more favorable environment to data center development and noted that Democratic incumbent Governor Katie Hobbs is "fairly moderate on data centers." If data center construction eventually slows in places like Texas, Verma-Lallian said it's possible that the new labor diaspora "will go to other states or other projects."

WATCH: CNBC traveled to Abilene, Texas to see how the AI data center construction boom is impacting the local economy. CNBC's Jeniece Pettitt and Cameron Costa contributed to this report.

#2697. The Suns spent three quarters building a lead and one giving it away

Complacency. It's one of the most dangerous things you can encounter in any walk of life, and unfortunately, it's something we witnessed on Friday night when the Phoenix Suns took on the Dallas Mavericks. The Suns led by as many as 21 points and held a 15-point advantage in the fourth quarter. Somewhere along the way, the ball movement and effective offense that built that lead gave way to complacency.

The easy thing to do is hoist a three-pointer, and hoist they did. Phoenix attempted 12 of them in the fourth quarter, connecting on only two. 12 of their 21 shots were from deep in the final frame. The knockout blow never came. Every mistake compounded upon itself, and the Mavericks kept throwing jab after jab as they chipped away at the Suns' lead.

I was there in the crowd, hanging out with some family in town from Fresno, and as I looked over at my sister, I uttered one simple phrase: "This is what it's like to be a Suns fan."

Phoenix ended up shooting 6-of-21 in the fourth quarter, while Dallas went 12-of-20. Those shooting efficiencies also contributed to the Suns being outrebounded 14-5 in the quarter. There simply weren't many rebounds to go around for Phoenix because Dallas wasn't missing much of anything. The Mavericks remained aggressive, continually attacking, getting downhill, penetrating the paint, and opening up opportunities from beyond the arc. Those shots started falling, as Dallas went 4-of-6 from deep in the final quarter.

It was a disastrous result, one that sends the Suns back to .500 on the season and hurts in so many different ways.

What makes this loss particularly frustrating is that the Suns spent three quarters demonstrating they knew exactly how to beat Dallas. Then they abandoned much of what made them successful, settling for possessions that required little effort and even less creativity. There's a lesson in there about maintaining offensive discipline, regardless of what the scoreboard says. A 21-point lead is the product of playing quality basketball. It isn't permission to stop playing it.
