#2696. AI shopping agents are having a moment, but Amazon and a growing number of sites threaten to stunt their growth

Maia Bittner urgently needed to replace her car's windshield wipers. So last month, she turned to Meta's Muse personal artificial intelligence agent.

Bittner, chief product officer at fintech startup Imprint, gave Muse the greenlight to buy some wipers on her behalf from AutoZone. Or so she thought.

About 17 hours later, she got a notification from her Muse agent, saying AutoZone canceled her order due to an unspecified error.

"Honestly, the most reliable path now is you ordering on Amazon yourself," Muse said, according to a screenshot of the message.

The interaction encapsulates the current messy and complicated landscape of agentic shopping apps, the AI tools that can make purchases on behalf of users. Over the past year, tech companies such as OpenAI, Meta, Google and Instinct have introduced versions of personal agents that promise to take the hassle out of browsing and buying online.

While some major retailers, such as Walmart and Sephora, have opened up their sites to agents, companies including Amazon, eBay, United Airlines and Kohl's limit what actions agents can take or block them outright. Others have added provisions to their terms of service spelling out that they're not liable if users' AI-driven purchases go wrong.

"It's a very mixed bag," Emily Pfeiffer, a principal analyst at Forrester, said in an interview. "It means that a consumer who's using an agent to shop and browse and learn is not going to see the full set of options because some websites are unavailable."

The hiccups threaten to stunt the growth of shopping agents by creating a clunky and frustrating experience for users who are just starting to experiment with the tools. The question of who takes the blame when a transaction goes wrong also remains unclear.

Despite those challenges, analysts remain bullish about agentic commerce's potential. U.S. revenue from agentic commerce could reach up to $1 trillion by 2030, analysts at McKinsey and ICSC forecast in April.

Retailers and tech companies are also positioning agents as a helping hand for holiday shopping. Walmart on Tuesday touted its partnership with Meta, saying consumers could "put an AI agent to work" to nab Black Friday deals.

Earlier this month, Shopify President Harley Finkelstein predicted this year would be the "first holiday season" where AI-driven shopping goes mainstream.

"I think last year we saw AI influence more in discovery," Finkelstein told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." "I think now we're going to see more of that conversion."

For users hoping to turn their online shopping over to AI agents, the experience can be unpredictable.

Agents encounter a patchwork of permissions across various websites. Retailers such as Coach and Gap allow AI bots to browse but not check out, while others such as Target sometimes "block shopping agents by accident," according to an audit conducted in August by agent security company Billions Network.

Amazon has responded the most aggressively to agents by walling off its site entirely. The company rankled Elon Musk last month after it prevented his Grok Bot from placing orders.

In September, the company also blocked Meta's Muse from shopping on its platform, saying it never agreed to participate in the program. And last November, Amazon sued Perplexity, accusing the startup of concealing its Comet agent, which can purchase products on behalf of users.

Amazon CEO Andy Jassy has said the company expects "over time, to partner with third-party agents," but that any partnerships will require the right "value exchange" between agents and retailers.

Bots encounter issues even when retailers welcome them in. They often fumble through webpages and get stumped by login requirements at checkout. In some cases, the agents may require human intervention to bypass a Captcha test and continue browsing.

A Visa study published earlier this month instructed 22 models from 12 AI labs to carry out a range of shopping tasks, such as buying a TV, a laptop and a hair appliance. The agents failed about 43% of test runs, and in more than half of those failed runs, an agent purchased a product "that did not meet the customer's identified requirements," according to the study.

Bittner said she is a "big believer" in AI agents' potential. Earlier this year, she said, she was delighted when Perplexity's shopping agent found and purchased a pair of Adidas sneakers that were sold out for months.

Other experiences have been clumsier. Last week, she said, she asked Meta's Muse to help her find a Jellycat octopus plushie for her kids. It suggested a product marked 75% off from "Crazy Hobby Supply," a retailer she'd never heard of and that didn't appear to have any reviews, she said.

"I sort of trust Google to only surface sites that are not scams to me in their sponsored ads," Bittner said. "In the same way, I want AI to only surface to me websites that are not scams. I don't think we're there yet."

Users have reported mishaps with other AI tools, including one case where Instinct's text-based agent booked a hotel and signed up for a loyalty program without the consumer's consent.

Many retailers are approaching agents cautiously because they're concerned about fraudulent bot activity. San Francisco-based Skyfire is trying to solve that problem by arming retailers with more data to identify which bots are visiting their site and to verify that a human has actually consented to let an agent buy a product on their behalf.

Typically, when a human visits a website, there are "all kinds of signals" to prove it's not a fraudulent user, Skyfire CEO Amir Sarhangi said in an interview.

None of those things are available to retailers when an agent accesses their site, Sarhangi said. Agents might appear even more suspicious because they mask their IP address or origin via data centers, and they often use one-time, virtual cards to check out.

"So we talked to a lot of merchants and banks and financial institutions, and the things they told us were: We want to know who the end user is, and we want to know it's a real human," Sarhangi said. "We want to know who these agents are, because we're not going to just let willy-nilly anybody do these types of transactions, and we need a way to kind of verify that."

AI companies, retailers, financial institutions and other industries have announced an alphabet soup of "protocols," rules that propose ways to make it easier for agents to transact on websites.

There's yet to be a broad consensus around who takes responsibility when an agent purchase goes sideways. American Express in April said it would protect eligible customers "from charges related to AI agent error." Stripe, whose Link digital wallet is integrated with Muse, protects some agent purchases and offers a "refund guarantee."

Others are more wary. Last month, JPMorgan Chase blocked external agents from operating on behalf of customers, citing security concerns. Best Buy is among several retailers that deem users responsible for agent purchases in their terms of service. Best Buy says it views those transactions "as if you performed them yourself."

"Best Buy is not responsible for conduct, errors, actions, or inaction by any Digital Agent," the company's terms say.

Sarhangi said he can see why retailers, especially Amazon, are shutting their doors to external agents when the user experience remains inconsistent.

"At the scale [Amazon] operates in, I would think they need it to be much more aligned with their existing processes, so that they're not going to have a bunch of unhappy users saying, 'What's going on here?'" he said.

For now, agents remain a niche tool for shopping, with agentic purchases making up just 0.4% of global e-commerce transactions and 0.2% of value, according to Bain & Co. research published earlier this month.

Uber disclosed last month that AI agents booked only 504 rides and Uber Eats orders during its busiest week in October. Airbnb also said it has seen little volume from agents, while DoorDash said it has recorded only "several hundred" agent orders so far.

But retailers may not be able to ignore them forever. Roughly a quarter of Muse's 10 million users are shopping online via an AI agent with an average spend of $200 per order, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg said in October.

Instinct CEO Noah Shinn told CNBC earlier this month that the average transaction value among its users exceeds $500 per person.

Asked about some companies' efforts to block agents, Shinn said he tries to "give them some slack."

"It's a new interface, it's a new mode of where users' attention is coming from, and it might just disrupt existing large services of theirs," Shinn said at a CNBC CEO Council dinner in San Francisco. "They just need time, and we've just done a lot of thinking on our side."

"We can obviously move very fast and certain businesses cannot," he said. "So, how can we collaborate with them?"

#2694. 4 Things we learned from the Broncos 27-5 loss to Pittsburgh

Bo Nix and the Denver Broncos were embarrassed.

Even that is being kind to what the Broncos put on the field for Black Friday against the Pittsburgh Steelers. Who was the team dealing with all of the drama this week?

Somehow, Denver managed to score five points as a team that looked like it didn't get off the plane in a 27-5 drubbing. Don't let the score fool you; it wasn't that close.

Yet despite that pitiful outcome and performance, the sky isn't falling. The emotion has settled, and the Broncos are still 8-3.

The Steelers should have given Nix a game ball.

The Broncos quarterback just played his worst game as a quarterback. For certain in the NFL, maybe going back to when he was a kid.

He looked and played on Friday like he had no confidence. He doesn't trust himself. He doesn't trust his line. He had happy feet. But more than anything is the decision-making. What the hell was that pass on the pick-six?

Everyone has an opinion about Nix. Now this game is just more fodder.

Who knows what it means for the rest of the season? For the here and now, Nix was atrocious. But just like the team, it's all in front of him. It's all about how he responds.

But it wasn't just Nix. There's plenty of blame to go around.

The Broncos' offensive line still hasn't blocked anyone on Pittsburgh's defense.

That was one of the worst games by that unit in a long, long, long time.

Jonah Coleman played like a rookie and had the vision of a Pee-Wee running back.

Jaylen Waddle continues to have drop issues.

There should be an alert put out on Courtland Sutton. Aside from an offensive pass interference penalty, he did nothing. Sutton running routes right now is painful to watch. It's like his cleats are in cement.

If that wasn't enough, the play calling was equal garbage. Find something you love as much as Sean Payton does screen passes. Or failed end-around runs with Marvin Mims Jr.

I guess I could have saved a lot of words by just typing "yikes."

Obviously, no defense is helped by your quarterback gifting the opponent 14 points. That still doesn't excuse how bad Denver's defense played.

The big plays remain a major problem for this unit, highlighted by Pat Surtain II getting torched by headcase DK Metcalf. Or how about Alex Singleton being on rookie receiver Germi Bernard? He had one catch for 58 yards. That's elite defensive game planning right there.

Knowing the Steelers were going to run, the Broncos still can't stop it or tackle Pittsburgh's backs. There were at least three missed tackles behind the line of scrimmage. That's the other maddening aspect of this defense is the inability to tackle consistently.

The Steelers finished with 117 yards rushing for the game.

Yes, how Denver looked and played was disastrous, starting with the quarterback. The team looked like it hadn't stepped on the field in a year.

But putting any stock in this game and saying it's a harbinger for the rest of the season is foolhardy. The last time the Broncos were embarrassed like this, they won eight of their next nine games.

Just look at the Steelers; last week they were flogged by the Joe Milton-led Philadelphia Eagles.

Denver's loss was embarrassing, and it sucks. But the Broncos still lead the AFC West and have the No. 2 seed in the AFC playoffs.

Oh, and Denver hosts the Buffalo Bills on Christmas Day, so the No. 1 seed is still in play.

Move on from Pittsburgh and prepare to play better against the Miami Dolphins next Sunday.

#2693. The biggest money-making energy trade looks over. What to buy instead

Is one of the greatest money-making trades of the last few years coming to an end?

No, I'm not talking about Nvidia or any of the data center- or AI-related power companies (although they have been crushed lately too… more on that on CNBC TV. Tune in!).

I'm talking about whether the epic boom in refinery-related stocks is unwinding. Over the last year, the jump in some of these companies has been nothing short of remarkable.

That jump is largely due to massive increases in margins, earnings, and cash flow. Those have occurred because of a big jump in what is called a crack spread. That is the difference between the price of oil and the finished price of refined products such as diesel and jet fuel. For most of this year, spreads have been soaring as disruptions and attacks on facilities and shipping in the Arabian Gulf, Hormuz, and deep inside Russia have played havoc with the market. In the last week or so, however, something big has changed: many of those spreads have started to tighten.

This seismic drop in spreads is causing a selloff in the aforementioned refinery companies.

If spreads continue to fall, these stocks may well keep selling off from recent highs. The upshot for investors may be that, as the shares come down, analysts see more upside. The median price target for Phillips 66 (PSX) now indicates a 20% gain from here, while smaller-cap Par Pacific (PARR) is double that.

Here are a few other key points to focus on heading into the Thanksgiving-shortened week.

I like to keep politics as much at arm's length as possible — we all know that energy and politics (sadly) often go hand in hand. Energy policies coming from Washington, D.C., can drive — or destroy — investment dollars. With all the big shifts in the White House over the last 10 years (four years of Trump, four years of Biden, and now 2 years into Trump #2), energy policies can blow through Washington faster than a summer squall hits a wind turbine. But because federal policies can mean so much for specific companies, sectors, and investments, it's important to follow the news flow.

That said, I'd like to take a moment to introduce you to the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, known as BAAJA (and pronounced like the state in Mexico). Like much legislation, BAAJA is an obscure but important bill working its way through the American Congress. BAAJA does a lot of things, but what Power Insider readers care about is its focus on energy permitting reform. In a nutshell, BAAJA would help speed up the building of new electricity infrastructure. Again, that's an overly simplistic view of a big bill, but it needs to be on your radar. The utility trade group Edison Electric Institute came out in favor of the legislation this month after the industry received some 11th-hour changes. See more about it in our recent interview with EEI President Drew Maloney here.

The bill passed the U.S. Senate in a vote late Thursday night. Now it moves to the House. Support for its final passage is also coming from the big LNG trade group, whose executive director said he "applauds the Senate for its bipartisan effort to pass meaningful, bipartisan, and durable permitting reforms aimed at delivering greater predictability, consistency, and certainty in the permitting, production, and export process for U.S. LNG. This legislation moves us closer to untangling red tape and eliminating overlapping agency reviews that can keep U.S. energy projects delayed for years."

The Natural Gas Supply Association also chimed in, saying it urges Congress to see the bill as "a resource-agnostic and balanced reform agenda for boosting American energy security. It keeps strong environmental reviews in place, but it draws a firm line against the endless procedural delays that tie critical energy projects in knots for a decade."

In my view, passing BAAJA is an important test of Congress's appetite for helping the energy and electrical power industry, lowering costs, and speeding time to market. The old adage was correct: time is money. Literally. If BAAJA moves on through the House, we could see an upside move in some utility stocks and any company related to energy infrastructure.

Speaking of politics, the news is getting lost in the massive Anthropic (ANT) IPO, but the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) — whose importance I wrote about in the last edition — just decided to resume permitting for data centers. The Texas Tribune did a nice write-up on it, and you can read it here.

It's tough to say, but it's true. The two are inextricably linked by policies created at both the federal and state levels. As I wrote in the last edition, some federally elected officials may have less impact on state-level issues — like the data center buildout — but they certainly have power over national laws. While many of you are enthralled, disgusted, or confused by sordid tales of Nvidia and Jensen-gate, keep one eye open on what happens in Washington, D.C.

Have a great week, and have a wonderful Thanksgiving, everyone.

Perhaps the big investor story — which is, like most things lately, related to Jensen-gate and Nvidia—is the sizable selloff in many AI, neocloud, and data center power-related stocks.

Painful for current shareholders, but big drops can also bring big opportunities. Wall Street believes that's the case, at least right now. Look at some of the potential upside based on the stocks' prices versus their price targets.

We spoke with the CEO of Applied Digital (APLD) about all of this on CNBC last week, and you can watch it here. He highlighted how the market may be getting these stock stories wrong because, at least with his company, he has long-term contracts with solid-credit companies. In that way, Applied Digital is more like a landlord than a "pure-play" AI company.

My take → While I'm not going to recommend a specific stock, I would add that it is critical for investors to understand the differences between these companies. While they may have some things in common, they are also different, with different business models, customers, leases, and sources of revenue.

Speaking of recommendations… here are two ideas for you:

A recent decline presents a nice opportunity in SOLV Energy (MWH). UBS analyst Jon Windham upgrades SOLV to buy from neutral, believing the market is "underappreciating several key tailwinds for SOLV given uncertainty around data center demand and higher interest rates." UBS highlights how the stock has fallen lately despite making two deals and beating and raising recent guidance. UBS says now is an "attractive entry point" for SOLV. (Side note: MWH is a great stock ticker.)

While markets overall fall, BMO remains bullish on Bloom Energy (BE). After speaking with the company, the analyst is upgrading Bloom to outperform, saying he remains "increasingly confident of BE's commercial traction" and that he still has conviction even with the recent drop in shares. While analyst Ameet Thakkar did cut his target to $230 from $260, that's still 50% upside from the current price. Bloom shares have tumbled 45% since October 1.

I was recently asked for my opinion on the price of oil. Here's my take. The market clearly wants to push oil contract prices lower and has for a few months. But, as I've said and reported for a while, don't confuse the futures (paper) price with the actual price. Yes, shipping rates and insurance rates have come down from recent highs — and there is a good amount of oil now flowing through Hormuz — but the cost of a physical barrel of crude is still well above the futures contract. If we get real, longer-lasting peace around Iran, oil prices will fall. There is plenty of oil in the world. That said, if bombs and missiles start firing again, oil prices will firm. I know that's a non-answer answer, but the oil market is truly headline-driven right now. My late good friend Boone Pickens (later in life, he didn't like being called by the "T" in his first name) used to avoid giving a set price target and instead give a directional quote, so I'll do the same: the odds of oil being lower in two months than it is today are 75%.

Last week, I had a one-on-one with Vimal Kapur, chair and CEO of Honeywell International (HON), at an RBC conference in New York City. While the conversation wasn't filmed, I can tell you that Kapur offered investors reassurance that his order book is doing very, very well. This is important because Honeywell is huge in the things that make other things work. Its sensors go into a ton of energy facilities, from refineries to LNG facilities. Honeywell is a leading indicator of spending and buildouts, so if it keeps getting orders, it's a good sign for macro energy.

Bottom line: Watch Honeywell stock as a potential "tell."

This week's Inside Line interview takes a different approach. We hit our friend Craig Johnson up for actionable insights into energy and stocks. Craig is managing director and chief market technician at Piper Sandler. He recently appeared on Power Lunch to talk about markets and stocks. He mentioned being bullish on energy, so we wanted to dig in just a little bit more.

This week, we had the pleasure of chatting with Balaji Krishnamurthy, president of Chevron Australia. We spoke at length about natural gas, the impact of disruptions, and long-term demand from Chevron customers in the Asia-Pacific region.

This is a bit of a local RBI for the New York City area, but one I felt was important to highlight. New York's electric grid operator just came out with a massive warning for everyone in our region: in just a couple of years, there may not be enough electricity supply to power our needs. A new report shows a deficit in seven years, with a nearly 2 GW power hole in a decade. That's a number of big power plants' worth of energy. Get your solar panels on and buy that backup generator! I'll call this one random, but scary.

Catch up with more on energy including interviews and video content from CNBC and Power Insider.

#2692. The Iran war could shatter rising expectations of surplus oil supply in coming months

Crude oil prices have sold off sharply in November as investors see a recovery in Middle East exports tipping the market into a surplus for the first time since the Iran war began.

Brent crude futures, the international benchmark, are down about 20% after approaching wartime highs in late October when the contract hit $118 a barrel.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate has also fallen about 21% after topping $104 a barrel in the same period. Brent closed at $95.44 a barrel Friday while WTI settled at $83.08.

U.S. retail gasoline prices have fallen well below $4 a gallon and diesel has eased below $6 a gallon.

In October, investors were pricing in the risk of a major military escalation in the Persian Gulf after the U.S. midterm elections. But President Donald Trump has held back so far from launching renewed military strikes against Iran after the Democrats wiped out Republican congressional majorities.

The selloff this month has likely gone too far, Giovanni Staunovo, a strategist at UBS, wrote in a note clients. The market has become "too comfortable with the supply outlook," Staunovo said. "The rebound in Gulf exports should not be mistaken for a normalization of the oil market," the analyst said.

The International Energy Agency has forecast an oil surplus beginning in the fourth quarter. This would represent a major turning point nine months after the Iran war triggered the largest supply disruption in the history of the oil market.

Goldman Sachs sees a crude surplus of 2 million barrels per day in 2027 as Gulf producers continue to adapt their supply chains to war in the Middle East. Commodity and ship tracking firm Kpler sees oversupply of 3 million bpd next year.

"If the recovery in the Arabian Gulf region continues, we're going to have oversupply," said Bob McNally, president of Rapidan Energy and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush.

But predictions of a surplus assume a sustained recovery in flows from the Middle East. The IEA also cautioned that downside risks "persist as attacks on shipping undermine maritime security."

"If pigs had wings, they'd fly," said David Goldwyn, who served as the State Department's special envoy for international energy under President Barack Obama, in reference to the surplus forecast.

The more likely scenario is that oil prices trade in a wide range through the end of Trump's term as the security situation in the Gulf remains volatile, McNally at Rapidan said. During periods of relative calm, crude will trade between $70 to $80, he said. Prices could spike well above $100 when the conflict escalates, he said.

"We think there will be more disruptions, more risk and that those surpluses won't materialize next year," McNally said.

Trump recently told Fox News that he might launch renewed strikes on Iran after the new year. A major military escalation in the region would lead to structurally higher prices, said Goldwyn, the Obama administration official. Iran would likely retaliate against U.S. strikes with attacks on ports, refineries or processing facilities, he said.

Crude exports from the Middle East have rebounded since the summer, though they have trended lower in recent weeks and remain well below pre-war levels as tankers in and around the Strait of Hormuz are attacked on a near daily basis.

At least 20 attacks on commercial ships have been reported this month, according to maritime security agencies. There were 35 such incidents in October, the highest level since the war began.

Middle east crude exports averaged 12.8 million barrels per day in the week ended Wednesday, about 24% below pre-war levels of 17 million bpd, according to Kpler data. Fuel shipments stood at 3.2 million bpd in the same period, down about 49% from 6.3 million bpd before the war.

Production has also increased in the Americas and demand has pulled back due to seasonal factors associated with the end of the summer driving season and refinery turnarounds, Goldwyn said.

Assuming a persistent surplus, JPMorgan forecasts Brent prices in the high $70s in the first half of 2027 before falling into the $60s in the second half of next year. Goldman Sachs sees Brent trading around $88 a barrel next year and WTI averaging about $78 barrel next year.

But Kpler warned the predicted surplus could easily tip into a deficit again if Iran or its Houthi allies bomb the United Arab Emirates export terminal at Fujairah or key Saudi infrastructure such as the kingdom's facilities at Ras Tanura, Yanbu or its East-West Pipeline.

Any further disruption to Mideast supplies would strike when the world's inventory buffers have already been drawn down to historic lows without time to restock them, Goldwyn said. The CEOs of ExxonMobil and Chevron have warned for months about the depletion of the world's oil stockpiles.

There is room to potentially release another 100 million barrels from the U.S. Strategic Petroleum Reserve and for the Europeans to deploy more refined products, Goldwyn said. But neither will be eager to dip into the reserves again, he said.

"If this looks like it's going to be a chronic situation, I think both the U.S and Europeans are going to be reluctant to draw on those reserves just to provide price relief," Goldwyn said.

China is also increasing its crude imports as the situation in the Middle East remains fragile, Staunovo at UBS said. Beijing's decision to dramatically cut its imports earlier this year was one of the major factors keeping prices from shooting even higher during the war.

"Together, these developments are contributing to a decline in oil on water and a tightening physical market," Staunovo said. "This supports our view that oil prices should recover from here."

#2690. Official report reveals exactly what sent 30 runners to hospital with ‘unusual symptoms’ at California half marathon

Health officials have finally concluded their investigation into why dozens of runners experienced "unusual symptoms" during the Santa Barbara Half Marathon earlier this month — and it turns out the culprit was the heat.

An investigation into the Nov. 8 race found no evidence of criminal activity, deliberate contamination or illicit drugs, officials said, putting to rest rampant speculation that something sinister may have been behind the mass medical scare.

"The concluded investigation did not identify evidence of criminal activity, intentional contamination, or the introduction of unknown illicit substances that could have contributed to medical incidents," the Santa Barbara County Public Health Department and local police said in a joint statement.

Participants run in the Santa Barbara Half Marathon on Sunday, Nov. 8, 2026, in Santa Barbara, Calif. Dennis Mihora via AP

Santa Barbara Police officers gather outside the Cottage Hospital emergency room to investigate after 19 runners fell ill during a half marathon. Facebook/John Palminteri

Roughly 30 of the 7,500 participants were taken to the hospital by ambulance, private transportation or as walk-ins after experiencing similar symptoms during the race.

Cottage Health initially raised alarms after reporting a "larger than expected number of emergency visits" involving patients with similar symptoms of an undetermined cause, prompting an investigation by the Santa Barbara Police Department.

Investigators tested for ketamine, psilocybin and kratom, among other substances, but found no trace of illicit drugs. They also inspected water stations set up for the event and found no evidence of tampering, despite widespread speculation on social media that runners may have been poisoned.

Health officials surmised that the heat was the more likely cause, and their investigation seemed to confirm that conclusion.  The temperature jumped 14 degrees, from 62 degrees to 76 degrees Fahrenheit between 6 a.m. and 9 a.m. The high temperature in 2026 was 17 degrees higher than the race a year prior.

An aerial view of the start of the Santa Barbara Half Marathon. @santabarbarahalf/Instagram

Santa Barbara Police officers gather outside the emergency room at Cottage Hospital after 19 half marathon runners required treatment for health concerns. Facebook/John Palminteri

"The collective findings are consistent with heat-related illness and indicate that significant differences in weather and environmental conditions, combined with the physical demands of the event, likely contributed," the statement said.

One mother whose daughter was hospitalized after participating in the race questioned whether heat alone could explain what happened.

The mother had expressed frustration with the suggestion that heat exhaustion was responsible, arguing that her daughter's fitness level and preparation made the explanation difficult to accept.

"Santa Barbara in November, no one's going to knock over from the heat unless you're completely wildly unprepared," she said to The Post.

#2688. Report: NFL scouts see much more talent on USC's roster than on UCLA's

UCLA Bruin fans have seen the difference that good coaching can make this season. A year after the dumpster-fire 2025 season, first-year head coach Bob Chesney has the Bruins on the doorstep of the Big Ten Conference championship game.

To get there, UCLA will likely need a win over No. 15 USC on Saturday at the Rose Bowl. The No. 9 Bruins are 10-1 but UCLA quarterback Ty Dieffenbach is a game-time decision after suffering a hip injury last week versus Michigan.

Even with Dieffenbach, UCLA is still facing quite the talent disadvantage. Say what you will about Lincoln Riley the coach, the man can recruit. USC has landed top classes, giving the Trojans talent just about everywhere on the field, and NFL scouts know it too.

Los Angeles Times writer Sam Farmer met with NFL scouts about Saturday's USC-UCLA showdown, with Farmer stating that scouts felt like USC had much more talent, but Chesney's coaching is what elevates the Bruins.

"I talked to NFL scouts about USC/UCLA talent. They said if you were just picking players getting off the bus, 20 of the first 22 would be from USC, but that UCLA's coaching is just that much better." Farmer said on Twitter on Friday.

While that makes USC a scary opponent, it's an encouraging sign for the future of the UCLA program. As Chesney and UCLA establish themselves, more talent will flock to Westwood, where the coaching gap will be able to make a difference and jettison the Bruin program into title contention.

UCLA is wanting that to happen sooner rather than later, as a win over USC on Saturday nearly guarantees the Bruins a spot in the College Football Playoff.

#2687. Three Things I’m Looking At in the New York Jets vs Miami Dolphins Game

I think this past week was the most divided the fanbase has been since the Jets unceremoniously cut Aaron Rodgers. What a coincidence that he was just unceremoniously cut by a different franchise, and Jets fans are still completely divided on how to feel about the team's future and the decision-makers in the room.

I saw the comments on last week's preview; it looks like we have a lot of tank commanders on board. I won't sit here and tell you how to fan. I explained my rationale in great detail last week, and the more we argue, the more entrenched we become in our original philosophies. Three first-round picks mean complete draft flexibility. That's just how it is.

This week is the first rematch of the 2027 season and another game where the Jets are favored by Vegas heading into the game. Of course, they were favored last time versus the Dolphins, and those fish ended up winning their first game of the season without ever relinquishing the lead.

Since then, the Dolphins have returned back to the bottom of the barrel and haven't won a game. The Jets have barely been better, losing three more games before ending their skid against a 1-9 Chargers team. If you can believe it, these Dolphins are worse than the Dolphins that dominated the Jets at MetLife Stadium, and the Jets are better than they were four weeks ago.

What changed? The Dolphins are down to QB2 Kyle McCord, who is currently starting his first few games in the NFL. So far, he's averaging 155.5 yards per game, is completing 44.1% of his passes, and has thrown 0 touchdowns. The Jets are marginally healthier, and Frank Reich seems more comfortable deploying his "Killer Bs," who combined for 161 rushing yards at 4.5 yards per carry last week. Breece Hall also scored a touchdown, which means he's scored in 15 of the Jets' 18 wins since the start of the 2023 season.

Still, a few factors are keeping me from spending U.S. currency on the Jets Moneyline this Sunday. Let's take a look:

At the time of the first Jets-Dolphins game this year, the Dolphins ranked 8th in rushing yards per game (126.6) and 5th in yards per attempt (4.9). Ollie Gordon II was breaking out as a starter in place of the injured De'Von Achane, rushing for 80+ yards or scoring a touchdown (or both) in three games leading up to that Jets game. They ran for 156 yards against the Jets, and Gordon had 103 of those with a touchdown.

Since then, the Dolphins haven't run for 100+ yards in a game. They had 74, 77, 78, and 99 rushing yards as a team in the four games since facing the Jets. That means Gordon hasn't surpassed 80 yards in any of those games, and he hasn't scored a touchdown in that span either. The Dolphins, who were firmly establishing an identity on the ground, have had no room to run since facing the Jets.

The offensive line is healthy. Malik Willis played the first two games of this skid and Kyle McCord the next two. Sure, we can acknowledge that 3 of the 4 opponents they faced are currently among the NFL's top-12 rushing defenses, but when the Dolphins faced the best team (SF 49ers) back in Week 2, they still managed to run for 100+ yards against them.

This week they have the best possible chance for a "get right" game – facing a Jets defense that has been atrocious at stopping the run. Refer to last week's chart to see the recent damage done to Aaron Glenn's porous run defense, then add Omarion Hampton's 89 yards to the list. The Jets allowed 158 rushing yards to the Chargers last week.

If you're the Jets, this is a great opportunity to showcase any semblance of growth or competent coaching. They have a glaring weakness that's been exploited week in and week out, but they are facing a team that is clearly struggling. Can they watch the tape and see what other teams have done to fill the running lanes? Can they then communicate those things to the players and execute them to keep the Dolphins under 100 yards rushing?

For those few (but loud) Aaron Glenn truthers still roaming the streets, this would be a huge, tangible sign of improvement that could potentially sway us doubters with objective evidence. Injured defensive tackles T'Vondre Sweat and David Onyemata have been practicing; maybe they will be activated and help solidify this feat for Coach Glenn.

For me, I've grown to expect the worst. Vegas has Gordon's line at 68.5 yards, a number he's surpassed in three of the four games in this down period. I feel very confident betting the over, but I'm not sure a Kyle McCord-led offense will produce any Gordon touchdowns. Let's play it safe: bet the over on Gordon's rushing yards, and don't add a touchdown to the slip.

All that talk about the Dolphins rushing attack; let's not forget that the Jets rushing attack isn't too shabby either! The same 100+ rushing-yard mark the Dolphins haven't reached since facing the Jets has been achieved by the Jets in six of their last seven games. The lone exception was a 97-yard game against the Patriots where Braelon Allen did his part, but no one else followed suit.

I already mentioned before how much I love how Frank Reich deployed the Killer Bs, Breece Hall and Braelon Allen, last Sunday against the Chargers. One of my favorite examples was the start of the second quarter, when Reich had Braelon convert a 3rd-and-3 on the ground and immediately dialed up a pass to Breece to score on the next play.

Allen has been bruising people on the inside and has shown the burst to be effective bouncing outside when the insane gaps close. Hall continues to be the same elusive demon in space he's been all his career, making guys miss and using his steady hands to bail out Geno Smith when needed. The dream was always for them to be an elite duo despite the overwhelming majority of fans and media angling to trade Hall during Allen's breakout as the starter this year.

In the first Dolphins game, Allen had 104 yards on 17 attempts (6.1 ypc) and was dominating before they inexplicably abandoned the run game. How can they keep their $14.5 million running back involved without hurting the efficiency of the run game? Allow me to show you the rushing metrics from last week's Chargers win per NextGenStats:

This is just one game, but it was their best game as a duo this year in terms of total yardage. The stat that should jump out at you is how much more successful Breece is running outside than between the tackles. It's a tad misleading because this data implies Braelon is successful wherever he runs, but a quick look at film (or any other game's metrics) shows he is better inside.

I'd love to see a situation where Braelon gets 10-15 inside carries and just 2-5 outside, and Breece gets the inverse. Let the backs excel at their individual strengths, and keep Breece involved in the passing game, where he's averaging an additional 35.6 yards per game since returning from injury. Are we going to get that? Probably not. We'll probably see them keep running Breece up the middle and bringing in Braelon whenever they deem it necessary.

I haven't talked much about David Bailey in these pieces because the defensive unit as a whole has continued to be below average under their defensive head coach, Aaron Glenn, but please don't mistake my silence for indifference. David Bailey is a budding superstar; he's an athletic freak with the mature, intelligent mind needed for sustained, consistent growth.

In a generation full of children chasing clout and paychecks, David Bailey doesn't even have social media. He is dedicated to the game, dedicated to self-improvement, and a film junkie who is perpetually hungry for that extra edge. It's a privilege to watch him perform for my favorite football team on Sundays.

This rookie leads the league in forced fumbles (6), ranks third in sacks (9.5), and fifth in tackles for loss (12). I don't care one iota about backups vs. starters in the trenches because offensive linemen always get injured, and we've seen his technique improve throughout the season. If anything, his dominance against backups just reassures me that once he reaches his potential, starters are in store for a beatdown of epic proportions.

What does that mean for this game? No backup tackles, but Kyle McCord is a backup quarterback. We mentioned at the top of this article how badly he's struggled in his first two games, and if that continues, we might witness another David Bailey legacy game. McCord already coughed up a fumble in his first NFL start and has been sacked five times in his two games played. He seems like a prime victim of the reign of terror David Bailey is on.

The Dolphins' saving graces are their running game and game script. If they can run the ball effectively, maybe they won't call on McCord to drop back 30+ times like he has the last two games. If the Jets can't score touchdowns, the game script will let Miami be creative on offense and march down the field steadily instead of looking for chunk plays.

However, if the Jets defense can finally stop a rushing attack or the Jets offense keeps beating up below-average defenses, the situation will be perfect for another standout David Bailey performance in what has been a standout rookie season. Can he possibly break the NFL rookie sack record (14.5)? I think he'll inch closer this week. Let's go Jets!

#2686. 10 Takeaways from the Celtics Disappointing NBA Cup loss to the Hawks

Although this game was the day after Thanksgiving, the Boston Celtics decided to treat it like the movie Groundhog Day because they once again lost in the same exact fashion. I made this comparison before but its getting really hard to ignore when every game is playing out in the same way.

The Celtics once again took a double-digit lead into the half. They once again had a bad third quarter shooting the ball. They once again could not hit a shot in the fourth quarter and lost another 1-point game. You could say this game was lost because of the referees but it doesn't matter if you can't hit shots. It is starting to become exhausting watching them lose games in the same fashion over-and-over again.

This is the 5th loss in the last 6 games for the Celtics and they have dropped from the 1st seed in the East to the 5th seed. This is the 2nd time in the last 3 games that Boston has failed to crack 90 points in a game and they are the only 2 games in NBA history since 2024 where both teams scored under that mark. Boston is also ranked 30th in the NBA in offensive rating in their last 6 games. No matter what stat you throw out there, nothing has been good for the Celtics offense right now.

The biggest reason why the Celtics lost this game was because they turned the ball over a season-high 26 times. This was the most turnovers Boston has ever had in the Joe Mazzulla Era and it absolutely killed any chance the Celtics had in coming back in this game. In the fourth quarter alone, Boston turned the ball over 8 times in the fourth quarter alone, 4 of which coming in the final five minutes of the game.

Joe Mazzulla said, "that one is on me" when asked about the amount of turnovers the Celtics had in this game. He talked about not putting them in the right position and not calling the right plays. I would say that is a part of it but the team themselves just did not look connected at all.

There were a lot of plays in this game where it looked like the players went one way in rotation but the pass went another. There was a time we saw Jayson Tatum cut to the basket and Paul George threw it back to the three-point for example. There were also turnovers where guys would catch it underneath the basket and just dropped it. Credit to the Hawks as well who have a top-10 defense in the NBA and have built their team with long, athletic wings who just hound you on that side of the ball. Just a really bad showing by Boston.

When the Celtics were clicking in the first half, the biggest exploit they used was Atlanta's inability to defend the pain. Boston ended up generating 24 points in the paint in the first half while only attempting 15 three pointers. It feels crazy to say about this team, but Boston could have benefitted by shooting more threes in the first half but the Hawks just had no answer for the Celtics downhill approach.

That approach changed in the second half when the Hawks made a key defensive adjustment. Atlanta decided to cut Boston off from driving to the basket anymore and forced them to beat them from the three-point line. You could tell this was the plan because the Celtics were left wide open from three a lot and they shot them, just not well. In the third quarter Boston shot 2-13 from three, almost matching their first half total in attempts. They didn't fair much better in the fourth quarter, shooting 3-9 and only generating 4 points in the paint.

This adjustment from the Hawks seemed to throw the Celtics off their game however it feels crazy to think that if they just made their threes, they would have won. This is a common trend the Celtics have been going through during this stretch of games. They are generating good looks, they just aren't falling.

Similar to Dr. Jekyll and Mr. Hyde, First Half and Second Half Jayson Tatum are two completely different players. In this game, we once again saw the shift happen in real time where the second half guy disappointed.

In the first half, Tatum was dominant. He scored 16 points on 6-11 shooting from the field and did a great job of getting to the basket. In the first quarter he scored the same amount of baskets (5) as the entire Hawks team did. One of my favorite plays was when he ran the fast break, navigating a screen and crossing Dyson Daniels on his way to an easy layup. This was the 7th time in the last 10 games that Tatum scored double-digits in the first quarter.

However, after halftime, Tatum did not have the same impact, finishing with only 4 points on 2-11 shooting with 0 points coming in the fourth quarter. The most detrimental play coming in the final seconds after forcing a steal but as he was pushed out of bounds, threw it away back to the Hawks. I will say he did get a bad whistle in this game and it was more blatant in the second half, but it doesn't excuse the face that he has had problems finishing games this season.

In the second half of games this season, Tatum is averaging 10.6 points on 35.3% shooting from the field and 24.1% from three versus 16.3 points on 49.2% from the field and 42.3% from three in the first half. I don't know what happens at halftime that changes Tatum but it feels weird he is having these issues. Last season he averaged 11.9 points on 47.2% shooting and that was in the first games coming off the Achilles tear. Is it just a matter of conditioning for Tatum? I'm not too worried about it but it is something that has been pretty glaring, especially during this losing stretch for the Celtics.

Even if Tatum is having a hard time shooting the ball in the second half, the biggest question for the Celtics is who else will step up? For most of the season it has been Paul George and he has been great but in this game against the Hawks he had a tough game, finishing with only 8 points on 3-8 shooting. If it's not George creating a shot, can Pritchard or White step up? This season has shown both players having issues on offense this season and haven't lived up to expectations.

This game was glaring when it came to the lack of outside help around Tatum. When Tatum was on the bench, the Celtics offense was not the same. They we're not able to collapse the Hawks defense and Atlanta forced them to take tough shots. It just didn't feel like anyone outside of Tatum was able to create any sort of offensive momentum for Boston and that is a problem.

When asked about continuing to pass to his teammates even if they are struggling, Tatum said he would continue to do so. "It's a long season" Tatum said, "I'm not gonna not pass somebody the ball because they're in a little slump, because then how are they gonna get out of the slump? … I got the utmost confidence in the guys on my team to knock down shots." It sounds like it's just a matter of time before the Celtics hit their shots again but for now it has just been a struggle.

I am really starting to get concerned when it comes to Payton Pritchard this season. Some Celtics fans (including me admittedly) thought Pritchard was going to take a leap this year offensively but it has not looked like that so far. In this game, Pritchard finished with 10 points on 3-12 shooting from the field and 2-8 from three. In the first quarter, I was encouraged by his aggressiveness.

When matched up 1-on-1 with Jalen Johnson in the first quarter, he made a great move to get space and then sped past him for the easy layup, forcing a Hawks timeout. This is what I thought we'd be getting a lot more of from Pritchard this season.

However, in the second half he went ice cold, shooting 1-6 from the field and 1-4 from three. It felt like after this play, the Hawks were able to defend him well, cutting off his lanes to the basket and forcing up a ton of difficult mid range shots or three-pointers that he just missed. In his last 6 games, Pritchard is shooting 14-48 (29.2%) from three and just isn't knocking down shots. Have defenses discovered how to stop his game? That's what it is feeling like but at the same time it feels like he just needs to start hitting the wide open shots he is getting.

I would argue that the most impactful player for the Celtics in this game was Neemias Queta. He finished with a double-double of 11 points and a career-high 22 rebounds on 4-8 shooting. Queta was an absolute monster on the glass, mostly due to the Hawks lacking a true center to stop him. He led the way to the Celtics out-rebounding the Hawks 51-37.

In a game where the Celtics had a hard time generating much from three in the second half, Queta did a great job being able to find some space to try and generate offense for the Celtics. His ability to cut hard to the basket resulted in some nice baskets when he was matched up with Onyeka Okongwu underneath.

However, he did also have his shortcomings, finishing with 7 turnovers, a lot of them being miscommunications. Overall, I would say he feasted on the boards in this game and definitely was a big reason why Boston was able to stay in the game but he did also cost them late with some bad offensive fouls and turnovers that were back breakers in the moment.

Joe Mazzulla changed the starting lineup once again going into this game, replacing Sam Hauser with Hugo Gonzalez. Gonzalez actually had a pretty solid first half on both sides of the ball, finishing with 9 points, 3 rebounds, and 1 block on 4-6 shooting.

In the second quarter, we saw flashes of offensive development from him. With under 2 minutes to go in the first half, Queta found Gonzalez open at the three-point line with the shot clock running low. Gonzalez would pump fake, dribble around a Nickiel Alexander-Walker close out, and throw up a floater from the free throw line that swished in.

Not only was his offense looking solid, his defense was swarming. He was a big reason why Atlanta shot so poorly to begin the game. Despite a solid start, he only played 3:11 in the second half for Boston, not taking another shot. In a game where the Celtics were struggling to find energy on offense, it felt like Gonzalez could have been a good shot in the arm of energy. It was a solid game from him in the first half and I hope we can continue to see more offensive development.

Derrick White has mostly been talked about for his offensive struggles this season and that is valid, but in this game it was his defense that stood out to me. He finished with 13 points, 2 steals and 1 block on 4-8 shooting and did a good job of keeping Atlanta away from the rim.

In the second quarter, Okongwu tried to drive past Queta on the perimeter. White saw that, tracked him, and blocked the shot from the Hawks center that led to Paul George getting a layup on the other end for the Celtics. He had another play in the second quarter where he blocked a lob attempt on a 2-on-1 fast-break for the Hawks. White also drew a charge in this game so although he didn't make the biggest impact when it came to shooting the ball tonight, he was still a positive for the Celtics overall.

If I had a nickel for every time the Celtics lost the number 1 seed in their group due to a head-to-head matchup with the Atlanta Hawks despite having the better point differential I would have two nickels. Which isn't a lot, but it's weird that it's happened twice.

In the 2024 NBA Cup, the Celtics had a record of 3-1 with their only loss coming against the Atlanta Hawks in the first game of group play. Due to Hawks also finishing 3-1, Boston finished 2nd in the standing due to the head-to-head matchup, knocking them out of NBA Cup contention. In 2026, all Boston had to due was beat Atlanta in this game and they clinched the number 1 seed in the East, with home court advantage. Since they lost however, Boston is now the 4 seed in the East, facing off against the Detroit Pistons.

The Celtics had the third highest point differential in NBA Cup history at +67 this year and still don't get to host a home game during the playoffs. This feels like a flaw with the tiebreaker rules but at the same time it doesn't help that the Celtics just didn't take care of business. Although the NBA Cup has it's flaws, it has made the regular season a little more exciting and since the Celtics are now in the playoffs, I want them to win it.

#2684. No love lost between Ohio State and Michigan as benches clear

It's halftime of The Game, and everything is going just about as well as it can go for Ohio State. The Buckeyes are leading 21-0 over Michigan with two quarters left to play. Based on what we've seen from the Wolverine offense all year long, that should be more than enough to come out with a victory.

Both sides were assessed unsportsmanlike penalties, meaning that if anyone else gets another unsportsmanlike penalty, they will be ejected from the game.

There is no love loss between these two programs, and it's known as the best rivalry in all of sports for good reason.

#2680. Nvidia surge: What investors should do with the stock and the AI trade now

Nvidia shares gapped higher at the open by more than 30%, on pace for its biggest one-day gain in 23 years on Monday, following the news just before the bell that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is going to be reinstated.

Investors and traders said that while this reflex rally may have more to run, cautiousness is warranted on Nvidia and the AI trade because of the overhang of investigations into the alleged Chinese espionage involving the company and others, as well as the possibility for data center moratoriums as Democrats take control of Congress and key governor posts in January.

"Am I as bullish on Nvidia now as I was on Friday? Of course not, you can't be," said Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, which counted Nvidia as its largest position as he bought on the dip over the last few weeks. "I'm trimming back."

"All the same issues that were here before Jensen resigned are still here," he added.

The day before Huang resigned on Nov. 12 as CEO, Nvidia closed at $173.73. It's intraday high on Monday was $171.50, up from its recent low around $125 as it was selling off in the wake controversies. The VanEck Semiconductor ETF closed at $506.96 the day before his resignation. It reached a high of $469.35 before losing steam as trading wore on Monday.

Mike Khouw, YieldMax strategist at Tidal Financial Group, told CNBC on Monday that there were "sighs of relief," "applause" and even "cheering" among investors at the news of Huang's potential reinstatement. But he also said there are still considerable risks facing the AI trade.

Jensen Huang was not the only problem at Nvidia, he said, and there are still many vulnerabilities in the overall AI trade, which should give investors pause. Credit-sensitive companies are still at risk of faltering, Khouw added.

The national security implications of the scandal that has rocked Nvidia and the global AI trade more broadly aren't going away any time soon even as the Trump administration assured the Nvidia board there were none. Investors on Wall Street are expressing strategic concerns about ways to play the tech space just as lawmakers in Washington are demanding answers.

Democrats are demanding answers on what exactly happened with Nvidia and the Chinese government.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) said in a Monday statement. "The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what 'input' was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement."

House Ways and Means ranking member Richard Neal (D-Mass.) stressed the importance of Congressional oversight in determining the best course of action to move forward. The outcome of any Congressional investigations will likely be crucial for Nvidia's long-term viability as an investment opportunity, especially since the company does so much international business.

"This is an export control issue," Neal told CNBC on Monday. "Congress is not subservient to this president or any other president as it relates to trade and tariff policies. This is a trade related issue."

Many of the same Democrats will call for a slowdown in the data center buildout, a strong issue for the public partly responsible for their Blue Wave earlier this month.

"You're going to have to work your way through what these data center moratoriums all mean," said Niles. "Now I think the broader market keeps going but I am not as convinced on the move in the data-center centric names from here."

To be sure, many Wall Street analysts were rushing to upgrade Nvidia's stock on Monday after Huang's reinstatement. Most restored price targets well above $200 with one as high as $270. But many traders think the rebound may fail before getting back to those levels.

Famed short seller Michael Burry, who made a boatload betting against Nvidia with put options as it cratered, told his Substack audience that he thinks the rebound here could be "outsized," especially with low volume in the market overall because of the Thanksgiving holiday this week.

But he too sees the rally eventually losing steam.

"Jensen being back without much more information might even restore NVDA to the high 100s," Burry wrote. "I won't short it on this information I have today until it gets closer to or maybe hurdles $200."

#2679. “Our mentality was good today”: Youri Tielemans makes shocking post-match assessment following 4-0 Brentford drubbing

Saturday's Premier League clash against Brentford at Old Trafford was set to be Mauricio Pochettino's first home game in charge of Manchester United.

Expectations were high despite the Liverpool humbling, as the players showed in midweek that they were taking on board the new head coach's advice.

It all came crumbling down as the Bees thrashed the Red Devils 4-0, the third league game running in which United have conceded four goals.

The hosts did start well, but the sheer number of chances spurned by an inexperienced forward line came back to bite them as the London side were far more clinical.

Not too many people will have positive things to say about that dreadful result, but Youri Tielemans felt the mentality was good from the team!

"I thought our mentality was good today, but obviously when you [lose] 4-0 it's hard to say that.

"We just concede goals and then we can't score either, so it's a mix of everything. I don't think it's down to the mentality."

Y. Tielemans: Manchester United · 2026/27

#2677. Kalshi daily volume of ‘perps’ trading begins to top its prediction markets

Kalshi is known for its prediction markets across sports, politics and culture.

But now, for the first time, perpetual futures, Kalshi's second financial product, has surpassed the notional trading volume of its event contracts on consecutive days.

On Thursday, perpetual futures — commonly known as "perps" — on Kalshi saw $3.4 billion in notional volume as artificial intelligence laboratory Anthropic stock debuted on public markets while the rest of the market sold off. That exceeded about $3 billion in notional volume for Kalshi's prediction markets on the same day.

Perps are futures-style contracts where there is no expiration and traders do not need to own the underlying asset. Contracts instead continuously track the price of an asset, with funding payments to keep the contract price aligned with that of the market. Funding payments are made periodically between holders of short or long positions.

Perps trading surpassed event contracts one day in September, but on Friday perps topped prediction markets volume for a second day. Now Kalshi expects that performance to continue as it introduces perps tied to more individual stocks. On Monday, for example, Kalshi launched contracts linked to Anthropic.

Kalshi was the first to debut perps in the U.S. after winning federal regulatory approval in May to list perps tied to cryptocurrencies.

Perps trading volume includes any leverage that speculators acquire along with their trade, while event contracts are fully collateralized — for now.

Since the original launch in May, Kalshi has received approval to introduce perps tied to metals, crude oil, stock indexes and individual stocks.

Offshore perps — those available on exchanges not regulated in the U.S. — generated more than $90 trillion in notional trading volume in 2025, so it's not surprising that the product has grown so rapidly on Kalshi, according to Udesh Jha, chief risk officer at Kalshi Klear, the company's clearing house.

"We're just in very early innings. We're launching products, we're growing the participant base."

Perps were Kalshi's first product to mark its transition from a prediction market-focused platform to a full-blown financial exchange where various assets are traded. Last week, reports said that Kalshi is planning to offer trading on equities on its platform in the coming months.

At the same time, the company's perps product has come under fire. Some incumbent exchanges where futures are traded — which saw their stock prices decline following the approval of U.S. perps — believe the product was improperly approved. CME Group is suing the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, the federal derivatives regulator, for approving perps.

Others have simply expressed concerns about the efficiency of the markets. CNBC previously reported that trading volume on Kalshi's most popular perps markets — tied to bitcoin and ether cryptocurrencies — was dramatically greater than open interest, or cash actively in the markets. It's a phenomenon that isn't common with U.S.-regulated traditional futures, nor other offshore perps markets.

Kalshi said at the time it has "zero concerns" about the distinction, and that it appears because less leverage is offered compared to offshore exchanges and there are fewer incentives for traders to park capital on its perps markets.

While Kalshi started with cryptocurrencies, Owen Lau, an analyst at Clear Street, said perps tied to individual stocks likely represent the most promising opportunity for growth in the U.S.

"That's how I foresee the way perps will penetrate further into traditional asset classes," Lau said. "Perps on single stocks and [key performance indicators on individual companies] could change how investors hedge their portfolio or even make a directional bet."

Kalshi's growth has been powered by the fact that right now it's the only exchange that can actively list perps in the U.S., though crypto exchange Coinbase received approval in May to route U.S. users to offshore platforms for trading. That monopoly status, however, will probably prove short lived.

Coinbase has filed to list single-stock perps in the U.S., and President Donald Trump in August said the CFTC is working to bring Hyperliquid — a dominant player in offshore perps activity — to domestic traders.

But Jha at Kalshi isn't worried about the competition.

"What will carry us is ... making sure that we have the right products for the marketplace, making sure we have the right surveillance of the products that we are trading."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2674. AI debt markets shudder as Nvidia's top 'banker' returns

AI credit markets are shuddering again with Jensen Huang back as CEO of Nvidia.

Fears about the massive debt being taken on to fund the artificial intelligence buildout climbed Tuesday as Wall Street weighed what Huang's return to Nvidia means for its bankrolling of the AI boom.

Huang is considered one of the top financiers, or bankers, in the AI industry, spearheading deals with neoclouds like CoreWeave and other firms to buy chips, backed in some cases by Nvidia guarantees. His return to Nvidia has revived prospects for the company to continue "circular financing" of AI chips and data centers — raising concerns among credit investors.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child of AI debt concerns, saw its stock sink 13% and the yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 top 17%. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

At the same time, the spread on the neocloud company's five-year credit default swaps jumped 8.5% to over 1,200 basis points. Credit default swaps are insurance against nonpayment and are considered a barometer of perceived risks. Rising CDS spreads mean investors view the debt as riskier.

Huang was reinstated as CEO on Monday after stepping down on Nov. 12 amid allegations of multiple affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. His reinstatement on Monday was cheered by investors, who pushed stocks higher, yields lower and spreads on CDS tighter. However, stocks retreated, bond yields rose and CDS spreads widened on Tuesday.

Ben Emons, founder and chief investment officer at FedWatch Advisors, called it a waning of the "Jensen effect."

The moves suggest "markets see government intervention as 'moral suasion' that carries credit and interest-rate risks," he said.

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday he "convinced" the board to bring Huang back.

CoreWeave wasn't the only name seeing heightened concerns in CDS spreads. Oracle's five-year CDS spread jumped 7% to 355.68. Even highly rated hyperscalers Meta Platforms and Amazon.com saw spreads widen by more than 2%. Nvidia's CDS added 3.4%.

Larry Holzenthaler, senior portfolio manager for fixed income at Catalyst Funds, believes the drama that unfolded with Huang's return to Nvidia won't necessarily sit well with credit investors.

"A lot of old school credit people are going to look at what kind of materialized there, the administration's involvement in putting him back in, and I think a lot of credit participants are going to step back and tiptoe away from that a bit," he said. "Credit market investors typically don't like that type of drama or uncertainty."

While Nvidia has commitments across the AI ecosystem, it is the neoclouds that have been under the most scrutiny. Many of the companies, which rent out AI computing power and cloud capacity, are highly leveraged and have relied on Nvidia financing to reduce their exposure to the bond market.

With so much riding on Nvidia's funding, Huang's next move is being closely watched.

During his brief absence, chief financial officer Colette Kress said during the company's earnings call that Nvidia would be "rigorously evaluating future investments" and will remain disciplined in how it commits capital.

That suggested Nvidia was going to be more judicious with financing, says Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"That concern should be put to rest with Jensen [Huang] back," he said.

Investor Steve Eisman, of "Big Short" fame and host of "The Real Eisman Playbook" podcast, believes Huang feels emboldened now that he's back in charge.

"[H]e'll double down on the circular financing," Eisman predicted on CNBC's "Squawk Box" Tuesday.

After Huang's departure, Eisman believed that the ecosystem the CEO created by pouring money into AI companies would likely unwind and leave some of the companies in "big trouble."

"Now that he's back, they're not," he said.

Others are not so certain the level of funding will continue.

"Given scrutiny of disclosure around financing and guarantees … we do not expect Mr. Huang's return to bring a quick reversion to broader customer financing," Stifel analyst Ruben Roy said in a note Monday.

Nvidia said on its earnings call that it has $118 billion in guarantees, as of Oct. 25, to back AI projects.

Concerns about the level of debt to fund the AI infrastructure buildout had already taken hold before Huang's departure and reinstatement.

"These issues have not disappeared just because the White House pressured the weak board to reinstate Jensen Huang," said economist David Rosenberg, founder and president of Rosenberg Research.

John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson Investors, believes having Huang back at the helm "reaffirms his existing go-to-market strategy." However, the credit market is still taking a cautious look at the neoclouds as it waits for more clarity on long-term supply/demand dynamics.

Still, Lloyd is cautiously positive on CoreWeave, which he owns in portfolios he manages, and noted all the data point to a "pretty healthy environment right now." That includes strong residual values on graphics processing units and spot compute rates, he noted.

"The company is doing everything in its power to execute," he said. "They don't need the high yield market at this time."

In the meantime, investigations into Nvidia and Huang are ramping up. Huang is expected to appear before three House committees next week. Nvidia is conducting an independent investigation into his activities, and California Attorney General Rob Bonta has launched a probe. At the federal level, President Trump said Monday that U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

"There's other shoes to drop," Rosenberg said. "I don't think that the book has been closed on this file."

#2671. Nvidia's billion-dollar board bet: Who's protected as lawsuits mount?

Jensen Huang is back as CEO of Nvidia, after President Donald Trump urged the company's board to rehire Huang. But investigations into the company, its executive leadership and its board continue as outside criticism mounts.

"Clearly they caved to external pressure," said Betsy Atkins, an author and longtime public-company director. "The board failed," she said, when it initially allowed Huang's removal. "The board's role is to think of all stakeholders."

California Attorney General Rob Bonta is investigating whether Nvidia misrepresented information to investors. "There is classic corporate securities law on the books in California that prevents investors and the public from being misled by companies," Bonta said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Tuesday.

Directors are already defendants in a shareholder lawsuit accusing them of failing to oversee Nvidia. A separate securities class action targets the company and Huang, its co-founder.

Now the board’s insurance choices are part of the story. A risk advisor with direct knowledge of Nvidia’s coverage, who asked not to be named because they are not authorized to speak publicly about client matters, said the company built an approximately $1 billion D&O tower, insurance jargon for directors and officers insurance policies from different companies that combine to cover one massive risk.

Nvidia "loves insurance," the advisor said. “They buy insurance as if they have no balance sheet.”

A billion-dollar tower would essentially mean Nvidia "bought the market," purchasing all the coverage it could get, according to another industry expert, who has 25 years working in financial insurance. The expert asked not to be named due to sensitivity surrounding relationships with past or potential future clients.

But Nvidia directed all that coverage to Side A only, according to the risk advisor. Side A protects directors and officers personally when the company cannot indemnify them. Side B covers the company for legal defense costs or settlements on behalf of directors and officers, and Side C protects the company when it's named as a defendant in a suit such as a securities claim.

The risk advisor says Nvidia believed it had "the war chest" to protect itself against a securities class action suit.

That choice is raising questions about whether the board protected itself at the company’s expense. CNBC asked Nvidia for comment but received no response.

An insurance CEO, who asked not to be named in order to protect client relationships, characterized a billion-dollar policy as a lot of coverage, but said a Side A-only allocation was a surprising move because it results in imbalanced coverage, protecting only directors.

That structure is not automatically a governance failure, according to the D&O insurance expert with long experience in coverage for banks, drugmakers and other businesses that often face litigation. Side A-only programs are common among companies with the capital to manage the corporate risk internally.

"For a company with Nvidia’s resources, retaining the company-side risk can make financial sense," said the industry expert with 25 years working in financial insurance. It's Side A D&O that's "imperative to corporate governance."

"It is a capital-allocation decision," the expert said. The company decides that buying insurance for its own balance sheet is not worth the premium. In other words, Side B and Side C are considered too expensive for the coverage the company gets.

The question for shareholders is whether the board’s decision to hold that risk was a sound use of the company’s resources and whether the company can absorb the costs if the claims grow.

A shareholder derivative complaint filed Nov. 11 names Huang, directors and other officers. It alleges that they failed to oversee risks involving Huang’s relationship with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang and made misleading statements. It also alleges that some defendants sold roughly $2 billion in Nvidia stock while holding material nonpublic information. Those remain allegations, not findings.

A separate securities class action, filed Nov. 9, names Nvidia and Huang. It seeks damages for investors who bought Nvidia shares between Oct. 9, 2025, and Nov. 8, 2026, alleging the company and Huang misled investors.

The distinction matters: The derivative suit is brought on Nvidia’s behalf, while the class action seeks recovery for investors.

"Insurance is already at work," said Michel Léonard, chief economist at the Insurance Information Institute, describing standard policy mechanics, not Nvidia’s specific policy. "Once the suit is filed, D&O starts paying defense costs, long before anyone proves anything."

A claim still has to meet the policy’s terms, and defense costs can reduce the remaining limits. Nor does a drop in Nvidia’s market value by itself establish an insurable loss. Plaintiffs would need to prove legally recoverable damages; the policy would respond only to covered losses.

The board has already taken steps it says are aimed at accountability. Nvidia announced an independent investigation on Nov. 8, and independent directors retained outside counsel two days later. Huang resigned as CEO and a director on Nov. 11; the board appointed CFO Colette Kress interim CEO. Nvidia said it had no reason to believe national security or company secrets had been compromised by Huang's extramarital relationships.

Atkins, who joined the board of Wynn Resorts in 2018 after founder Steve Wynn resigned, said allegations are not conclusions and that boards should also weigh governance concerns against the value a founder brings to a company’s innovation. Nvidia's investigation is continuing even as Huang resumes leadership.

“Will companies’ hyper-accelerated innovation speed be the same without them? I don’t think so,” said Atkins, author of "Don't Waste a Board Seat: A Decision-Maker's Guide to Choosing Directors Who Create Value."

Nvidia’s 2026 proxy also says the company has indemnity agreements with its directors and officers — separate from its insurance. Those agreements provide for the company to cover certain costs and liabilities when the law permits.
