#2670. Knicks Week Preview: November 29 – December 5

After Miami snapped New York's five game win streak on Friday, the upcoming week presents a mixed bag for the Knicks (12-7). They'll have one competitive home test against a hot Eastern contender, followed by a back-to-back road swing against two struggling Central Division sides. Seems like a partly cloudy forecast with reason for sunny optimism later in the week, no? Let's peep the slate.

Sunday, November 29 — Knicks vs. Atlanta Hawks, 6 p.m. ET (MSG, ABC)

Atlanta just squeaked past the Celtics in Boston, 87-86, to wrap up NBA Cup group play. The Hawks are tied for second in the East at 12-6, and seven of those wins came on the road. This is their first crack at the Knicks, who took three of four last season from Quin Snyder's birds and knocked them out of the playoffs.

The Hawks like to run, move the ball, and get their hands in passing lanes. A few careless passes can turn into a quick six points the other way and, as shown in recent games, the 'Bockers can get a little turnover happy (they coughed up the rock 15 or more times in 10 games so far). Karl-Anthony Towns can help slow things down by cleaning up the glass.

Nickeil Alexander-Walker leads the team at about 21 points per game. Jalen Johnson averages roughly 19 points, nine boards, and six assists.

Tuesday, December 1 — Knicks at Chicago Bulls, 8 p.m. ET (MSG, NBC, Peacock)

The Knicks have seen the Bulls twice already. They won a closer-than-expected shootout at the United Center, 133-127, on October 28, then thumped them 135-105 at the Garden on November 4. Chicago has won two of its last three, including a close one in Charlotte, and can score enough to hang around if the Knicks let them.

The Bulls give up more than 122 points a night, so Jalen Brunson should have room to fill up the scorecard. If the Knicks can establish defensive dominance over Chicago's shooters early on, they might spare themselves another shootout on the first night of a back-to-back. For the Bulls, veteran Norman Powell has been one of their main scoring options when available.

Wednesday, December 2 — Knicks at Milwaukee Bucks, 8 p.m. ET (MSG, ABC)

The second night of the back-to-back takes the Knicks to Fiserv Forum, where the Bucks have a single win this season. Milwaukee is 2-18 and dead last in the East. The Knicks beat them 117-85 at the Garden on November 10.

Keys to the game: stay awake, protect the ball, control the paint, and see if there's a chance to rest Brunson and Towns early. For the Bucks, Tyler Herro has been a main scoring option on a roster that reads like a mess of trading cards thrown into a pile.

Atlanta is the toughest game on the schedule, and the Knicks get it at home. Win that one and they'll have a good shot at sweeping the week.

#2668. Bo Nix throws directly to Jalen Ramsey for pick-six as Steelers dominate the first half

Broncos quarterback Bo Nix has been awful today in Pittsburgh. And he just made his worst play yet.

Nix threw a perfect pass directly to a wide-open Jalen Ramsey for a touchdown, which would be great if Ramsey played for the Broncos, but was not so great because Ramsey plays for the Steelers.

It’s impossible to tell what Nix thought he was doing on the play, but he threw the ball right into Ramsey’s hands, and Ramsey returned the interception 36 yards to the house for a pick-six that gave the Steelers a 24-5 lead.

It was the second Steelers defensive touchdown off a Nix turnover; Nix also dropped the ball in the first quarter and T.J. Watt recovered for a touchdown.

Steelers quarterback Mason Rudolph hasn’t been great, but he’s been a lot better than Nix, and the Steelers are rolling over the Broncos in the first half.

#2666. Semiconductor chips are the new oil. Here's why

The artificial intelligence boom has pushed semiconductors past oil and gas in global export value for the first time, according to a Deutsche Bank analysis of International Trade Centre data.

Yet the milestone arrives at an awkward moment, just as investors reassess the outlook for artificial intelligence spending and drive down the price of semiconductor stocks. Last week, before Monday's breathtaking reversal, semiconductor stocks suffered their worst weekly loss in a quarter century, with the PHLX Semiconductor Index (SOX) plunging nearly 11% last Friday alone, capping a more than 17% weekly decline.

Nvidia soared more than 31% Monday after the leading maker of artificial intelligence chips had fallen for a seventh straight week, plunging 46% during that stretch. Then it fell again, dropping almost 10% on Tuesday and Wednesday. Advanced Micro Devices tumbled 20% in two days last Thursday and Friday. Micron Technology sank 27% over the seven days ended last Friday.

"For decades we've talked about the petrodollar. Perhaps increasingly we need to think about the chip dollar," Jim Reid, global head of macro and thematic research at Deutsche Bank wrote in a report this month.

Semiconductor exports overtook oil and gas for the first time during the January-March quarter of 2026, reaching about $1.61 trillion, compared with $1.53 trillion for oil and gas, the report said, using four-quarter rolling totals.

The gap widened in the second quarter, when semiconductor exports totaled about $1.60 trillion and oil and gas exports reached about $1.47 trillion. Disruptions to oil supplies through the Strait of Hormuz after the Iran war helped widen the gap. Daily oil flows through the Strait collapsed nearly 77%, to 4.9 million barrels in the second quarter of 2026 from 21.6 million barrels in the fourth quarter of 2025, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

Yet the demand for semiconductors has proven insatiable in the face of soaring data center construction, boosting global trade in the increasingly scarce AI component. Hundreds of billions of dollars of orders for graphics processing units and data centers aim at providing AI computing power, Rothschild & Co. Redburn wrote in a report last Thursday.

"Compute will be the new commodity fueling this transformation on a global scale," Rothschild analyst Cara Thomson said.

The shift toward chips has macroeconomic implications just as the explosion in oil trade did in the 1970s.

Oil-exporting countries historically recycled much of their surpluses into the purchase of U.S. Treasuries and other bonds, helping finance U.S. deficits and underpin demand for other government debt.

Reid at Deutsche Bank said that since semiconductor-surplus countries in Asia are more closely tied to U.S. tech companies, some chip-export earnings could flow back into U.S. technology stocks, startups and data centers, further helping to finance the AI investment cycle.

The changing dynamics in global trade could also tilt the mix of global financial flows, Reid said. "So welcome to the chip-dollar era: potentially more growth, more excitement, more volatility, and structurally higher yields" in corporate and government debt.

#2664. How have Cleveland Browns defined expectations ahead of Week 12 matchup against Las Vegas Raiders?

Week 12 pits the Las Vegas Raiders and Cleveland Browns against each other on Sunday Night Football, with both teams in contention for an AFC Wild Card spot. To preview the game for Raider Nation, Silver and Black Pride reached out to our friends at Dawgs By Nature, and Chris Pokorny was kind enough to answer a few questions about the Browns.

1) The Browns are the biggest surprise in the NFL this season, heading into Thanksgiving weekend with the AFC's second Wild Card spot. How have they managed to defy expectations, and what's the confidence level that they'll finish the campaign strong enough to punch their postseason ticket?

A: It's certainly been a wild ride this season through ten games, considering all six victories have involved game-winning drives by the Browns. It actually could have gone that way against the Jets and Saints too, but Cleveland didn't hang on in those games. Only in two games — the opener against the Jaguars, and the second time they faced Pittsburgh — was Cleveland outclassed for much of the game.

I think it speaks to the adjustments this coaching staff is willing to make in-game. We're coming off of a coaching regime that was the polar opposite, seeming incapable of making any adjustments when a team had them figured out. This team is young, and with every comeback, I think they believe that an early deficit doesn't mean they're out of it, and that snowball effect has worked against their competition.

Given their easy schedule, I think there's a decent chance they'll punch a postseason ticket. However, unless they can start winning games with more dominance, the odds of them beating a good team in the playoffs aren't the best.

Q: It seems like Deshaun Watson has revived his career under new head coach Todd Monken. What's been the difference between the quarterback who nearly got run out of town and the guy Cleveland has had this year? Since Watson is in a contract year, has he shown enough to get re-signed and be the organization's long-term solution?

A: It's hard to say whether it's anything Todd Monken did specifically, because I don't think Deshaun Watson's struggles (prior to his Achilles injury) were due to the Browns' coaching staff at the time. Back then, he was in his own head too much. He never seemed comfortable or confident on an NFL field with the Browns; whether it was the speed of the game or decision-making, it always seemed like something was 'off,' and I attributed it to the off-the-field distraction.

He wasn't able to shake the stigma of being hated. In training camp this season, something seemed different with him. He would still make some incorrect reads at times, but there was a visible confidence in his game. I wish I could put it into better words, but from the eye test, it made me pause and think, "Hmmm, I know we're assuming Watson is done with the Browns, but…he looks sure of himself finally." And then once he was named the starter, he piled up game-winning drive after game-winning drive.

He doesn't have a lot of touchdown passes to show for it and hasn't been perfect, but he's played respectably. That's becoming a tough question regarding a possible contract extension for him. With Cleveland's youth, I think there should still be more options out there who can succeed with this team, and the Browns have the draft ammunition to get the right player too. But now it's like, 'We're finally winning again, do we want to throw that away yet again?'

Q: Rookie wide receivers KC Concepcion and Denzel Boston were on the Raiders' radar this past spring. What have they added to the Browns' offense so far?

They have both been great. Even though I just praised Deshaun Watson (to a degree) in the previous question, with better quarterback play, I think KC Concepcion and Denzel Boston would be doing even better than what their stats show today.

Boston has been a good intermediate and deep threat, using his body to haul in contested passes. Concepcion hasn't been plagued by drops like many thought he would be, and he can obviously excel in open space. Concepcion isn't just a yards-after-the-catch guy, though. I think he's done a good job at getting open, but you're not expected to see him targeted on a back shoulder throw against a defensive back.

Q: The Browns were on the less popular end of the biggest offseason trade the NFL has seen in a while, sending Myles Garrett to the Los Angeles Rams a year after Garrett set the single-season sack record. However, Cleveland is the early winner of that deal, especially since its defense still ranks fourth in pressure rate at 41.5 percent, per Sumer Sports. Who has stepped up to mitigate the loss of a future Hall of Fame pass-rusher?

A: Cleveland has been on the winning side of that trade if we're looking at it solely from the perspective of the Browns' win total not suffering, and also the defense in general continuing to pile up sacks. But there is one big elephant in the room with the trade, and it's about defensive end Jared Verse.

He was never supposed to be 'the next Myles Garrett,' but he was supposed to be the equivalent of a first-round pick, and valued as a guy who would be the team's top disruptor like he was with the Rams. Instead, he's basically been the worst defensive player on the team, and one of the least effective defensive ends in the NFL. It's unfathomable how much of a non-factor he's been.

Fortunately, defensive coordinator Mike Rutenberg has the Browns bringing pressure from every position, adding to the unpredictability, and getting everyone involved in the game plan. We can only imagine how much better it could be if Verse got back to being the player he was with the Rams.

Q: The Browns are coming off their bye week. What changes do you expect (or hope) the coaching staff made during the time off that could impact Sunday's game?

A: Getting off to a better first-quarter start. I think Cleveland is the only team without an opening-drive touchdown this year, and they've also allowed the most opening-drive touchdowns this year. It's tough because the coaching staff is great at adjustments, are fully cognizant of the slow starts, have tried different things to try changing those slow starts, and nothing has worked. It's kind of unexplainable how it happens every week.

That would go a long way toward turning the Browns' close victories into more dominant victories, so they can be viewed as a better playoff contender. Their run game also needs a boost overall, but I don't know if a simple bye week will fix that: I think at this point, the ground game is what it is.

#2663. Bucks vs. Pistons Player Grades: Burries shows fight, rest of team lays down

Playing against a wounded Detroit Pistons team, the Milwaukee Bucks let an ugly third quarter—which they lost 42-26—be their undoing, losing 113-98. It was a great start to the game for the Bucks, leading 1903 at one point, but as soon as the Pistons put their second unit in—and the Bucks put theirs in—the game completely shifted. Overall, Detroit's physicality slowly sucked the life out of Milwaukee as they rode a strong second half to victory. Read our full summary of the game here and catch a six-minute audio recap on the Bucks+ podcast, Bucks In Six Minutes, below.

35 minutes, 20 points, 3 rebounds, 4 assists, 3 turnovers, 7/15 FG, 2/4 3P, 4/4 FT, -13

I actually liked how Herro started the game, even though he wasn't all that involved from a scoring POV. Detroit put Ausar Thompson on Herro, so he deferred to Rollins early and dragged Thompson away from the action. That said, taking just three shots in the first half isn't striking the right balance. And even when Herro got more aggressive in the second half, I thought he was trying too much foul-baiting instead of just trying to go past dudes. With all that said, it is hard to produce when a juggernaut defence is keyed into stopping you.

30 minutes, 12 points, 4 rebounds, 6 assists, 6 steals, 4 turnovers, 5/13 FG, 2/7 3P, -6

There was a lot to like about the way Rollins played; his defence was back to where it needs to be, and while I never measure good defence by how many steals and/or blocks a player gets, Rollins getting six steals felt very impactful to his overall performance on that end (in a good way). On the other hand, he still hasn't found his offensive rhythm, and four turnovers is too many.

30 minutes, 8 points, 3 rebounds, 3 assists, 3/12 FG, 2/9 3P, -3

I liked how Kuzma started the game, but the Pistons were completely fine leaving him open, and he proved that to be a fruitful strategy as the game progressed. It really sucks when the team moves the ball well, and the final link is Kuz with an open three, and he bricks it. Really takes the air out of your balloon.

34 minutes, 19 points, 11 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal, 1 block, 2 turnovers, 8/14 FG, 2/5 3P, -3

Jaquez was probably one of Milwaukee's better players on an otherwise bad night. He's getting back to providing something on the inside, and his size helped to drag down rebounds against one of the league's most physical teams.

29 minutes, 7 points, 12 rebounds, 2 blocks, 2 turnovers, 2/5 FG, 1/4 3P, 0

The good news: Turner had 10 rebounds in the first half. The bad news: he had two rebounds in the second half. More bad news: Turner, a seven-footer, got absolutely monstered by the 6'7" Ausar Thompson under the rim, like, four times. The lack of physicality was jarring. Yet another night where Turner got outshined by the opposing backup centre.

19 minutes, 7 points, 7 rebounds, 2 assists, 2 blocks, 2 turnovers, 3/7 FG, 1/3 3P, -14

Ware played a solid game. He took the right shots and did his best to make an impact as a screener and rebounder. I do think Ware needs to look at how a guy like Paul Reed, whose game isn't flashy at all, stays a net positive out there.

28 minutes, 23 points, 6 rebounds, 4 turnovers, 9/17 FG, 2/7 3P, -2

Burries was far and away the Bucks' best player. Sure, he made some shots and was one of the only guys with any modicum of success getting downhill (despite the odd turnover), but what was most impressive was the fight he played with. Like, the rookie was about the only guy who wanted to match Detroit's physicality.

13 minutes, 0 points, 4 rebounds, 1 turnover, 0/2 FG, -12

Nothing game for Ament against a level of physicality he was not ready for. That said, I can't act like I didn't expect this, so Ament's grade reflects that.

More random rotations from Jenkins. Caris LeVert five minutes here; AJ Green five minutes there; in goes Pete Nance after not playing for a while. The second unit broke down again this game because all the veteran know-how is crammed into the starting unit. As for the game plan, I still find it hard to completely crucify Jenkins because the roster is just really, really bad, but as Frank Madden notes below, there isn't much creativity in what the Bucks are trying to a lot of the time. Most of the offence seems to be: pass to Turner at the top, screen away, find a guard for a handoff and play out of that.

Limited Minutes: Pete Nance, AJ Green, Caris LeVert, Jericho Sims

Inactive: Cormac Ryan, Kasparas Jakucionis, Kam Jones, Bogoljub Markovic

Milwaukee continues to be less than the sum of its parts. You can change the coach and basically the entire roster, but apparently that feature remains from past Bucks teams.

Jenkins: "Their bench was the difference… They brought the pop, the level of physicality. Isaiah Joe was a big focal point, and we let him get too many easy looks. There were good moments, for sure, but that third quarter really got away from us."

The Pistons had seven (!) guys reach double-digit scoring. Now that is a team that is more than the sum of its parts.

Detroit beat Milwaukee 24-11 in fast-break points and a whopping 60-32 in bench points.

The Bucks clearly came in with a strategy that certain guys (Paul Reed, John Collins) were going to be left wide open, and while I generally agree with the "work smarter, not harder" ethos, sometimes I felt like the big was way too far away from the play, which allowed shooters to come off screens from those players and get open looks. Moreover, Reed is actually shooting nearly 60% (!) from three this season; granted, on low volume, but he still needs to be respected.

The Bucks are back at it tonight at home against another wounded team, the Oklahoma City Thunder. Watch at 7:00 p.m. CST on MY24.

#2662. Former boxing champ Jorge Linares released after 11 days in ICE custody

Former three-division world champion Jorge Linares is back with his family after spending 11 days in U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement custody in Texas.

World Boxing Association President Gilberto J. Mendoza announced Linares' release Friday, saying the Venezuelan boxer was "back with his family" and thanking his attorney, Richard Wilner, and his legal team.

The 41-year-old was detained by ICE agents on Nov. 15 at El Paso International Airport, where Linares first broke the news on X.

In a statement to Chron, ICE said Linares, who is based in Miami, had permission to enter the U.S. temporarily on Oct. 23 but stayed beyond the period he was authorized to remain. The agency did not specify how long that authorization lasted or why he was detained when he was.

Linares' legal team, however, said he did in fact have authorization to stay in the U.S. and was fully cooperative with immigration authorities.

Linares later told Telemundo that his 11 days in custody were the "11 most difficult days I've ever had in my life." He also said a GPS tracker he was required to wear made him "feel like a criminal."

Nicknamed "The Golden Boy," Linares won his first world title in 2007 and retired from professional boxing in 2023 after a 21-year career with a 47-9 record. He held world titles in the featherweight, super featherweight and lightweight divisions, with his final professional fight against Britain's Jack Catterall.

Neither Linares' attorneys nor ICE have indicated that his release resolves the underlying immigration matter.

This article originally published at Former boxing champ Jorge Linares released after 11 days in ICE custody.

#2661. Brock Purdy not practicing on Friday, putting Mac Jones in line to start

It looks like 49ers quarterback Brock Purdy will not be able to play Sunday’s game against the Seahawks.

Purdy is not practicing on Friday, according to multiple reporters on the scene. He has not been able to practice all week due to an ankle injury.

Jones has thrown only one pass this season in three appearances. Last year, he started eight games for San Francisco, going 5-3. In 11 total appearances for the club last year, he completed 69.6 percent of his passes for 2,151 yards with 13 touchdowns and six interceptions.

Receiver Jordan Watkins also was not practicing on Friday.

The 49ers’ full injury report with game statuses will be released later in the day.

#2657. The Broncos must admit what everyone already knows about Bo Nix

You invite a friend over to your house. He brings his dog. The dog then takes a massive deuce on your floor. You rightfully tell your friend in an angry tone that his dog just BM'd on your carpet.

"Uhh what are you talking about, I'm staring directly at it, and it smells horrible," you say, slightly concerned for your friend's well-being.

"Nah, there's nothing there. Don't know what you're talking about," your friend shoots back.

You then, rightfully, snap at your friend.

"There is a massive pile of sh-t on my floor right there, now pick it up!"

Your friend rolls his eyes, grabs a paper towel, and picks it up. Though he refuses to admit the dog dropped a massive landmine on the floor. "Are you not going to yell at the dog for doing that?" You ask in an irritated tone. "Why should I yell at him when it didn't leave a stain?" your clearly insane friend says back to you.

This is what it's like arguing with Broncos fans about Bo Nix. Despite all of us witnessing the consistently god awful quarterbacking he puts on display, the Mile High natives will gaslight you until you are both blue in the face, stubbornly denying what we all just witnessed.

It was another disaster-class for Nix on Friday, as he went 22-of-40 for 169 yards, two interceptions, and a fumble lost in Denver's 27-5 loss to the Steelers. Miraculously, the 4.2 yards per attempt weren't the lowest of the season for Nix. In Week 6 against the Seahawks, he went 17-of-27 for 107 yards – good for just four yards per attempt.

But the Broncos won, because Sam Darnold threw two horrible interceptions in the fourth quarter and overtime. And those kinds of wins have been the norm for Denver. They were down 16-3 to the Los Angeles Rams before special teams gaffes, a terrible pick-six by Matthew Stafford, and an even worse pass interference call allowed the Broncos to steal a win in Week 3. The week prior, a bad Trevor Lawrence interception stalled the Jaguars from going up two scores.

Overall on the season, Nix has six games where he has averaged fewer than six yards per attempt. Per Sumer Sports, of the 38 quarterbacks to have at least 100 dropbacks this season, Nix ranks 36th in success rate and 33rd in total EPA. Things get worse when he's pressured, which the Steelers did in bunches on Friday. On the season, Nix ranks 35th in completion percentage when pressured with just one touchdown pass to three interceptions.

Nix also just can't throw the ball further than 10 yards down the field. On intermediate passes (10-19 yards downfield), Nix is completing just 42.7 percent of his throws, which ranks 36th out of 38 qualified quarterbacks. His total EPA on such throws ranks ahead of only Michael Penix and Jalon Daniels.

It gets even worse when the Broncos dare let him throw deep balls. On passes 20+ yards downfield, Nix has zero touchdown passes to four interceptions. His completion percentage on deep balls ranks 28th in the league, and his EPA per pass on deep balls ranks 30th.

Because of this, the Broncos live almost exclusively in the short passing game. Nix ranks third in the league in short passing percentage, and leads the league in the same stat among quarterbacks who have started every game. Because of this, Nix has just 17 impact plays all season – the same number as Aaron Rodgers, who was benched then released, and Sam Darnold, who has played in four fewer games.

And yet, in spite of all of that, there will still be Broncos fans who will tell you he's good because of the Broncos' record. As if the Bears didn't make it to a Super Bowl with Rex Grossman. Or the Broncos themselves didn't win a Super Bowl with historically bad quarterback play a decade ago with Peyton Manning. They take Denver's win-loss record and slap it over any sort of argument that rightfully points out how horrible Nix is like the meme of the Flex Seal guy. And genuinely, it's very weird the length Broncos fans will go to defend Nix. I don't know why it's so hard for them to say the quarterback stinks. The defense is good (6th in success rate, 10th in scoring), and Denver would be a lethal team if it had a quarterback who didn't piss down his leg at any sign of pressure or with one that had the capability to push the ball downfield. They should want a good quarterback. Instead, they bend over backwards defending a bad one.

At his best, Bo Nix is what? The 19th best quarterback in football? At his worst, he is unplayable. Say what you what about Sean Payton – and you can certainly say a lot – but he's not blind. He and the rest of the Broncos' brain trust certainly recognize that this quarterback is going to keep them from reaching their full potential, right? Bo Nix is not beating any combination of the Chiefs, Bills, Jaguars, and/or Bengals to get the Broncos to the Super Bowl. It's not happening – no matter how many fans with 720 area codes try gaslighting themselves into believing it will.

#2656. Victor Wembanyama delivers message against Luka Dončić and the Lakers in MVP showdown

SAN ANTONIO — Mitch Johnson didn't have much use for his clipboard at the moment.

The Spurs head coach sat in the middle of the huddle with a crowd around him, gesturing flagrantly with a 17-point deficit staring his team in the face. No X's-and-O's pep talk was necessary; Johnson was demonstrating how his players needed to respond to the Lakers' force. A message heard by many was truly intended for one very tall alien.

On Friday, every step Victor Wembanyama took was met with more resistance than Agent J and Agent K from "Men in Black." The men in purple had made it very clear from the opening tip of their intention to rough Wembanyama up. Pressure in the backcourt. Pressure in the frontcourt. Double teams. Fronting him with a second — and sometimes a third — helper rotating over the second his hands touched the ball.

The old Wemby might have wilted, faded away as the game wore on, frustrated with the overwhelming physicality. This updated version simply downloads the data and adjusts like artificial intelligence. So the Spurs stormed back in the second half, and with the game hanging in the balance, it was Wembanyama who answered brute force's call. At one end, the Frenchman barrelled through Luka Dončić and Walker Kessler en route to the rim for an emphatic slam. Seconds later, he darted to the restricted area, leaping as high as his limbs and outstretched fingertips could go, sending Collin Sexton's shot all the way back to La Brea.

"There was some maturity tonight that was good to see," Johnson said following the 99-96 win, which earned the Spurs a spot in the NBA Cup quarterfinals. "When things aren't going well, there are some things you can't control. But I thought we did a good job of working the game, staying in the game. It was a good step against a very high-level team."

Friday night — or the latest reboot of "Alien vs. Predator" — was a celebration of differences. On one hand, this was the apex of Western Conference talent, a showdown of Wembanyama and Dončić, MVP favorites who do their respective damage in contrasting styles and their relationship with space. Dončić, the swashbuckling offensive hub who has mastered the art of manipulating areas around him, against Wembanyama, the defensive wizard who can magnify or minimize game plans on a whim.

On the other hand, it was unique insight into Wembanyama's growth and exactly what sets him apart from his peers. This game was not aesthetically pleasing. No team shot better than 50% from the field, 35% from deep or even 75% from the charity stripe. There were 39 combined turnovers and 32 fouls. Dončić finished with 31 points on 31 shots, Wembanyama ended up with 25 points on 25 shots.

But even amidst the ugliness, beauty can be uncovered. The postgame locker room mood suggested as much, with the Spurs fully understanding the Lakers' unique, aggressive approach to Wembanyama. Between now and a possible playoff matchup months down the line, opposing teams — that were surely tuned in to such a hotly contested meeting — will dissect Los Angeles' strategy, taking bits and pieces and attempting to replicate it. On some levels, sending numerous waves of pressure at Wembanyama's frame should yield positive results, granted a team has the right mix of defensive personnel. In the same breath, however, Wembanyama seemingly embraced the added physicality, with 17 points and 13 rebounds in the second half to show for it.

"Vic just finds a counter for everything teams send at him," Stephon Castle said. "Teams guard him differently every night — and I could say every quarter — switching up the schemes on him. One of those things is to be physical with him and try to take him off his spots, not let him roll. But for us we need him to do that. So being able to fight through and not getting in his head, playing the right way."

Victor Wembanyama blocks the shot of Collin Sexton in the second half at Frost Bank Center on November 27, 2026, in San Antonio, Texas. (Photo by Ronald Cortes/Getty Images)

In theory, the version of Wembanyama that emerged in the final minutes could certainly be extrapolated for longer stretches of the game. But that would require wholesale internal changes, not only for Wembanyama, but for San Antonio as a whole. Both Dončić and Wembanyama spend quite a bit of time handling the ball in the frontcourt, but the stark difference in both average time of possession and seconds per touch (nearly five and three more seconds, respectively, per Second Spectrum) speaks to a larger theme around how both are used. Everything starts and ends with Dončić, the brains of LA's operation. Not a possession goes by without his influence with the ball directly in his hands.

(The 22-year-old could also benefit from seeking more contact and getting to the free-throw line. He's currently wedged between the likes of Paul Reed and Wendell Carter Jr. in shooting foul rate, not necessarily where a player of his caliber should be — especially with how defenses are geared to slow him down.)

Wembanyama is utilized a bit differently. He's just as likely to sprint down the floor and set a pindown for a teammate as he is to relocate to the top of the key and urge someone to fill the lanes. Johnson has fully empowered Wembanyama to call his own number at any given moment he's on the floor, even though there's an understanding he'll still play within the scheme. And all of this is occurring while taking high-percentage looks (top 25 in expected field-goal percentage), taking more shots at the rim and improving as a decision-maker (while keeping turnovers at a career low). Getting into his on/off splits (he had four much-needed steals and two blocks on Friday) is a mind-numbing exercise at this point — the Spurs are 36 points per 100 possessions better with him on the floor, the best among his peers. The Lakers scored just 36 points in the paint, which shouldn't come as a surprise given that 29 other teams struggle with the same concept.

"He understands his spots," Johnson said. "We understand how and where to pass him the ball. But he's 22 — he's a long way from being a finished product. So there's gonna be natural evolution, things learnt on this journey that will continue to help him improve. That's what you see with these guys that are the best of the best."

It's odd to suggest a team that has won 80% of its games at the 20-game marker has areas for improvement, but much like Wembanyama's growth, there are still areas to clean up. San Antonio is still a bottom-five 3-point unit, despite boasting floor spacers like Julian Champagnie and Devin Vassell. (Johnson once again bemoaned the lack of volume and is urging more attempts.) Their ball security in tense moments — 13 combined turnovers from Castle, Dylan Harper and De'Aaron Fox — needs tuning up. But this is a group abundant with talent, hunger and an alien who has settled in quite nicely on human soil.

"It's a different scenario that we've faced," Wembanyama said. "But I like how my guys reacted. It just showed character. It's getting us ready for the next steps of the season. Even if we had lost this game somehow, we learned a lot from it. So we're learning these games are coming and we're going to be ready."
